From veering triangulations to link spaces and back again
Steven Frankel, Saul Schleimer, Henry Segerman

Abstract
. This paper is the third in a sequence establishing a dictionary between the combinatorics of veering triangulations equipped with appropriate filling slopes, and the dynamics of pseudo-Anosov flows (without perfect fits) on closed three-manifolds. Our motivation comes from the work of Agol and Guéritaud. Agol introduced veering triangulations of mapping tori as a tool for understanding the surgery parents of pseudo-Anosov mapping tori. Guéritaud gave a new construction of veering triangulations of mapping tori using the orbit spaces of their suspension flows. Generalising this, Agol and Guéritaud announced a method that, given a closed manifold with a pseudo-Anosov flow (without perfect fits), produces a veering triangulation equipped with filling slopes. In this paper we build, from a veering triangulation, a canonical circular order on the cusps of the universal cover. Using this we build the veering circle and the link space. These are the first entries in the promised dictionary. The link space and the circle are, respectively, analogous to the orbit space of a flow and to Fenley’s boundary at infinity of the orbit space. In the other direction, and using our previous work, we prove that the veering triangulation is recovered (up to canonical isomorphism) from the dynamics of the fundamental group acting on the link space. This is the first step in proving that our dictionary gives a bijection between the two theories.



\fail

2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 37C85, 57M50, 57M60

Chapter 1
Introduction

This is the third in a series of papers
[
53
,
54
]

providing a dictionary between veering triangulations, equipped with appropriate filling slopes, and (topological) pseudo-Anosov flows (without perfect fits) on closed three-manifolds.
This fulfils part of Smale’s program for dynamical systems, outlined in his 1967 Bulletin article
[
56
]
;
one first finds the appropriate notion of stability for flows (pseudo-Anosov without perfect fits), and then combinatorially classifies such systems (veering triangulations with filling slopes).
Christy gives an exposition
[
11
, page 759]
of Smale’s program, and proves (following Williams
[
66
]
) that smooth transitive pseudo-Anosov flows determine and are determined by a dynamic branched surface.
Christy further promises
[
11
, page 760]
an algorithm to determine topological equivalence between such flows.
This work never appeared; such an algorithm (for pseudo-Anosov flows without perfect fits) is one consequence of our dictionary.

Figure 1.1.
The veering structure on the canonical triangulation for the figure-eight knot complement.

1.2.
The dictionary

We outline our overall programme.

Link spaces

In this third paper, given a
veering triangulation

\mathcal{V}
of a cusped three-manifold
M
, we build the
link space

\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}({\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}})
of the universal cover
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
.
(The link space is a copy of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
equipped with two foliations and an action of
\pi_{1}(M)
.)
To do this, we first associate to
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
a canonical circular order on its cusps.
We then construct the
veering circle
and a pair of invariant laminations.
Following the exposition of Casson and Bleiler
[
10
, Section 6]
, we collapse these laminations to produce the link space
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}({\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}})
.
This done, we prove that the link space is a
loom space

[
54
]
.
In fact, we show that
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}\colon\operatorname{\mathsf{Veer}}\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Loom}}
is a functor from the category of veering triangulations to the category of loom spaces.
In
[
54
, Section 5]
we give a functor
\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}\colon\operatorname{\mathsf{Loom}}\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Veer}}
.
In this paper we show that
(\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}\circ\operatorname{\mathsf{L}})({\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}})
is canonically isomorphic to
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
.
Furthermore, any loom space
\mathcal{L}
is canonically isomorphic to
(\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}\circ\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})(\mathcal{L})
.
We give a more detailed outline of this paper in Section
1.5
.

Dynamic pairs

In our fourth paper
[
55
]
we isotope the
upper and lower branched surfaces

B^{\mathcal{V}}
and
B_{\mathcal{V}}
(see Section
6.1
) to form a
dynamic pair
in the sense of Mosher
[
43
, Section 2.4]
.
These branched surfaces may coincide in many sectors:
to tease them apart we first construct a semi-local notion of “horizontal” using a canonical decomposition of
|\mathcal{V}|
into
shearing regions
.
We then perform an intricate sequence of isotopies.

In the appendix we find the
maximal rectangles
in the glued-together horizontal cross-sections;
we show that these correspond to the original veering tetrahedra.
We also show that these rectangles “overlap” along face rectangles.

Flow-box decompositions

Following Mosher
[
43
, Theorem 3.4.1]
, a dynamic pair gives an
ideal flow-box decomposition
of the ambient cusped three-manifold.
Filling along appropriate slopes one obtains a topological pseudo-Anosov flow on the closed manifold.
Thus, in our fifth paper
[
51
]
, we start with a veering triangulation (equipped with appropriate filling slopes), and applying our fourth paper, we produce a transitive pseudo-Anosov flow.
We prove that this flow has no
perfect fits

[
14
, Definition 2.2]
.

Remark 1.0.1
.

Agol and Tsang
[
3
, Theorem 5.1]
have recently given another construction of a pseudo-Anosov flow (without perfect fits) from a veering triangulation (equipped with filling slopes).
Their construction begins with the
flow graph

[
37
, Section 4.3]
.
They then remove
infinitesimal cycles

[
3
, Section 3]
.
Each remaining edge gives a flow box.
The resulting flow-box decomposition gives a pseudo-Anosov flow without perfect fits on the filled manifold.

We then prove the following.
Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are (ideal) flow-box decompositions of a three-manifold.
Then there is an equivariant homeomorphism between their
leaf spaces

\operatorname{\mathsf{Le}}(\mathcal{A})
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{Le}}(\mathcal{B})
if and only if
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are
flow equivalent
:
that is, connected by a sequence of isotopies,
slide-isotopies
, splittings, and merges.
The forward direction is more difficult:
we first match up the transverse foliations in
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
.
We then engulf the vertical faces of
\mathcal{A}
within those of
\mathcal{B}
by splitting
\mathcal{B}
and appealing to (local) finiteness.
Dealing with the horizontal faces is by far the hardest step.
This requires a delicate induction on the topological complexity of their intersection.

Drilling the singular flow lines of a (material) flow-box decomposition
\mathcal{A}
gives an ideal flow-box decomposition
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
.
We show that if
\operatorname{\mathsf{Le}}(\mathcal{A})
has no perfect fits then
\operatorname{\mathsf{Le}}(\mathcal{A}^{\circ})
has the
finite shadow covering property
.
In general, given an ideal flow-box decomposition with this property, we show that its leaf space is a loom space.

The correspondence

In the remainder of our fifth paper we establish the equivalence of
\operatorname{\mathsf{Veer}}
, the category of veering triangulations, and
\operatorname{\mathsf{Box}^{\circ}}
, the category of (flow equivalence classes of) ideal flow-box decompositions.
That is, we give functors

\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}\colon\operatorname{\mathsf{Loom}}\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Veer}},\qquad\operatorname{\mathsf{Le}}\colon\operatorname{\mathsf{Box}^{\circ}}\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Loom}},\qquad\operatorname{\mathsf{B}}\colon\operatorname{\mathsf{Veer}}\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Box}^{\circ}}

and show that the compositions
(\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}\circ\operatorname{\mathsf{Le}})\circ\operatorname{\mathsf{B}}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{B}}\circ(\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}\circ\operatorname{\mathsf{Le}})
are naturally isomorphic to the identities on the categories
\operatorname{\mathsf{Veer}}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{Box}^{\circ}}
respectively.
In particular,
\mathcal{V}
is combinatorially isomorphic to
(\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}\circ\operatorname{\mathsf{Le}}\circ\operatorname{\mathsf{B}})(\mathcal{V})
;
also,
\mathcal{A}
is flow-equivalent to
(\operatorname{\mathsf{B}}\circ\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}\circ\operatorname{\mathsf{Le}})(\mathcal{A})
.
The above, together with the equivalences mentioned in Section
1.2
, prove that the following diagram commutes (up to natural transformations).

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{Veer}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{Loom}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{Box}^{\circ}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{Le}}}

This establishes the desired correspondence between, on the one hand, veering triangulations equipped with appropriate filling slopes, and on the other hand, flow-box decompositions (without perfect fits) of closed three-manifolds (up to a finitely generated equivalence relation).

Remark 1.0.2
.

The flow-box decomposition given by Agol and Tsang
[
3
, Theorem 5.1]
appears to have fewer boxes than our
\operatorname{\mathsf{B}}(\mathcal{V})
.
On the other hand, it is not currently known if their construction gives an inverse to the Agol–Guéritaud construction.

1.3.
Cannon–Thurston maps

As a further application of the work of this paper, we will prove with Jason Manning
[
40
]
that there is a Cannon–Thurston map from the veering circle to the boundary of hyperbolic space.
Very roughly, our proof proceeds as follows.

(A)

We compactify the link space
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
by the veering circle
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
to obtain the
veering disk

D^{2}(\mathcal{V})
.

(B)

We analyse Hausdorff limits in
D^{2}(\mathcal{V})
.
This requires the
astroid lemma

[
54
, Lemma 4.10]
.

(C)

We prove that the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(M)
acts as a convergence group on the
veering sphere

S^{2}(\mathcal{V})
: a certain equivariant quotient of
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
.

(D)

All cusps project to bounded parabolic points of
S^{2}(\mathcal{V})
.
This requires Lemma
A.0.3
.

(E)

All other points of
S^{2}(\mathcal{V})
are conical limit points.
This requires suitably controlled neighbourhood bases, as given by Lemmas
A.0.6
and
A.0.9
.

(F)

We finally appeal to a result of Yaman
[
68
, Theorem 0.1]

characterising the Bowditch boundary of a relatively hyperbolic group pair.
In particular,
S^{2}(\mathcal{V})
is equivariantly homeomorphic to the boundary of hyperbolic space.

As a consequence, we find examples of Cannon–Thurston maps for cusped manifolds that do not arise, even up to commensurability, from surface groups.
In the closed case such examples can be deduced from the work of Fenley
[
14
, Theorems B and D]
.
It is not known if our (cusped) examples
slither over the circle
in the sense of Thurston
[
59
]
.

1.4.
Veering triangulations

Inspired by
[
13
]
, Agol introduced veering triangulations in order to better understand the surgery parents of the mapping tori of pseudo-Anosov maps with bounded normalised dilatation
[
1
]
.
His innovation, following ideas of Hamenstädt
[
26
]
, was to produce a canonical train track splitting sequence for the monodromy.
In particular, he showed that the veering triangulation of the drilled mapping torus (together with the corresponding point of the fibred face of the Thurston norm ball) is a complete conjugacy invariant for the monodromy.
More generally, veering triangulations have wide applications in geometric topology
[
5
,
23
,
41
,
57
,
34
,
33
,
35
,
37
,
48
,
47
,
46
,
4
]
, as well as in dynamics
[
15
,
36
,
38
,
3
,
62
,
63
]
, and their properties have been a subject of study by numerous authors
[
28
,
17
,
31
,
27
,
50
,
67
,
18
]
.

Agol asks his readers
[
1
, Section 7, third question]
to ponder the possibility and meaning of non-layered examples.
The first such was found via computer search by Hodgson, Rubinstein, Tillmann and the third author
[
28
]
.
In other work
[
21
]
, we give the census of all transverse veering triangulations with up to 16 tetrahedra; there are 87,047 of these.
The evidence strongly suggests that non-layered veering triangulations dominate.

The first hint that Agol’s question has a general answer comes from the work of Guéritaud
[
23
, Theorem 1.1]
.
He gives an alternate construction of the veering triangulation, starting from a singular euclidean structure on the fibre of the given mapping torus.
Agol and Guéritaud extend this: given a manifold equipped with a pseudo-Anosov flows without perfect fits, there is an associated veering triangulation on its surgery parent
[
2
]
.
Given this, it is natural to ask if there is a map in the other direction.
This was the inspiration for our dictionary, given in Section
1.2
.

Figure 1.6.
The main objects and constructions in this paper.

The flowchart shown in Figure
1.6
illustrates the interrelations between the main objects as well as their constructions.

In Chapter
2
we review the definitions of
circular orders
,
transverse taut ideal
triangulations, and
layered
triangulations.
We then define what it means for a circular order to be
compatible
with a given transverse taut structure.
In Example
3.0.1
we show that layered triangulations are
rigid
: that is, they admit a unique compatible circular order.
In a striking contrast, in Section
3.5
we give a non-layered taut ideal triangulation which is far from rigid –
by Theorem
3.6
it admits uncountably many compatible circular orders.
On the other hand, layered or not, veering triangulations are rigid.

.

Suppose that
M
is an oriented three-manifold equipped with a transverse veering triangulation
\mathcal{V}
.
Then there is a unique compatible circular order
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{V}}
on the cusps of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
.
Furthermore,
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{V}}
is dense and
\pi_{1}(M)
–invariant.

To prove this, in Chapter
4
we quickly review the terminology of
train tracks
.
We then introduce the new tools required to obtain Theorem
5.2
.
The most important of these is the idea of a
continental exhaustion
, introduced in Definition
4.0.18
.
This is a kind of developing map;
however, instead of constructing a geometric position for each tetrahedron in
\mathbb{H}^{3}
, we produce combinatorial positions in a “thickened version” of
\mathbb{H}^{2}
.
The heart of the proof of Theorem
5.2
is given by Proposition
5.0.2
; in a delicate induction we promote a transverse veering triangulation to a continental exhaustion.

In Chapter
6
we construct various
branched surfaces
.
Using these and the techniques developed for the proof of Theorem
5.2
, we promote the circular order
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{V}}
on the cusps
\Delta_{\mathcal{V}}
of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
to the
veering circle

S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
.

.

Suppose that
M
is an oriented three-manifold equipped with a transverse veering triangulation
\mathcal{V}
.
Then the order completion of
(\Delta_{\mathcal{V}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{V}})
is a circle
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
with the following properties.

(1)

The action of
\pi_{1}(M)
on
\Delta_{\mathcal{V}}
extends to give a continuous, faithful, orientation-preserving action on
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
.

(2)

If
\mathcal{V}
is finite then all orbits are dense.

The veering circle
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
depends only on
\mathcal{V}
, and not on any other choices.
This is in striking contrast with Thurston’s
universal circle
for a given closed manifold equipped with a taut foliation; many choices are required for his construction
[
9
, Remark 6.27]
.
Our situation is instead similar to Fenley’s
ideal circle boundary
for a closed three-manifold equipped with a pseudo-Anosov flow without perfect fits
[
14
, Theorem A]
.
There the circle is unique.
We stress however that our results require only a finite amount of combinatorial data, while
[
9
,
14
]
both require substantial topological or dynamical inputs.

Remark 1.0.3
.

Since our manifold
M
comes equipped with a taut ideal triangulation, it necessarily has cusps.
Thus the fundamental group
\pi_{1}(M)
surjects
\mathbb{Z}
.
We deduce that
\pi_{1}(M)
has
left orders
and so has
left circular orders
.
See
[
7
, Definitions 2.26 and 2.40]
.
We note that Theorem
7.1
gives a new left circular order on
\pi_{1}(M)
, by
inserting gaps at the cusps and, in these gaps, adding left orders on the peripheral groups.

We next build the
upper
and
lower laminations
.

.

Suppose that
M
is a three-manifold equipped with a transverse veering triangulation
\mathcal{V}
.
Then there is a lamination
\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}
in
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
with the following properties.

(1)

The lamination
\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}
is
\pi_{1}(M)
–invariant.

(2)

The lamination
\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}
suspends to give a
\pi_{1}(M)
–invariant lamination
{\widetilde{\Sigma}}^{\mathcal{V}}
in
{\widetilde{M}}
; this descends to
M
to give a lamination
\Sigma^{\mathcal{V}}
which

(a)

is carried by the upper branched surface
B^{\mathcal{V}}
,

(b)

has only plane, annulus, and Möbius band leaves, and

(c)

is essential.

(3)

Suppose that
\Sigma
is a lamination fully carried by
B^{\mathcal{V}}
.
Then, after collapsing parallel leaves,
\Sigma
is tie-isotopic to
\Sigma^{\mathcal{V}}
.

There is also a lamination
\Lambda_{\mathcal{V}}
with the corresponding properties with respect to
B_{\mathcal{V}}
.

Theorem
8.1
(
3
) is surprising; in Corollary
8.0.35
we use this to show that any two non-empty laminations carried by the stable branched surface associated to a pseudo-Anosov homeomorphism are tie-isotopic (after collapsing parallel leaves).
This answers a question of Danny Calegari
[
8
]
.

Using the upper and lower laminations, we build the
link space
with its upper and lower foliations.
After reviewing the axioms of loom spaces
[
54
]
, we prove the following.

.

Suppose that
M
is a three-manifold equipped with a (locally) veering triangulation
\mathcal{V}
.

(1)

The link space
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
, equipped with the foliations
F^{\mathcal{L}}
and
F_{\mathcal{L}}
is a loom space.

(2)

Every tetrahedron rectangle is a loom-tetrahedron rectangle; and conversely.

(3)

The set of cusp rectangles in
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
, with ideal point a given cusp class of
\operatorname{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{V})/{\sim}
, is a loom-cusp; and conversely.

(4)

The action of
\pi_{1}(M)
is faithful and by loom automorphisms.

The proof of Theorem
10.18
is quite involved.
To build the link space, we must collapse a pair of transverse laminations in a circle to obtain a pair of foliations in a plane.
There are various expositions of similar collapsing procedures in the literature;
see
[
10
, Chapter 6]
and
[
30
, Section 11.9]
.
However, we do not have any of the usual hypotheses:
we do not have a surface group action,
we do not have (projectively) invariant measures,
and we do not have compactness (of the original three-manifold).

In Theorem
11.9
, we prove that our construction, of a link space from a veering triangulation, and Guéritaud’s construction, of a veering triangulation from a loom space, are essentially inverses.
We do this by interpreting points and leaves of the link space as combinatorial objects in the veering triangulation.
Here is one application of Theorem
11.9
.

.

Suppose that
M
is a connected three-manifold.
Suppose that
\mathcal{V}
is a (locally) veering triangulation on
M
.
Suppose that
\Gamma=\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}\circ\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\pi_{1}(M))
is the induced subgroup of
\operatorname{Aut}(\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}\circ\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}({\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}))
.
Then
\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}\circ\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}({\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}})/\Gamma
is canonically isomorphic to
\mathcal{V}
.

The material in Appendix
A
is required for our forthcoming work with Manning described in Section
1.3
.
Appendix
B
generalises
[
53
]
from carried loops to carried arcs; Corollary
B.0.2
is used here in Chapters
2
and
4
.
Appendix
C
is an index of commonly used notation.

Chapter 2
Circular orders and taut ideal triangulations

2.1.
Circular orders

References for this material include
[
60
, Section 2]
,
[
7
, Section 2.6]
, and
[
16
, Chapter 3]
.

Definition 2.0.1
.

Suppose that
\Delta
is a set.
A function
\mathcal{O}\colon\Delta^{3}\to\{-1,0,1\}
is a
circular order on
\Delta
if for all
a,b,c,d\in\Delta
we have the following.

•

\mathcal{O}(a,b,c)\neq 0
if and only if all of
a
,
b
, and
c
are distinct.

•

\mathcal{O}(a,b,c)=\mathcal{O}(c,a,b)=-\mathcal{O}(b,a,c)
.

•

If
\mathcal{O}(a,b,d)=\mathcal{O}(b,c,d)=1
then
\mathcal{O}(a,c,d)=1
.

Example 2.0.2
.

The canonical example is the unit circle
S^{1}
equipped with the anticlockwise circular order.

Definition 2.0.3
.

We say that a circular order
\mathcal{O}
on
\Delta
is
dense
if, for all
a,b,c\in\Delta
, if
\mathcal{O}(a,b,c)=1
, then there is a point
d\in\Delta
so that
\mathcal{O}(b,d,c)=1
.

Definition 2.0.4
.

Suppose that
\Gamma
is a group acting on
\Delta
.
A circular order
\mathcal{O}
on
\Delta
is
\Gamma
–invariant
if for all
a,b,c\in\Delta
and for all
\gamma\in\Gamma
we have
\mathcal{O}(\gamma a,\gamma b,\gamma c)=\mathcal{O}(a,b,c)
.

Example 2.0.5
.

A second example comes from taking
F
to be a compact, connected, oriented surface with non-empty boundary and with
\chi(F)<0
.
Let
{\widetilde{F}}
be the universal cover of
F
.
Let
\Delta_{F}
be the set of boundary components of
{\widetilde{F}}
;
these are the
cusps
of
{\widetilde{F}}
.
Note that the orientation on
F
gives a dense
\pi_{1}(F)
–invariant circular order on
\Delta_{F}
.
See Figure
2.2
.

Figure 2.2.
Equipping
F
with an ideal triangulation and lifting to
{\widetilde{F}}
yields the Farey tessellation.
The cusps of
{\widetilde{F}}
correspond to the rational numbers
\mathbb{QP}^{1}
.
Figure by Roice Nelson.

For our next example we go up another dimension.
Suppose that
M
is a compact, connected, oriented three-manifold, with non-empty boundary, and where all boundary components are tori.
Let
{\widetilde{M}}
be the universal cover of
M
.
Let
\Delta_{M}
be the set of boundary components of
{\widetilde{M}}
;
we call these the
cusps
of
{\widetilde{M}}
.
Note that these are naturally in bijection with the peripheral subgroups of
\pi_{1}(M)
.
We want to find a dense
\pi_{1}(M)
–invariant circular order on
\Delta_{M}
.

Example 2.0.6
.

Suppose that
F
is a compact, connected, oriented surface with negative Euler characteristic and with non-empty boundary.
Suppose that
f\colon F\to F
is an orientation-preserving homeomorphism.
Define the
surface bundle

M=M_{f}
with
monodromy

f
to be the three-manifold obtained by forming
F\times[0,1]
and then identifying the point
(x,1)
with
(f(x),0)
, for all
x\in F
.
The surfaces
F_{t}=F\times\{t\}
are the
fibres
of the bundle
M
.
The intervals
\{x\}\times[0,1]
glue together to give an oriented flow
\Phi
transverse to the fibres.
We choose the orientation on
M
so that the orientation of
F_{0}
, followed by the orientation of
\Phi
, makes a right-handed frame.
One simple example is
M_{\operatorname{Id}}\mathrel{\cong}F\times S^{1}
;
more interesting ones are given in Example
3.0.1
.

We now identify
F
with the fibre
F_{0}
.
This induces a homeomorphism between
{\widetilde{F}}\times\mathbb{R}
and
{\widetilde{M}}
and thus a bijection between
\Delta_{F}
and
\Delta_{M}
.
The orientation of
{\widetilde{F}}
gives us a dense circular order
\mathcal{O}_{F}
on
\Delta_{F}
and thus on
\Delta_{M}
.
Finally, note that
\mathcal{O}_{F}
is
\pi_{1}(M)
–invariant.

Remark 2.0.7
.

The circular order
\mathcal{O}_{F}
of Example
2.0.6
possesses a certain kind of rigidity.
Suppose that
F^{\prime}
is a properly embedded, connected, oriented surface in
M=M_{f}
.
Suppose that
F^{\prime}
is transverse to the flow
\Phi
, meets every flow line of
\Phi
, and the orientations of
F^{\prime}
and
\Phi
, in that order, form a right-handed frame in
M
.
Then
F^{\prime}
is a fibre of a surface bundle structure on
M
.
We deduce that
F^{\prime}
gives a circular order
\mathcal{O}_{F^{\prime}}
on the cusps of
{\widetilde{M}}
.
Since
{\widetilde{F}}
and
{\widetilde{F}}^{\prime}
are both transverse to the flow,
they are both homeomorphic to the leaf-space.
We deduce that
\mathcal{O}_{F}=\mathcal{O}_{F^{\prime}}
.

2.3.
Taut ideal triangulations

To discuss more general examples, we will replace bundle structures by ideal triangulations equipped in various ways.
From now on we will always assume that
M
is a tame connected three-manifold, possibly with boundary.

A
triangulation

\mathcal{T}
is a collection
\{t_{i}\}
of
model tetrahedra
(copies of
\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{4}\mathbin{\mid}x_{i}\geq 0,\sum x_{i}=1\}
) glued via
face pairings

\{\phi_{j}\}
(linear maps between faces).
The faces, edges, and vertices of a model tetrahedron will be called
model cells
.
Suppose that
\mathcal{T}
is a triangulation and that
|\mathcal{T}|
is its
realisation space
(the disjoint union of the
t_{i}
modulo the maps
\phi_{j}
).
We will assume that the interior of every model cell of
\mathcal{T}
embeds in
|\mathcal{T}|
.
Following
[
58
, Section 4.2]
, we say
\mathcal{T}
is an
ideal triangulation
of
M
if
|\mathcal{T}|
, minus its zero-skeleton, is homeomorphic to the interior of
M
.
We use
{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}
to denote the induced ideal triangulation of the universal cover,
{\widetilde{M}}
.
The
cusps
of
\mathcal{T}
, denoted simply as
\Delta_{\mathcal{T}}
, is the set of ideal vertices of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}
.
When
M
is (the interior of a) compact three-manifold with Klein bottle and torus boundary components,
there is an induced bijection between
\Delta_{\mathcal{T}}
and
\Delta_{M}
(the peripheral subgroups of
\pi_{1}(M)
).

A
taut angle structure

[
28
, Definition 1.1]
on an ideal triangulation
\mathcal{T}
is an assignment of dihedral angles as follows.

•

Every model edge of every model tetrahedron has dihedral angle zero or
\pi
.

•

For every edge, the angle sum of its models is
2\pi
.

•

For every model vertex, the angle sum of the three adjacent model edges is
\pi
.

A model tetrahedron
t
with such dihedral angles is called a
taut
tetrahedron.
The four model edges with angle zero give the
equator
of
t
.
See Figure
2.4a
.

A
transverse
taut angle structure on
\mathcal{T}

[
28
, Definition 1.2]
has, in addition to the dihedral angles, a co-orientation on the faces of
\mathcal{T}
.
These are arranged so that, for any pair of model faces
f,f^{\prime}
of any model tetrahedron
t
with common model edge
e
, we have the following.

•

The dihedral angle of
e
inside of
t
is zero if and only if exactly one of the co-orientations on
f,f^{\prime}
points into
t
.

We extend the co-orientation to the edges of
\mathcal{T}
so that, along an edge
e
, it points into the tetrahedron
t
above
e
.
We call
e
the
lower
edge for
t
.
Reversing orientations, we obtain the
upper
edge for
t
.
We define the
upper
and
lower
faces for
t
similarly.
The co-orientation on the faces of a model taut tetrahedron are shown in Figure
2.4a
.
Note that our terminology (following
[
28
]
) is a slight variant of the original definition
[
32
, page 370]
.

\labellist
\hair

2pt

\pinlabel
0 at 20 130

\pinlabel
0 at 240 120

\pinlabel
0 at 135 27

\pinlabel
0 at 135 217

\pinlabel
\pi
at 125 140

\pinlabel
\pi
at 125 87

\endlabellist

(
a
)
Co-orientations and angles in a transverse taut tetrahedron.

(
b
)
Co-orientations around an edge.

Figure 2.4.

In the following, a transverse taut angle structure on
\mathcal{T}
will be called simply a
transverse taut structure
.

2.5.
Horizontal branched surface

References for this material include
[
43
, Section 1.2]
and
[
7
, Section 6.3]
.
Suppose that
B
is a branched surface.
The one-skeleton
B^{(1)}
is here called the
branch locus
.

Remark 2.0.8
.

This terminology is fairly common in the literature;
for example, see
[
43
, page 26]
and
[
32
, page 371]
and
[
39
, page 157]
.
It is called the singular locus in
[
7
, Definition 6.16]
and is also closely related to the
sutures
of
sutured manifolds

[
19
, Definition 2.6]
.

The branch locus decomposes as a union of
branch components
:
these are connected one-manifolds immersed into
M
meeting (and possibly self-intersecting) only at the vertices of the zero-skeleton
B^{(0)}
.
The components of
B-B^{(1)}
are called the
sectors
of
B
.

Suppose now that
M
is a three-manifold equipped with a transverse taut ideal triangulation
\mathcal{T}
.
We now isotope the two-skeleton of
\mathcal{T}
near each edge, as shown in Figure
2.4b
, to obtain
B(\mathcal{T})
:
a co-oriented branched surface without vertices
[
32
, page 371]
.
The branched surface
B(\mathcal{T})
is
taut

[
32
, page 374]
;
this gives taut ideal triangulations their name.
Note that the sectors of
B(\mathcal{T})
are exactly the original triangular faces of
\mathcal{T}
.

Let
{\widetilde{B}}
be the preimage of
B(\mathcal{T})
in the universal cover
{\widetilde{M}}
.
Note that
{\widetilde{B}}
is again a co-oriented branched surface.
We now have a basic result.

Theorem 2.0.8
.

Suppose that
M
is a three-manifold equipped with a taut ideal triangulation
\mathcal{T}
.
Suppose that
t
is a model tetrahedron of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}
.
Then
t
embeds in
{\widetilde{M}}
;
furthermore, the model vertices of
t
lie in four distinct cusp of
\Delta_{\mathcal{T}}
.

Proof 2.0.9
.

Let
{\widetilde{B}}
be the preimage, in
{\widetilde{M}}
, of
B(\mathcal{T})
.
Let
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
be the lower model faces of
t
.
Let
f
and
g
be the upper model faces of
t
.
Note that
f\cup g
is a connected surface carried by
{\widetilde{B}}
, as is
f^{\prime}\cup g^{\prime}
.
From
[
53
, Corollary 1.1]
we deduce that each of
f\cup g
and
f^{\prime}\cup g^{\prime}
is a disk embedded in
{\widetilde{M}}
.
From Corollary
B.0.2
we deduce that each of
f\cup g
and
f^{\prime}\cup g^{\prime}
meets four distinct cusps of
\Delta_{\mathcal{T}}
.
(In
[
53
]
we assumed that
M
is compact, but that hypothesis is never used.)
It follows that no pair of model faces (or model edges) of
t
are identified in
{\widetilde{M}}
.

2.7.
Layered triangulations

Suppose that
B
is a general branched surface.
Recall that a
tie-neighbourhood

N(B)
of
B
is a regular neighbourhood of
B
equipped with a foliation by intervals, called
ties
.
See
[
43
, Figure 1.2]
.
A surface
F
, properly embedded in
M
, is
carried
by
B
if it is properly isotopic into
N(B)
and is there transverse to the ties.
A carried surface
F
is
fully carried
if, after the isotopy, it intersects all ties.
Properties of
B
are generally inherited by its carried surfaces: decomposition into sectors, co-orientations, and so on.

Definition 2.0.10
.

Suppose that
M
is a three-manifold equipped with a transverse taut ideal triangulation
\mathcal{T}
.
A
layering

\mathcal{K}=(K_{i})
of
\mathcal{T}
is a collection of pairwise disjoint surfaces, all carried by
B(\mathcal{T})
.
Here the index set is either

•

\mathbb{Z}/n\mathbb{Z}
if
\mathcal{T}
has
n
tetrahedra or

•

\mathbb{Z}
if
\mathcal{T}
is infinite.

The surfaces
K_{i}
inherit a transverse orientation from
B(\mathcal{T})
.
Furthermore they have the following property.

•

For every tetrahedron
t
of
\mathcal{T}
there is a unique
j
so that
t-N(B(\mathcal{T}))
lies in the component of
M-(K_{j}\sqcup K_{j+1})
immediately above
K_{j}
and immediately below
K_{j+1}
.

•

For every
K_{i}

there is a tetrahedron
t_{i}
immediately above
K_{i}
.

We call the surfaces
K_{i}
the
layers
of
\mathcal{K}
.

Lemma 2.0.11
.

Suppose that
M
is a three-manifold equipped with a transverse taut ideal triangulation
\mathcal{T}
.
If
\mathcal{T}
is layered then so is
{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}
.

Proof 2.0.12
.

If
M
is simply connected there is nothing to prove.
Suppose that
\mathcal{F}=(F_{i})
is the given layering of
\mathcal{T}
and
t_{i}
is the unique tetrahedron between
F_{i}
and
F_{i+1}
.
Fixing
i
, we define an
elevation
of
F_{i}
to be an induced map from
{\widetilde{F}}_{i}
to
{\widetilde{M}}
.

The elevations of the
F_{i}
do not give a layering of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}
;
adjacent elevations of
F_{i}
and
F_{i+1}
cobound infinitely many tetrahedra (all of which are lifts of
t_{i}
).
Thus we must rearrange the order of attachment.

Fix
G
, an elevation of
F_{0}
.
Since
G
is properly embedded,
it separates
{\widetilde{M}}
.
Choose a linear order
(t^{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
on the tetrahedra of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}
lying
above

G
.
We now build a layering onto
G
.
By induction suppose that we have attached all of the tetrahedra
(t^{i})_{i=0}^{n-1}
, plus perhaps finitely more, to
G
.
Now consider the tetrahedron
t^{n}
.
If it is already attached, there is nothing to prove.
If it is not we proceed as follows.
Consider all possible paths
\gamma
in
{\widetilde{M}}
so that

•

\gamma
starts on
G
and ends in
t^{n}
,

•

\gamma
is transverse to the two-skeleton, and

•

\gamma
crosses each face
f
in the direction of its co-orientation.

Note that each such
\gamma
crosses the elevations of the layers
F_{i}
the same number of times; we call this number,
h(t^{n})
, the
height
of
t^{n}
.
Thus each
\gamma
meets at most
h(t^{n})
tetrahedra.
Also, since a tetrahedron has only two lower faces, there are at most
2^{h(t^{n})}
such paths.
So, we attach all of the finitely many tetrahedra met by any of these paths, in height order.
The last one attached is
t^{n}
, completing the inductive step.

Doing the same below
G
completes the proof.

2.8.
Compatibility

Suppose that
M
is an oriented three-manifold equipped with a transverse taut ideal triangulation
\mathcal{T}
.
Recall that
\Delta_{\mathcal{T}}
is the set of cusps of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}
.
Suppose that
f
is a face of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}
.
Let
\Delta_{f}\subset\Delta_{\mathcal{T}}
be the cusps that
f
meets.
By Theorem
2.6
there are exactly three of these.
The co-orientation of
f
and the induced orientation of
{\widetilde{M}}
picks out a unique circular order
\mathcal{O}_{f}
on
\Delta_{f}
(say, anticlockwise as viewed from above).

Definition 2.0.13
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{O}
is a circular order on
\Delta\subset\Delta_{\mathcal{T}}
.
We say that
\mathcal{O}
is
compatible
with
\mathcal{T}
if it has the following property.
For any face
f\in{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}
if
\Delta_{f}\subset\Delta

then
\mathcal{O}|\Delta_{f}=\mathcal{O}_{f}
.

Lemma 2.0.14
.

Suppose that
M
is an oriented three-manifold equipped with a transverse taut ideal triangulation
\mathcal{T}
.
If
{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}
is layered then there is a unique compatible circular order
\mathcal{O}
on
\Delta_{\mathcal{T}}
.
Furthermore,
\mathcal{O}
is dense and
\pi_{1}(M)
–invariant.

Proof 2.0.15
.

We are given a layering
(F_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
of
{\widetilde{M}}
.
Applying
[
53
, Corollary 1.1]
, each
F_{i}
is a copy of the Farey tessellation.
Each gives the next via a single flip across a tetrahedron.
So
F_{i}
and
F_{i+1}
meet the same subset of cusps of
\Delta_{\mathcal{T}}
.
But every cusp meets some
F_{j}
.
Thus every
F_{i}
meets all cusps of
\Delta_{\mathcal{T}}
.

By Corollary
B.0.2
, the layer
F_{i}
meets every cusp at most once.
Since
F_{i}
is disk, it gives a circular order
\mathcal{O}_{i}
on
\Delta_{\mathcal{T}}
.
Picking an oriented edge of
F_{i}
determines an order isomorphism between
(\Delta_{\mathcal{T}},\mathcal{O}_{i})
and
\mathbb{QP}^{1}
, the rational points of
S^{1}
.
Thus the circular order
\mathcal{O}_{i}
is dense.
Since
F_{i}
differs from
F_{j}
by a finite number of flips, we deduce that
\mathcal{O}_{i}=\mathcal{O}_{j}
.
Also, every face of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}
lies in some
F_{i}
.
So, setting
\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{O}_{0}
, we deduce that
\mathcal{O}
is compatible with
\mathcal{T}
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
is another compatible circular order on
\Delta_{\mathcal{T}}
.
Then
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}
and
\mathcal{O}
agree on all faces of
F_{0}
;
we deduce that
\mathcal{O}^{\prime}=\mathcal{O}
.
Thus
\mathcal{O}
is the unique circular order on
\Delta_{\mathcal{T}}
compatible with
\mathcal{T}
.

Finally, fix any
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(M)
and define
\mathcal{O}_{\gamma}
via

\mathcal{O}_{\gamma}(a,b,c)=\mathcal{O}(\gamma a,\gamma b,\gamma c).

The action of
\gamma
on
\Delta_{\mathcal{T}}
is bijective, so
\mathcal{O}_{\gamma}
is a circular order on
\Delta_{\mathcal{T}}
.
Now, if
f
is a face of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}
with vertices
x
,
y
, and
z
then
\mathcal{O}_{\gamma}(x,y,z)=\mathcal{O}(\gamma x,\gamma y,\gamma z)
.
Note that
\gamma x
,
\gamma y
, and
\gamma z
are the vertices of the face
\gamma f
.
Also,
\gamma
sends the co-orientation of
f
to that of
\gamma f
;
also
\gamma
preserves the orientation of
M
.
It follows that
\mathcal{O}_{\gamma}
is compatible with
\mathcal{T}
.
Thus, by uniqueness, we have
\mathcal{O}_{\gamma}=\mathcal{O}
.

Chapter 3
Examples

Here we give examples of circular orders coming from transverse taut structures.

3.1.
Layered surface bundles

We follow the notation of Example
2.0.6
.

Example 3.0.1
.

Suppose
M=M_{f}
is an oriented surface bundle with fibre
F
and monodromy
f
.
Let
\mathcal{T}
be a layered ideal triangulation of
M
.
The best-known example is shown in Figure
3.2
;
it is the canonical triangulation of the figure-eight knot complement.
In general, since
\mathcal{T}
is layered, Lemma
2.0.11
implies that
{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}
is layered.
Thus, by Lemma
2.0.14
the cusps of
\Delta_{\mathcal{T}}
admit a unique circular order
\mathcal{O}_{F}
which is compatible with
\mathcal{T}
.
Furthermore, it is
\pi_{1}(M)
–invariant.
Since the layering of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}
has
{\widetilde{F}}
as one of its layers, the circular order
\mathcal{O}_{F}
is the same as the one constructed in Example
2.0.6
.

Figure 3.2.
The veering structure on the canonical triangulation for the figure-eight knot complement.
This manifold is
m004
in the SnapPy census
[
12
]
.
This veering structure is
cPcbbbiht_12
in the veering census
[
21
]
.
See Chapter
5
for the definition of veering; in this section we only use the transverse taut structure.
The
\pi
–edges of the transverse taut structure are the diagonals of the squares.
The model edges on the sides of the squares all have dihedral angle zero.

3.3.
Geometric structures

Before we give our next family of examples, there is a bit of necessary background.
Useful references include
[
58
, Chapter 4]
and
[
61
, Section 2]
.
Take
\mathcal{P}=\mathbb{C}-\{0,1\}
.
Suppose that
M
is a compact, connected, oriented three-manifold with boundary being a single torus.
Suppose that
\mathcal{T}=(t_{i})_{i=0}^{n-1}
is an ideal triangulation of
M
.
We say that
\mathcal{T}
admits a
geometric structure
if there is a tuple of
shapes

\rho_{\infty}=(z_{i})\in\mathcal{P}^{n}
as follows.

•

The tuple
\rho_{\infty}
solves Thurston’s gluing and holonomy equations (called the completeness equations in
[
61
, page 800]
).

•

Each
z_{i}\in\rho_{\infty}
has positive imaginary part.

It follows that
\rho_{\infty}
gives the interior of
M
a complete, finite volume, hyperbolic metric.
The tuples of
\mathcal{P}^{n}
that solve the gluing equations (ignoring the holonomy and positivity) make up the
shape variety

\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{T})
.
Let
\mathcal{S}_{\infty}
be the irreducible component of
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{T})
which contains
\rho_{\infty}
.
Our assumptions on
M
and the existence of
\rho_{\infty}
imply that
\mathcal{S}_{\infty}
is a complex curve
[
45
, page 314]
.

Fix a triangle
f
of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}
and a circular order on ideal vertices of
f
.
This choice allows us to define, for every
\rho\in\mathcal{S}_{\infty}
, a
developing map

\operatorname{dev}_{\rho}\,\colon\,\,{\widetilde{M}}\,\,\to\,\,\mathbb{H}^{3}

We extend
\operatorname{dev}_{\rho}
to give a function from
\Delta_{\mathcal{T}}
, the set of cusps, to
\mathbb{CP}^{1}=\partial_{\infty}\mathbb{H}^{3}
.
Our choices ensure that the vertices of
f
are sent to
0
,
1
, and
\infty
, respectively.

Definition 3.0.2
.

If
u
and
v
are distinct cusps then we say that
u
and
v

collide
at
\rho\in\mathcal{S}_{\infty}
if
\operatorname{dev}_{\rho}(u)=\operatorname{dev}_{\rho}(v)
.

Lemma 3.0.3
.

Distinct cusps
u
and
v
collide only finitely many times.

Proof 3.0.4
.

For any cusp
w\in\Delta_{\mathcal{T}}
we define a function
\operatorname{dev}(w)\colon\mathcal{S}_{\infty}\to\mathbb{CP}^{1}
by taking
\rho\mapsto\operatorname{dev}_{\rho}(w)
. Note that the coordinate functions, restricted to
\mathcal{S}_{\infty}
, are meromorphic. Also,
\operatorname{dev}(w)
can be written as a rational function in terms of the coordinates. Thus
\operatorname{dev}(w)
is also meromorphic.

Since
u
and
v
are distinct, the functions
\operatorname{dev}(u)
and
\operatorname{dev}(v)
disagree at
\rho_{\infty}
. Thus their difference is not identically zero.

Figure 3.4.
The triangulation of the universal cover of the cusp torus of the figure-eight knot complement, as induced by the canonical triangulation (shown in Figure
3.2
). The tetrahedron shapes are all regular (
z=w=e^{\pi i/3}
). The dashed rectangle is a fundamental domain for the tiling, with the meridian
m
drawn vertically and the longitude
l
drawn horizontally. For more general shapes
z
and
w
we obtain the complex dihedral angles shown, following the usual conventions
[
58
, page 47]
.

3.5.
Exotic taut structures

We are now ready for our next example.
Let
M
be the figure-eight knot complement.
Let
\mathcal{T}
be the canonical triangulation of
M
, shown in Figure
3.2
, there equipped with a taut structure.
The triangulation
\mathcal{T}
admits two more taut structures, each of which is again transverse.
These
exotic
structures are generated by
leading-trailing deformations
of the veering triangulation
[
17
, Proposition 6.8]
.
We have the following.

Theorem 3.0.4
.

Each of the exotic taut structures on
\mathcal{T}
admits uncountably many compatible circular orders.

This result, and the techniques needed for its proof, are not used later in the paper.
Thus the proof can be skipped upon a first reading.
However, see Question
3.0.6
.

Proof 3.0.5

(Proof of Theorem
3.6
)
.

The triangulation
\mathcal{T}
induces a triangulation of the boundary torus
\partial M
.
Taking preimages gives a triangulation of the universal cover of the cusp torus;
this is shown in Figure
3.4
along with other details.
This is a view of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}
as seen from a fixed cusp
c_{\infty}
.
The cusps connected to
c_{\infty}
by an edge of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}
form a combinatorial copy of the lattice
\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}e^{\pi i/3}
.
Let
c_{0,0}
be any fixed cusp in this lattice.
Let
m
and
l
be the usual meridian and longitude of the figure-eight knot.
We define a sublattice by taking
c_{p,q}=m^{p}l^{q}(c_{0,0})
.

As shown in Figure
3.4
, we label the corners of the cusp triangles with the corresponding complex dihedral angles of the ideal tetrahedra. We can now derive Thurston’s gluing equations for this triangulation. The equations for the two ideal edges are identical: namely

z(z-1)w(w-1)=1.

As discussed above, the solutions
\rho=(z,w)\in\mathcal{P}^{2}
form the shape variety
\mathcal{S}(\mathcal{T})
. Again consulting Figure
3.4
, we compute the holonomies for
m
and
l
and obtain

H(m)=1/(w(1-z))\quad\mbox{and}\quad H(l)=z^{2}(1-z)^{2}.

Recall that Thurston’s hyperbolic Dehn surgery equation
[
58
, page 57]
, with real
surgery coordinates

\mu
and
\lambda
, is

\mu\log H(m)+\lambda\log H(l)=2\pi i.

Here analytic continuation must be used to obtain the correct branch of the logarithm.
Suppose we have found shapes
z
and
w
with positive imaginary part solving the gluing equations, as well as
\mu
and
\lambda
solving the surgery equation.
Then the tetrahedra with those shapes fit together to give an incomplete hyperbolic structure on
M
.

We now concentrate on the case where
-4<\mu<4
and
\lambda=1
.
This is the top edge of the hourglass shown in Figure
3.7
.
These “surgeries” are on the “boundary” of Dehn surgery space: the tetrahedron shapes
z
and
w
are real and their volume has decreased to zero.
The shapes along the top edge give the triangulation a fixed transverse taut structure: one of the two exotic structures from
[
17
, Proposition 6.8]
.
The other exotic structure corresponds to the bottom edge of the hourglass.
Again, see Figure
3.7
.

Figure 3.7.
The Dehn surgery space of the figure-eight knot complement.
We call the white region the “hourglass”.
The complement of the hourglass is the component of the positive volume locus which contains the shapes giving the complete, finite volume, hyperbolic structure.
The origin
(0,0)
is at the centre, the point
(0,1)
is the midpoint of the top of the hourglass, and the point
(4,1)
is the top right corner of the hourglass.
For a discussion of the colour scheme and more see
[
25
]
.
Also see
[
58
, page 4.21]
and
[
65
, page 70]
.

Let
\mathcal{I}\subset\mathcal{S}_{\infty}
be the set of (pairs of) shapes corresponding to the top edge of the hourglass.
So, for each
\mu\in(-4,4)
we have shapes
\rho_{\mu}=(z_{\mu},w_{\mu})\in\mathcal{I}
and a map
\operatorname{dev}_{\mu}\colon\Delta_{\mathcal{T}}\to\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}
.
We adapt the conventions that
\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}=\mathbb{R}\cup\infty
and that
\operatorname{dev}_{\mu}(c_{\infty})=\infty
.

Figure
3.8
is a cartoon of the image of the developing map for the shapes
(\mu,\lambda)=(-1,1)
.
The vertical positions of features are purely combinatorial; all of the cusps of the original lattice
\mathbb{Z}\oplus\mathbb{Z}e^{\pi i/3}
map into a single horizontal line.
For some
\mu
(for example, those at the rational points)
\operatorname{dev}_{\mu}
is not one-to-one and we do not get a circular order.
However, by Lemma
3.0.3
, a pair of cusps collide at only a finite number of points of
\mathcal{I}
.
The collection of pairs of distinct cusps is countable;
thus the set of
\rho_{\mu}\in\mathcal{I}
having some collision is only countable.
The uncountable remainder give circular orders;
these are denoted by
\mathcal{O}_{\mu}
.
The definition of
\operatorname{dev}_{\mu}
implies that
\mathcal{O}_{\mu}
is compatible with
\mathcal{T}
.
The deck group
\pi_{1}(M)
acts on cusps
\Delta_{\mathcal{T}}
directly and acts on
\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}=\mathbb{R}\cup\{\infty\}
via the holonomy representation to
\operatorname{PSL}(2,\mathbb{R})
.
The circular orders
\mathcal{O}_{\mu}
are obtained by pulling back from
\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}
and so are invariant.

Finally, we show that all of the circular orders
\mathcal{O}_{\mu}
are distinct.
We begin with an example.
Figure
3.9
shows the tetrahedron shapes at Dehn surgery coordinates
(\mu,\lambda)=(-1.1,1)
.
Since these are not integral, unlike Figure
3.4
the associated developing map now does not factor through a tiling.
Instead, we have a cut along the negative real axis where the ends of the longitude and meridian do not line up.
The cusps associated to the end of the longitude have slid to the left relative to the cusps associated to the end of the meridian.
However, if we go eleven times backwards along the meridian and ten times forwards along the longitude, the corresponding holonomy is again the identity, and again the cusps line up.

In general, if
\mu=p/q
is rational (and in lowest terms) then the holonomy of
m^{p}l^{q}
is trivial.
We deduce that
\operatorname{dev}_{\mu}(c_{p,q})=\operatorname{dev}_{\mu}(c_{0,0})
.
If
\mu<p/q
then
\operatorname{dev}_{\mu}(c_{p,q})
is to the left of
\operatorname{dev}_{\mu}(c_{0,0})
in our picture.
If
\mu>p/q
then
\operatorname{dev}_{\mu}(c_{p,q})
is to the right of
\operatorname{dev}_{\mu}(c_{0,0})
in our picture.

Therefore, if
\rho_{\mu}\in\mathcal{I}
has no collisions, then the circular order
\mathcal{O}_{\mu}
satisfies

\mathcal{O}_{\mu}(\operatorname{dev}_{\mu}(c_{\infty}),\operatorname{dev}_{\mu}(c_{0,0}),\operatorname{dev}_{\mu}(c_{p,q}))=\pm 1

as
\mu<p/q
or
p/q<\mu
, respectively.
Now, for any two circular orders
\mathcal{O}_{\mu}
and
\mathcal{O}_{\mu^{\prime}}
, there is a rational number
p/q
between
\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}
;
therefore
\mathcal{O}_{\mu}
and
\mathcal{O}_{\mu^{\prime}}
are distinct.
This completes the proof of Theorem
3.6
.

A cartoon of the image of the developing map, as applied to the triangulation of the universal cover of the boundary torus of the figure-eight knot complement. Here the tetrahedron shapes are

and

[

]

. Since the shapes are real, the tetrahedra are flat. Thus the cusp triangles degenerate to intervals. The vertical scale in this figure has no geometric significance;
we draw the triangles as rectangles instead of as intervals in order to see their gluings. The vertical sides of each rectangle give the vertices (of the cusp triangle) with dihedral angle zero;
the small black arrow in each rectangle points at the vertex with dihedral angle
\pi
.

.

.

Question 3.0.6
.

As discussed in Example
3.0.1
the transverse taut structures coming from layered triangulations of surface bundles have unique compatible circular orders.
As we shall show in Theorem
5.2
, veering triangulations also have unique compatible circular orders.

On the one hand, most layered triangulations have no veering structure.
On the other hand, Hodgson, Rubinstein, Tillmann, and the third author
[
28
, Section 4]
give a veering triangulation for the SnapPy manifold
s227
;
they also show that
s227
is not fibred.
Thus Theorem
5.2
gives examples of circular orders that do not come from fibrations.
We give many more non-fibred manifolds with veering triangulations in
[
21
,
veering_census_with_data.txt
]
.
We also use
veering Dehn surgery
to give an infinite family of such manifolds
[
52
]
.

However, from Theorem
3.6
we have that the exotic transverse taut structures (of
[
17
, Proposition 6.8]
) each have uncountably many compatible circular orders.
This raises several questions:
Suppose that we are given a three-manifold equipped with a transverse taut ideal triangulation.
What are necessary and sufficient conditions on the taut structure to ensure there is a unique compatible circular order?
Is this property decidable?
Is there a transverse taut ideal triangulation that admits
no
compatible circular orders?

Chapter 4
Geography of taut triangulations

In this section, after reviewing material on
train tracks
, we introduce the new concepts of
landscapes
,
continents
, and
continental exhaustions
.

4.1.
Train tracks

Here we will closely follow the drawing style, and thus the imposed definitions, introduced in
[
1
, Figure 11]
.
A
pre-track

\tau
in a surface
F
is a locally finite, properly embedded, smooth graph.
The vertices of
\tau
are called
switches
while the edges of
\tau
are called
branches
.
Every switch of
\tau
is equipped with a tangent line.
We call
\tau
a
train track
if

•

every switch in the interior of
F
has at least one branch entering on each of its two sides and

•

every switch in
\partial F
has its tangent perpendicular to
\partial F
and has valence at least one.

This is a variant of the usual definition of train tracks (see, for example,
[
58
, Definition 8.9.1]
);
we are omitting the Euler characteristic condition on the components of
F-\tau
.

Suppose that
s\in\tau
is a switch in the interior of
F
.
We say
s
is
large
if
s
has exactly two branches entering on each side.
We say
s
is
small
if
s
has exactly one branch entering on each side.
We say
s
is
mixed
if one branch enters on one side of
s
and two enter on the other.
See Figure
4.2
.

(
a
)
A large switch.

(
b
)
A small switch.

(
c
)
A mixed switch.

Figure 4.2.
Switches.

We may
split
(either to the right or to the left) the track
\tau
along a large switch to obtain a new track
\tau^{\prime}\subset F
. See Figure
4.3
. After a split the components of
F-\tau^{\prime}
are homeomorphic to those of
F-\tau
. The reverse of a split is called a
fold
.

Figure 4.3.
A large switch can split either to the left or to the right.

A
train route
in a train track
\tau
is a smooth embedding of
[0,1]
, of
S^{1}
, of
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, or of
\mathbb{R}
into
\tau
.
Such a route is called, respectively, a
train interval
, a
train loop
, a
train ray
, or a
train line
.

4.4.
Landscapes and rivers

For the next several sections, we fix
M
a three-manifold equipped with a transverse taut ideal triangulation
\mathcal{T}
.
Let
B=B(\mathcal{T})
be the associated taut branched surface, made from the faces of
\mathcal{T}
.
Let
{\widetilde{B}}
be the preimage of
B
in the universal cover
{\widetilde{M}}
.

Definition 4.0.1
.

A
landscape

L\subset{\widetilde{B}}
is a connected embedded carried surface, which is a union of triangles of
{\widetilde{B}}
.

A landscape
L
inherits an ideal triangulation and a co-orientation from
B
.
Boundary components of
L
(if any) are necessarily carried by edges of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}
.
We call these
coastal edges
of
L
.
We now restate
[
53
, Corollary 1.1]
and Corollary
B.0.2
in terms of landscapes.

Lemma 4.0.2
.

The interior of a landscape
L
is an open disk.
Any pair of vertices of a landscape are distinct cusps of
\Delta_{\mathcal{T}}
.
∎

Thus any landscape
L
induces a circular order
\mathcal{O}_{L}
on the cusps of
\Delta_{L}\subset\Delta_{\mathcal{T}}
;
furthermore this circular order is compatible with
\mathcal{T}
in the sense of Definition
2.0.13
.

Simple examples of landscapes include: a single face of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}
, the upper or lower boundary of a single tetrahedron of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}
, or any elevation of a surface carried by
B
.

Definition 4.0.3
.

Suppose that
f
is a face of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}
.
Suppose that
t
is the tetrahedron attached to
f
, above
f
.
Let
e
be the lower
\pi
–edge of
t
;
let
e^{\prime}
and
e^{\prime\prime}
be the remaining edges of
f
.
We define
\tau^{f}
, the
upper track
for
f
as follows.
The track
\tau^{f}
has three switches;
we place these at the midpoints of the three edges.
The track
\tau^{f}
has two branches, running from
e^{\prime}
to
e
and from
e^{\prime\prime}
to
e
.
See Figure
4.5
.

The
lower track

\tau_{f}
is defined similarly;
we use the tetrahedron attached to, and immediately below,
f
.

In this section, it sometimes is convenient to think of water flowing along the branches of
\tau^{f}
;
in the notation of the previous definition, the water flows away from
e^{\prime}
and
e^{\prime\prime}
and flows towards
e
.

Definition 4.0.4
.

We define the
track-cusp
of
\tau^{f}
by taking the switch of
\tau^{f}
and adding a small neighbourhood of it in the region of
f-\tau^{f}
between the two branches.
See Figure
4.5
.
We define the track-cusp of
\tau_{f}
similarly.

Definition 4.0.5
.

Suppose that
L
is a landscape carried by
{\widetilde{B}}
.
We define the
upper track

\tau^{L}
to be the union of
\tau^{f}
as
f
runs over the ideal triangles of
L
.
We define
\tau_{L}
similarly.
See Figure
4.2
for simple examples.

\labellist
\hair

2pt

\pinlabel
f
at 88 71

\endlabellist

(
a
)
Two taut tetrahedra
t^{f}
and
t_{f}
above and below a face
f
.

\labellist
\hair

2pt

\pinlabel
\tau^{f}
[tr] at 65 42

\endlabellist

(
b
)
The upper train track
\tau^{f}
in
f
.

\labellist
\hair

2pt

\pinlabel
\tau_{f}
[tl] at 77 37

\endlabellist

(
c
)
The lower train track
\tau_{f}
in
f
.

Figure 4.5.

The upper track
\tau^{f}
points at the bottom
\pi
–edge of
t^{f}
while the lower track
\tau_{f}
points at the top
\pi
–edge of
t_{f}
.
The track-cusps in
t^{f}
and
t_{f}
are shaded darker grey.

Suppose
e
is an interior edge for a landscape
L
.
Drawing on the water analogy above, we call
e
a
sink
for
\tau^{L}
if
e\cap\tau^{L}
is a large switch (that is, the flows in the two adjacent triangles flow into
e
).
We call
e
a
fall
for
\tau^{L}
if
e\cap\tau^{L}
is a mixed switch (that is, the flows cross
e
).
We call
e
a
watershed
for
\tau^{L}
if
e\cap\tau^{L}
is a small switch (that is, the flows both flow out of
e
).
Again, see Figure
4.2
.

Suppose
e
is a coastal edge for a landscape
L
.
We call
e
a
coastal sink
for
\tau^{L}
if
e
meets a track cusp of
\tau^{L}
.

Remark 4.0.6
.

Note that if
e
is a (coastal) sink for
\tau^{L}
then there is a tetrahedron, immediately above
L
, which shares (one) two faces with
L
and whose lower
\pi
–edge is equal to
e
.
There is a similar statement for
\tau_{L}
.

We end this section with a special kind of landscape.

Definition 4.0.7
.

Suppose that
R
is a landscape with finitely many triangles.
We call
R
an
upper river
if

•

every triangle of
R
meets at most two others and

•

every interior edge of
R
is a fall for
\tau^{R}
.

For an example, see Figure
5.10
(0).
Note that the flows equip
\tau^{R}
with a consistent orientation.
The first triangle of
R
is the
source
of the river.
The final edge of
R
is the
mouth
of the river.
We use
\ell(R)
to denote the number of triangles in
R
.
This is the
length
of
R
.

Lower rivers are defined using
\tau_{L}
.

Lemma 4.0.8
.

Let
R
be a maximal upper river in a landscape
L
.
Then the mouth of
R
is a (possibly coastal) sink for
\tau^{L}
.
There is a similar statement for lower rivers. ∎

4.6.
Continents

Let
M
be an connected oriented three-manifold equipped with a transverse taut ideal triangulation
\mathcal{T}
.
Recall that
{\widetilde{B}}
is the preimage, in
{\widetilde{M}}
, of the co-oriented branched surface
B(\mathcal{T})
.

Suppose that
C\subset{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}
is a collection of model tetrahedra.
We say that
C
is
face-connected
if any pair of its tetrahedra are connected by a path in
C
(transverse to the two-skeleton of
C
).

We extend the definition of a layering to a finite face-connected collection of tetrahedra as follows.

Definition 4.0.9
.

Suppose that
C\subset{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}
is a face-connected collection of
n
model tetrahedra.
A
layering

(K_{i})_{i=0}^{n}
of
C
is exactly as in Definition
2.0.10
except that the index set is
\{0,1,\ldots,n\}
and the second condition holds for all
K_{i}
other than
K_{n}
.

We say that
C
is a
continent
if it admits a layering.

As a simple example, any single tetrahedron of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}
is a continent.
See Figure
4.7
for a cartoon of a more complicated continent;
we justify this cartoon in Lemma
4.0.13
.

Figure 4.7.
A continent.

Suppose that
C
is a continent.
We say that a face
f
of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}
is a
lower face
of
C
if

•

f
lies in
\partial C
and

•

the co-orientation on
f
points into
C
.

We define
upper faces
of
C
similarly.

Lemma 4.0.10
.

Suppose that
C\subset{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}
is a continent.
Suppose that
(L_{i})_{i=0}^{n}
is a layering of
C
.
Then the layer
L_{0}
is carried by the union of the lower faces of
C
;
the layer
L_{n}
is carried by the union of the upper faces.

Proof 4.0.11
.

We first prove that all upper faces lie in
L_{n}
and all lower faces lie in
L_{0}
.
We prove the contrapositive.
Suppose that
f
is a face of
C
not in
L_{n}
.
Let
j
be the largest index so that
L_{j}
contains
f
.
So
f
is not contained in
L_{j+1}
.
That is,
f
is a lower face of
t_{j}
.
Thus
f
is not an upper face of
C
.
We deal with lower faces similarly.

The other directions require the following.

The layers
L_{i}
are carried by
\mathcal{B}({\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}})
so they are landscapes.
By Lemma
4.0.2
, each layer
L_{i}
is an embedded triangulated disk with distinct vertices meeting distinct cusps of
\Delta_{\mathcal{T}}
.
Let
\mathcal{O}_{i}
be the induced cyclic ordering on the cusps
\Delta_{i}
of
L_{i}
.
Since
(L_{i})
is a layering, we deduce that
\Delta_{i}=\Delta_{i+1}
and
\mathcal{O}_{i}=\mathcal{O}_{i+1}
for all
i<n
.
To fix ideas, we choose an order preserving map from
\Delta_{0}
to
\partial\mathbb{H}^{2}
, the boundary of the hyperbolic plane.
The convex hull of
\Delta_{0}
is then an ideal polygon, and the edges of
L_{i}
give this polygon an ideal triangulation.

Suppose that
R
is a finite sided convex polygon in
\mathbb{H}^{2}
, perhaps with ideal vertices.
The
index
of
R
is one, minus a quarter for each material corner of
R
, minus a half for each ideal corner of
R
. By convention, the index of the empty polygon is zero;
the same holds for line segments.
For any two ideal triangles in
\mathbb{H}^{2}
, the index of their intersection is non-positive;
this is proven by examining the number of sides of the intersection.
We say that two ideal triangles in
\mathbb{H}^{2}

link
if their intersection has negative index.
See Figure
4.8
.

(
a
)
Index
-1/2
.

(
b
)
Index
-1/4
.

(
c
)
Index
-1/4
.

Figure 4.8.
Two ideal triangles can link in four ways;
the three shown in this figure, and where the triangles coincide (index
-1/2
).

From the above, we deduce the following.

Claim 1
.

Two triangles that lie in the same layer, and link, are equal.
∎

We now return to proving the lemma.
That is, we show that faces in
L_{n}
are upper faces and faces in
L_{0}
are lower faces.
Again we prove the contrapositive.
Suppose that
f
is a face of
C
which is not an upper face.
Thus there is a tetrahedron
t=t_{k}
of
C
immediately above
f
.
Thus
f
is a lower face of
t_{k}
.
We now build a sequence of faces
(f_{i})_{i=k}^{n}
, starting with
f_{k}=f
, so that
f_{i}
carries a face of
L_{i}
and so that
f_{i}
links
f
for all
i
.
For the induction step at
j\geq k
, suppose that we have
f_{j}
.
If
f_{j}
carries a face of
L_{j+1}
then set
f_{j+1}=f_{j}
.
Otherwise there is a tetrahedron
t
between
L_{j}
and
L_{j+1}
having
f_{j}
as a lower face.
Let
g_{j}
be the other lower face of
t
.
Let
h
and
h^{\prime}
be the upper faces of
t
.
Note that
f\cap(f_{j}\cup g_{j})=f\cap(h\cup h^{\prime})
.
By the additivity of index
[
44
, page 57]
, this polygon has negative index.
Thus one of the upper faces, say
h
, links
f
.
Set
f_{j+1}=h
.

By the definition of
f
and the construction of
f_{k+1}
we have that
f_{k}\neq f_{k+1}
.
Thus there is a vertical arc starting at the centre of
f_{k}
, transverse to the two-skeleton, and ending at the centre of
f_{n}
.
If
f=f_{n}
then the sequence
(f_{i})
gives a vertical loop (transverse to
B(C)
) from
f
to itself.
Since
{\widetilde{M}}
is simply connected, this contradicts
[
53
, Theorem 3.2]
.
If not, then by Claim
1
,
f
does not lie in
L_{n}
, as desired.
Similarly, all faces in
L_{0}
are lower faces.

A single continent may admit many different layerings;
these always differ by “commuting” flips.
However, by Lemma
4.0.10
, the union of the lower faces
L^{\prime}
is always the first layer, and the union of the upper faces
L
is always the last layer.
Thus we call
L^{\prime}
and
L
the
lower
and
upper
landscapes of
C
respectively.
The coastal edges of
L^{\prime}
and
L
are the same;
we call these the
coastal
edges of
C
.
We use
\Delta_{C}
to denote the set of cusps of
\Delta_{\mathcal{T}}
meeting
C
.

Corollary 4.0.12
.

Suppose that
C
is a continent in
{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}
.
Then there is a unique circular order
\mathcal{O}_{C}
on
\Delta_{C}
that is compatible with
\mathcal{T}
. ∎

Lemma 4.0.13
.

Suppose that
C
is a continent in
{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}
.
Let
L^{\prime}
and
L
be the lower and upper landscapes of
C
respectively.
Then
L^{\prime}\cap L=\partial L^{\prime}=\partial L
.

Proof 4.0.14
.

Lemma
4.0.10
implies that
L^{\prime}
and
L
cannot meet along a common face.

Suppose, for a contradiction, that
e
is an edge of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}
that lies in the interior of
L
and also lies in
L^{\prime}
.
By Corollary
4.0.12
, we deduce that
e
lies in the interior of
L^{\prime}
.

Let
a
and
b
be the cusps of
\Delta_{C}
meeting
e
.
Let
f
and
g
be the faces of
L
adjacent to
e
.
Let
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
be the faces of
L^{\prime}
adjacent to
e
.
Let
c
and
c^{\prime}
be the vertices of
f
and
f^{\prime}
(respectively) other than
a
and
b
.
We arrange matters so that
\mathcal{O}_{C}(a,b,c)=\mathcal{O}_{C}(a,b,c^{\prime})
.

Suppose that
(L^{\prime}=L_{0},L_{1},\ldots,L_{n}=L)
is a layering of
C
.
We now find a sequence of faces
(f_{i})_{i=0}^{n}
with
f_{i}
carrying a face of
L_{i}
.
We will require of each face
f_{k}
that either

•

a
and
b
are cusps of
f_{k}
and its third cusp
c_{k}
has

\mathcal{O}_{C}(a,b,c_{k})=\mathcal{O}_{C}(a,b,c)
or

•

a
is a cusp of
f_{k}
and the remaining cusps of
f_{k}
link
(a,b)
in the circular order
\mathcal{O}_{C}
.

\hair

2pt

\pinlabel
a
[t] at 229 0

\pinlabel
b
[b] at 229 468

\endlabellist

(
a
)

\labellist
\hair

2pt

\pinlabel
a
[t] at 229 0

\pinlabel
b
[b] at 229 468

\endlabellist

(
b
)

\labellist
\hair

2pt

\pinlabel
a
[t] at 229 0

\pinlabel
b
[b] at 229 468

\endlabellist

(
c
)

\labellist
\hair

2pt

\pinlabel
a
[t] at 229 0

\pinlabel
b
[b] at 229 468

\endlabellist

(
d
)

\labellist
\hair

2pt

\pinlabel
a
[t] at 229 0

\pinlabel
b
[b] at 229 468

\endlabellist

(
e
)

\labellist
\hair

2pt

\pinlabel
a
[t] at 229 0

\pinlabel
b
[b] at 229 468

\endlabellist

(
f
)

Figure 4.9.
The six ways, up to symmetry, to pass from
f_{k}
(below) to
f_{k+1}
(above).

For the base case we take
f_{0}=f^{\prime}
.
For the induction step, given
f_{k}
, if
f_{k}
carries a face of
L_{k+1}
then we set
f_{k+1}=f_{k}
.
Otherwise, we have that
f_{k}
carries a face of
L_{k}
but no face of
L_{k+1}
.
Thus there is a tetrahedron
t_{k}
above
L_{k}
, below
L_{k+1}
, and containing
f_{k}
as a lower face.
We deduce that exactly one of the two upper faces of
t_{k}
has the desired property;
we take this face to be
f_{k+1}
.
See Figure
4.9
.

It follows from the inductive hypothesis that
f_{n}=f
.
We claim that there are two indices
p<q

so that

•

f_{i}=f^{\prime}
for all
i\leq p
,

•

f_{i}=f
for all
q\leq i
, and

•

f_{i}
is an interior face of
C
for all
p<i<q
.

To prove this, we argue by contradiction.
Suppose that all of the faces
f_{k}
meet
e
.
Thus all of the layers
L_{k}
meet
e
.
Since
e
is an interior edge of
L
and
L^{\prime}
, and since no face supports both
L
and
L^{\prime}
, we deduce that
e
separates
C
.
Thus
C
is not face-connected, a contradiction.

Thus there is some largest
k
so that
f_{k}
does not meet the cusp
b
.
Thus
f_{k+1}
is an upper face of the tetrahedron below
e
.
We now consider the collection of faces meeting
e
and on the same side of
e
as
f_{k+1}
.
The transverse orientation of
{\widetilde{B}}
gives these faces an order with
f_{k+1}
being first.
All of the faces
f_{i}
, with
k+1\leq i<q
, are interior faces of
C
so not equal to
f^{\prime}
.
Thus
f
is below
f^{\prime}
in the ordering of faces meeting
e
(on that side).

The sequence of faces
(f_{i})
gives a vertical path
\gamma
(transverse to
B(C)
) from
f^{\prime}
to
f
.
We combine
\gamma
with the path of faces around
e
from
f
up to
f^{\prime}
to obtain a vertical loop.
Since
{\widetilde{M}}
is simply connected, this contradicts
[
53
, Theorem 3.2]
.

Corollary 4.0.15
.

Suppose that
C
is a continent in
{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}
.
Then
C
is a closed three-ball, minus finitely many points on its boundary corresponding to the cusps of
\Delta_{C}
.
Furthermore, the dihedral angle for any edge
e
in
\partial C
is either zero or
\pi
as
e
is or is not coastal.∎

We now discuss how to obtain new continents from old.

Lemma 4.0.16
.

Suppose that
C
is a continent in
{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}
.
Let
L
and
L^{\prime}
be the upper and lower landscapes of
C
, respectively.
Suppose that
e^{\prime}
is a (coastal) sink of
\tau^{L}
.
Suppose that
t
is a tetrahedron of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}
having
e^{\prime}
as its lower edge.
Then
t
is not in
C
, and
C\cup t
is a continent.
A similar statement holds, replacing lower with upper and
\tau^{L}
with
\tau_{L^{\prime}}
.

Proof 4.0.17
.

Let
(L_{i})_{i=0}^{n}
be the given layering of
C
.
Note that
L=L_{n}
and
L^{\prime}=L_{0}
.
Let
f
and
g
be the upper faces of
t
;
let
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
be the lower faces of
t
.
If
e^{\prime}
is a sink of
L
then
L
meets both of
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
.
If
e^{\prime}
is a coastal sink of
L
, then
L
meets only one, say
f^{\prime}
.
Since
f^{\prime}\subset\partial C
, we deduce that
t
is not contained in
C
.

Let
D=C\cup t
;
note that
D
is face-connected.

There are now two cases, as
e^{\prime}
is a sink or a coastal sink.

Case 1
.

Suppose that
e^{\prime}
is a coastal sink.
Then
e^{\prime}
is a coastal edge of each layer
L_{i}
.
Note that
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
are on opposite sides of
e^{\prime}
.
However,
f^{\prime}
carries
L_{n}
and
g^{\prime}
does not.
Thus
K_{i}=L_{i}\cup g^{\prime}
is a landscape for
i\leq n
.
We also define
K_{n+1}=(L_{n}-f^{\prime})\cup f\cup g
.
Note that
K_{n+1}
is obtained from
K_{n}
by flipping across
t
.
Thus
K_{n+1}
is a landscape and
(K_{i})
is a layering of
D
.

Case 2
.

Suppose that
e^{\prime}
is a sink.
Define
K_{i}=L_{i}
for
i\leq n
.
We also define
K_{n+1}=(L_{n}-(f^{\prime}\cup g^{\prime}))\cup f\cup g
.
Then
(K_{i})
is a layering of
D
.

4.10.
Continental exhaustions

Definition 4.0.18
.

A
continental exhaustion
of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}
is a sequence of continents
(C_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
so that

•

C_{n}\subset C_{n+1}
and

•

{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}=\bigcup C_{n}
.

Lemma 4.0.19
.

Any continental exhaustion can be refined so that
C_{n+1}
is obtained from
C_{n}
by adding exactly one tetrahedron.

Proof 4.0.20
.

Suppose that
C\subsetneq C^{\prime}
are continents.
Let
L
be the upper landscape of
C
.
Suppose (as the other case is similar) that there is a tetrahedron
t
of
C^{\prime}-C
attached to
C
along a face
f
of
L
.
Let
R
be the maximal upper river in
L
starting at
f
.
Let
(f_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
be the faces of
R
, with
f_{i}
flowing into
f_{i+1}
.
Let
(e_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
be the edges of
R
where
f_{i}
flows into
e_{i}
.
By Lemma
4.0.8
, the final edge
e_{n}
is a (coastal) sink for
\tau^{L}
.

We now induct on
n
, the length of
R
.
In the base case
n=1
and
e_{1}
is a sink.
We set
t_{1}=t
and note that
e_{1}
is the lower edge of
t_{1}
.
So, by Lemma
4.0.16
, the union
C\cup t
is a continent.

In the induction step we have a tetrahedron
t_{k}
of
C^{\prime}
which is attached to
C
along
f_{k}
.
Also,
e_{k}
is the lower edge of
t_{k}
.
If
e_{k}
is a sink then we proceed as in the base case.
Suppose instead that
e_{k}
is a fall.
Since
C^{\prime}
is a continent, and applying Corollary
4.0.15
, the dihedral angle at
e_{k}
is not
2\pi
.
Thus gives a tetrahedron
t_{k+1}
of
C^{\prime}
, attached to
C
along
f_{k+1}
, with lower edge
e_{k+1}
.

Applying a result of Brown
[
6
]
we have the following.

Lemma 4.0.21
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{T}
is a taut ideal triangulation and that
{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}
admits a continental exhaustion.
Then the realisation
|{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}|
is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
. ∎

Proposition 4.0.22
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{T}
is a transverse taut ideal triangulation.
Suppose that
{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}
admits a continental exhaustion.
Then
{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}
is layered.

Proof 4.0.23
.

Suppose that
{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}
has a continental exhaustion
(C_{n})
.
By Lemma
4.0.19
we may assume that
C_{n}
is a union of exactly
n
tetrahedra.

The continents
C_{n}
are layered by definition.
However, these layerings need not be compatible with each other.
We re-choose the layerings as follows.

Our induction hypothesis is that
C_{n}
is layered by
\mathcal{K}_{n}=(K_{i}^{n})_{i=p(n)}^{q(n)}
,
a sequence of
n+1
landscapes.
That is,

•

K^{n}_{p(n)}
is the lower landscape for
C_{n}
,

•

K^{n}_{q(n)}
is the upper landscape for
C_{n}
, and

•

K^{n}_{i}
and
K^{n}_{i+1}
cobound a single tetrahedron of
C_{n}
.

Thus
q(n)-p(n)=n
.
We further assume that for any
m<n
and for any
i
between
p(m)
and
q(m)
we have

•

K^{m}_{i}\subset K^{n}_{i}
.

Suppose now that
C_{n+1}
is obtained from
C_{n}
by attaching the tetrahedron
t
.
As the other case is similar we assume that
t
is attached to the upper landscape of
C_{n}
.
There are now two cases:
either
C_{n+1}
has the same cusps as
C_{n}
or
C_{n+1}
has one more cusp than
C_{n}
.

Suppose that
C_{n+1}
and
C_{n}
have the same cusps.
Recalling that
t
is attached above
C_{n}
,
we set
p(n+1)=p(n)
and
q(n+1)=q(n)+1
, we set
K^{n+1}_{q(n+1)}
to be the upper landscape of
C_{n+1}
, and we set
K^{n+1}_{i}=K^{n}_{i}
for all
i
between
p(n)
and
q(n)
.

Suppose that
C_{n+1}
has one more cusp than
C_{n}
.
Then there is a unique lower face
f
, of
t
, which meets the new cusp.
Note that
f
is not contained in
C_{n}
and meets exactly one coastal edge of
C_{n}
.
For
i
between
p(n)
and
q(n)
we take
K^{n+1}_{i}=K^{n}_{i}\cup f
and note that
K^{n+1}_{i}
lies in, and contains the coastal edges of,
C_{n+1}
.
Note that
t
is layered onto
K^{n+1}_{q(n)}
.
So set
p(n+1)=p(n)
, set
q(n+1)=q(n)+1
, and define
K^{n+1}_{q(n+1)}
to be the upper landscape for
C_{n+1}
.

Thus
\mathcal{K}_{n+1}=(K^{n+1}_{i})_{i=p(n+1)}^{q(n+1)}
is the desired layering of
C_{n+1}
.
Note that, in both cases, we have
K^{n}_{i}\subset K^{n+1}_{i}
.

Finally, for any
i\in\mathbb{Z}
we define
K_{i}=\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}}K^{n}_{i}
.
Note that
K_{i}
has no boundary edges and that
K_{i+1}
is obtained from
K_{i}
by a single in-fill.
Thus
(K_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
is the desired layering of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}}
.

Remark 4.0.24
.

The converse of Proposition
4.0.22
also holds;
the proof is similar to, but more difficult than, that of Lemma
2.0.11
.
The converse is not needed for Theorem
5.2
, so we omit it.

Remark 4.0.25
.

Note that Theorem
3.6
and Lemma
2.0.14
imply that the (universal covers of the) exotic triangulations of
[
17
, Proposition 6.8]
do not have layerings.
Thus by Proposition
4.0.22
they do not have continental exhaustions.

Chapter 5
Veering triangulations

We now give the definition of
veering
, following
[
1
,
28
]
.
Recall that the three-manifold
M
is equipped with an orientation.

Definition 5.0.1
.

A
veering triangulation

\mathcal{V}
is a taut ideal triangulation
\mathcal{T}
equipped with an edge colouring.
Each edge is coloured red or blue as follows.
Suppose that
t
is a model taut tetrahedron and
f\subset t
is a face.
Suppose
e
,
e^{\prime}
, and
e^{\prime\prime}
are the edges of
f
, ordered anticlockwise as viewed from the outside of
t
,
with
e
having dihedral angle
\pi
inside of
t
.
Then
e^{\prime}
is red and
e^{\prime\prime}
is blue.
See Figure
5.1
for a model veering tetrahedron.

If
\mathcal{V}
is a veering triangulation whose taut angle structure is transverse then we will say that
\mathcal{V}
is a
transverse veering structure
.

\labellist
\hair

2pt

\pinlabel
0 [tr] at 29 29

\pinlabel
0 [tl] at 85 29

\pinlabel
0 [br] at 29 85

\pinlabel
0 [bl] at 85 85

\pinlabel
\pi
[t] at 80 55

\pinlabel
\pi
[r] at 55 80

\endlabellist

Figure 5.1.
A model veering tetrahedron.
The dotted edges are red;
the dashed edges are blue.
The two edges with angle
\pi
may have either colour.
See Figure
5.5a
for the four possible veering tetrahedra.

We can now state the main result of this section.

Theorem 5.0.1
.

Suppose that
M
is an oriented three-manifold equipped with a transverse veering triangulation
\mathcal{V}
.
Then there is a unique compatible circular order
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{V}}
on the cusps of
{\widetilde{M}}
.
Furthermore,
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{V}}
is dense and
\pi_{1}(M)
–invariant.

Given the work we have already done, to prove Theorem
5.2
it suffices to prove the following.

Proposition 5.0.2
.

Suppose that
M
is a three-manifold equipped with a transverse veering triangulation
\mathcal{V}
.
Then
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
admits a continental exhaustion.

Proposition
5.0.2
and Proposition
4.0.22
give the following.

Corollary 5.0.3
.

Suppose that
M
is a three-manifold equipped with a transverse veering triangulation
\mathcal{V}
.
Then
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
admits a layering. ∎

Proof 5.0.4

(Proof of Theorem
5.2
)
.

Corollary
5.0.3
tells us that
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
admits a layering.
Lemma
2.0.14
now gives the desired unique, compatible, dense,
\pi_{1}(M)
–invariant circular order.

5.3.
Combinatorics of veering triangulations

We begin by setting down some of the combinatorics of veering triangulations.
Suppose that
M
is a three-manifold equipped with a transverse veering triangulation
\mathcal{V}
.
Suppose that
f
is a face of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
.
So
f
has at least one blue edge and at least one red edge.
Ordering the cusps of
f
as in Section
2.8
– that is, looking from above, in anticlockwise order – there is a unique cusp
u(f)
of
f
where the colours switch from blue to red.
Consulting Figure
5.1
we see that the branches of the upper track
\tau^{f}
flow away from
u(f)
.
(Swapping colours gives a corresponding statement for
\tau_{f}
.)
This implies that, in the presence of a veering structure, our upper track is the same as the train track used by Agol
[
1
, Main Construction]
to define veering triangulations of mapping tori.
See Figure
5.4
.

(
a
)
Upper tracks.

(
b
)
Lower tracks.

Figure 5.4.
The two possible faces in a veering triangulation, as viewed from above. We draw the upper track in green, and the lower track in purple.
Our colour scheme is chosen so that the following holds.
Suppose that
L
is a landscape.
In
L
we walk anticlockwise about a cusp (as viewed from above).
As we do so we pass, cyclically, a sequence of red edges, then an upper track cusp (green), then a sequence of blue edges, and then a lower track cusp (purple).
This follows the standard colour wheel ordering.

Definition 5.0.5
.

Suppose that
t
is a model veering tetrahedron.

•

If
t
has three red and three blue edges then we call
t
a
toggle tetrahedron
.

•

If
t
has more red than blue edges (more blue than red edges) then we call
t
a red (blue)
fan tetrahedron
.

The four possible model veering tetrahedra are shown in Figure
5.5
;
we also show all face gluings that respect the veering structure.
In Figure
5.6
we show one possibility for the tetrahedra on the two sides of an edge.

(
a
)
Upper tracks.

(
b
)
Lower tracks.

Figure 5.5.
In each diagram: moving anticlockwise from the top we have a toggle tetrahedron (blue on top), a blue fan tetrahedron, the other toggle tetrahedron (red on top), and a red fan tetrahedron. An arrow points from one tetrahedron to another if the second tetrahedron can be glued on top of the first. In Figure
5.5a
(Figure
5.5b
) we draw in each face the corresponding upper (lower) track; the tracks on the lower faces are more faintly drawn.
