\hair

2pt

\pinlabel
e
[tr] at 165 165

\endlabellist

Figure 5.6.
One possible neighbourhood of a blue edge,
e
.
To the upper right of
e
there is a single blue fan tetrahedron.
To its lower left there are two toggle tetrahedra, one of each type, and a stack of two red fan tetrahedra.
We have not drawn the tetrahedron above
e
(in front of the page) or the one below
e
(behind the page).
See also
[
1
, Figure 12]
.

Remark 5.0.6
.

In other literature the toggle tetrahedra are called
hinge tetrahedra
;
see
[
22
, page 1247]
or
[
17
, page 211]
.

We record, in Figure
5.7
, the upper tracks in all possible two-triangle veering landscapes.
Note that watersheds and sinks fit together to give the boundaries of veering tetrahedra.

(
a
)
A left watershed.

(
b
)
A right watershed.

(
c
)
A left fall.

(
d
)
A right fall.

(
e
)
A sink.

Figure 5.7.
Upper tracks on all possible two-triangle veering landscapes.
Black edges can be either red or blue.

Definition 5.0.7
.

Suppose that
R
is an upper river in
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
.
The
riverbank edges
of
R
are the boundary edges of
R
, other than the mouth.

From Figures
5.7c
and
5.7d

we deduce that the edges on the left riverbank of
R
are blue, while those on the right are red.

Veering triangulations are far more rigid than taut ideal triangulations.
In particular, the veering hypothesis strongly constrains the combinatorics about an edge, as follows.

Lemma 5.0.8
.

Suppose that
M
is a three-manifold equipped with a transverse veering triangulation
\mathcal{V}
.
Suppose that
e
is an oriented blue edge in
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
. Then the following hold.

(1)

There is exactly one tetrahedron above, and exactly one below,
e
.

(2)

There is at least one tetrahedron to the right of
e
, and similarly to its left.

(3)

If there is exactly one tetrahedron to the right of
e
, then it is a blue fan tetrahedron.
The same holds to the left of
e
.

(4)

If there are at least two tetrahedra to the right of
e
, then the resulting stack of tetrahedra contains, from bottom to top, a red-on-top toggle, some number of red fans, and finally a blue-on-top toggle.
The same holds to the left of
e
.

(5)

There are exactly four faces meeting
e
that have more blue edges than red.

Similar statements hold when
e
is red instead of blue. ∎

5.8.
Convex continents

Here we give two simple ways to enlarge a given continent.

Lemma 5.0.9
.

Suppose that
M
is a three-manifold equipped with a transverse veering triangulation
\mathcal{V}
.
Suppose that
C\subset{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
is a continent.
Suppose that the lower track on the bottom of
C
has no sinks.
Then this again holds after a single coastal landfill on top of
C
.
The corresponding statement holds after switching upper and lower as well as top and bottom.

Proof 5.0.10
.

Suppose that
t
is the given coastal landfill tetrahedron above
C
.
Suppose
e
is the lower
\pi
–edge of
t
.
Let
f
be the free lower face of
t
.
Suppose that
t^{\prime}
is the tetrahedron having
e
as its upper
\pi
–edge.
From Lemma
5.0.8
(
2
) we deduce that
t^{\prime}
cannot be glued to
t
along
f
.
Thus the lower track in the lower boundary of
C\cup t
again has no sinks.

Definition 5.0.11
.

Suppose that
C\subset{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
is a continent with upper and lower landscapes
L
and
L^{\prime}
.
We say that
C
is
convex
if neither
\tau^{L}
nor
\tau_{L^{\prime}}
have sinks.

For example, a continent containing only one tetrahedron is convex.

Lemma 5.0.12
.

Suppose that
M
is a three-manifold equipped with a transverse veering triangulation
\mathcal{V}
.
Suppose that
C\subset{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
is a continent.
Then there exists a convex continent
C^{\prime}
which contains
C
and has the same set of cusps as
C
.

In fact,
C^{\prime}
is unique;
this follows from Theorem
10.18
and
[
54
, Proposition 6.46]
.

Proof 5.0.13

(Proof of Lemma
5.0.12
)
.

Let
L
and
L^{\prime}
be the upper and lower landscapes of
C
.
Note that an in-fill above
C
leaves
L^{\prime}
unchanged.
Thus we can argue separately for the top and bottom of
C
.

Set
C_{0}=C
.
Now suppose that we have built from
C_{0}
a continent
C_{k}
via a finite sequence of in-fills.
Let
L_{k}
be the upper landscape of
C_{k}
;
let
\tau^{k}
be the upper track for
L_{k}
.
If
\tau^{k}
has no sinks we are done;
otherwise we build
C_{k+1}
from
C_{k}
by a single in-fill above.

Suppose that
t_{k}=t
is the given in-fill tetrahedron above
L_{k}
.
By Lemma
4.0.2
, the landscape
L_{k}
is a triangulated disk.
Let
\tau^{k}
be the upper track for
L_{k}
;
thus
\tau^{k}
is a tree, with all leaves on coastal edges.
The track
\tau^{k+1}
is obtained from
\tau^{k}
by doing a single split.
See Figure
5.5a
.
This gives a one-to-one correspondence between the track-cusps of
\tau^{k}
and those of
\tau^{k+1}
.

A track-cusp
s
of
\tau^{k}
meets some edge
e
of
L_{k}
;
the flows behind
s
give
e
a co-orientation in
L_{k}
.
The orientation divides the coastal edges into two collections:
those in front of
e
and those behind.
When we attach
t_{k}
, the track-cusp
s
moves forward to a new track-cusp
s^{\prime}
and a new edge
e^{\prime}
, both in
L_{k+1}
.
Again, see Figure
5.5a
.
Recall that
L_{k+1}
and
L_{k}
have the same coastal edges.
Now, at least one coastal edge goes from being in front of
e
to being behind
e^{\prime}
, and none move forward.
Any such sequence
s,s^{\prime},s^{\prime\prime},\ldots
of track-cusps eventually reaches the coast or otherwise halts.

Let
n=|L_{0}|
be the number of triangles in
L_{0}
, and thus in
L_{k}
.
Thus any sequence of track-cusps
s,s^{\prime},s^{\prime\prime},\ldots
as above has length at most
n-1
.
Also, the number of track-cusps of
\tau^{0}
equals
n
.
Thus after adding at most
n(n-1)/2
in-fill tetrahedra we arrive at an upper track
\tau^{k}
without sinks.

5.9.
Complexity and channelisation

Our aim is to build a continental exhaustion for the universal cover of a veering triangulation.
Given a continent
C
and a tetrahedron
t
, we must enlarge
C
to include
t
.
For this we wish to use Lemma
4.0.16
.
However this only permits us to add tetrahedra in certain locations;
by Lemma
4.0.8
these are found by following rivers downstream to (coastal) sinks.
Note that Theorem
3.6
shows that in the absence of the veering hypothesis,
this procedure may never arrive at
t
.

To show that the veering hypothesis suffices,
we require two more subtle definitions to control the rivers that appear in the argument.

Suppose that
R
is an upper river.
We orient
R
in the direction of the flows.
Let
e_{i}
be the
i^{\rm th}
fall, counting from the source.
We define
h_{i}
to be the
height
of the fall
e_{i}
;
that is,
h_{i}
is the degree of
e_{i}
in
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
minus the degree of
e_{i}
below the river
R
.
Recall that
\ell(R)
, the length of
R
, is the number of triangles in
R
.

Definition 5.0.14
.

The
complexity
of a river
R
is the list

c(R)=(\ell(R),h_{1},h_{2},\ldots,h_{\ell-1})

We order complexities lexicographically.

\hair

2pt

\pinlabel
f
[r] at 367 1197

\pinlabel
(0)
at 310 1145

\pinlabel
(1)
at 15 940

\pinlabel
(1^{\prime})
at 1190 940

\pinlabel
(2^{\prime})
at 15 760

\pinlabel
(2)
at 1190 760

\pinlabel
(3^{\prime})
at 15 577

\pinlabel
(3)
at 1190 577

\pinlabel
(8)
at 15 207

\pinlabel
(8^{\prime})
at 1190 207

\pinlabel
(A) at 15 27

\pinlabel
(B) at 1190 27

\endlabellist

Figure 5.10.
Some possible steps in a channelisation process.
The figures
(0)
,
(1)
,
\ldots
,
(8)
, (A), and (B) all show rivers:

(0)
is the initial river and the others arise from landfilling.
The figures
(1^{\prime})
,
(2^{\prime})
,
\ldots
,
(8^{\prime})
are forked rivers.
The primed indices count the total number of triangles in the two distributaries after the sink.
The riverbanks are shaded brown.
We place a subfigure on the left or right according to the colour of the upper edge of the most recently added tetrahedron.

Definition 5.0.15
.

Suppose
C
is a convex continent; thus all rivers in
\partial C
flow to the coast.
Suppose that
f
is a face of the upper landscape of
C
.
Let
R
be the maximal river with
f
as its source.
Let
f^{\prime}
be the last triangle of
R
and let
e^{\prime}
be the mouth of
R
.
See Figure
5.10
(0) for one possible example.
Let
t^{\prime}
be the tetrahedron above
f^{\prime}
.
Note that
e^{\prime}
is the lower edge of
t^{\prime}
.

We now landfill
t^{\prime}
onto
C
.
By Lemma
5.0.9
, the union
C\cup t^{\prime}
is again a continent, with no new sinks in its lower boundary.
We now appeal to Lemma
5.0.12
: after in-filling the top of
C\cup t^{\prime}
a finite number of times we obtain a convex continent
C^{\prime}
.
We say that
C^{\prime}
is a
channelisation
of
(C,f)
.
We make similar definitions for the lower landscape of
C
.

In general, a single channelisation process will not cover the face
f
.
The heart of the proof of Proposition
5.0.2
is in showing that a finite number of channelisations suffices to cover
f
.

Proof 5.0.16

(Proof of Proposition
5.0.2
)
.

We must build a continental exhaustion. We begin by choosing an enumeration
(t_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
of the tetrahedra of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
.
Our first continent is
C_{0}=t_{0}
; note that
C_{0}
is convex. Now suppose that we have obtained a convex continent
C_{n}
. Let
t_{m(n)}
be the smallest tetrahedron in our enumeration which is not contained in
C_{n}
and which meets
C_{n}
along a face
f
. It will suffice to find a convex continent
C_{n+1}
containing
C_{n}
and
t_{m(n)}
. (For suppose that
t^{\prime}
is any tetrahedron. Let
(t_{p(k)})_{k=0}^{K}
be a sequence of tetrahedra so that
t_{p(0)}=t_{0}
, so that
t_{p(K)}=t^{\prime}
, and the tetrahedra
t_{p(k)}
and
t_{p(k+1)}
share a face. Induction proves that
t_{p(k)}
belongs to
C_{p(k)}
.)

Breaking the symmetry of the situation, we assume that
t_{m(n)}
is above
C_{n}
.
Thus
f
is a face of the upper landscape of
C_{n}
.
Set
C_{n}^{0}=C_{n}
.
Suppose that we have constructed
C_{n}^{k}
, convex.
If
t_{m(n)}
is contained in
C_{n}^{k}
then we set
C_{n+1}=C_{n}^{k}
and we are done.
If not, then
f
is still a face of the upper landscape of
C_{n}^{k}
.
Then we define
C_{n}^{k+1}
to be the result of channelising the pair
(C_{n}^{k},f)
.
Note that
C_{n}^{k+1}
is again convex.
Let
R^{k}
be the maximal river in the upper landscape of
C_{n}^{k}
with
f
as its source.

Lemma 5.0.17
.

Suppose that
f
is a face of the upper landscapes of both
C_{n}^{k}
and
C_{n}^{k+1}
.
Then
c(R^{k+1})
is strictly smaller than
c(R^{k})
.

Note that Lemma
5.0.17
completes the proof of Proposition
5.0.2
;
since complexities are well-ordered, eventually
f
is not a face of some upper landscape.
At that stage
t_{m(n)}
is contained in the continent, as desired.

Before giving the proof of Lemma
5.0.17
we require an expanded notion of a river. We give this in the next definition. We also give an extended example to illustrate how the proof works.

Definition 5.0.18
.

Suppose that
C
is a continent with upper landscape
L
.
Let
f
be a face of
L
.
We define the
forked river

R(C,f)
as follows.

Let
R^{f}
be the maximal upper river in
L
with source
f
.
If
R^{f}
ends at the coast then we take
R(C,f)=R^{f}
;
in this case the forked river is simply a river.
Suppose instead that
R^{f}
ends at a sink
e
for
\tau^{L}
.
Let
f_{0}
be the triangle in
L-R^{f}
adjacent to
e
.
Let
e_{0}
and
e_{1}
be the edges of
f_{0}-e
.
Let
R^{e_{0}}
and
R^{e_{1}}
be the maximal rivers starting at
e_{0}
and
e_{1}
, respectively, and flowing away from
f_{0}
.
These are the two
distributaries
flowing from
f_{0}
; they may contain zero triangles.
Finally, the desired forked river is the landscape

R(C,f)=R^{f}\cup f_{0}\cup R^{e_{0}}\cup R^{e_{1}}

If
R^{e_{i}}
contains no triangles then we take the mouth of
R^{e_{i}}
to be
e_{i}
.

Suppose that
R
is a forked river.
The
length
of
R
, denoted
\ell(R)
, is the number of triangles it contains.
If
R
contains a sink, then we say that the
mouths
of
R
are the mouths of its distributaries.
The
riverbank edges
of
R
are the boundary edges of
R
other than the mouth(s).

For examples of forked rivers, with distributaries of various sizes (including zero), see subfigures
(1^{\prime})
,
(2^{\prime})
,
(3^{\prime})
, and
(8^{\prime})
of Figure
5.10
.

Example 5.0.19
.

We now discuss channelisation, illustrated in Figure
5.10
, in more detail.
Subfigure
5.10
(0) shows a possible river
R^{k}
, with source face
f
.
Suppose that
t^{\prime}
is the coastal landfill tetrahedron.
Note that attaching
t^{\prime}
fills the mouth of
R^{k}
and adds one new cusp and two new coastal edges.
After the landfill there are two possibilities according to the colour, blue or red, of the top edge of
t^{\prime}
.
These are shown immediately below
(0)
on the left and right in subfigures
(1)
and
(1^{\prime})
, respectively.

In
(1)
we see a river
R^{k+1}
.
This is isomorphic (as a landscape) to
R^{k}
; however the last fall of
R^{k+1}
is less high.
Thus, following Definition
5.0.14
, we have
c(R^{k+1})<c(R^{k})
.
In the remainder of the
(k+1)^{\rm st}
channelisation procedure we in-fill all sinks on the top of
C_{n}^{k}\cup t^{\prime}
.
There are none in
R^{k+1}
.
There are none outside of the subfigure (1) because
C_{n}^{k}
was convex.
Thus the only possible in-fill is at the track-cusp pointing out of the top of the subfigure (1).
This may lead to further sinks, which we in-fill in their turn.
However, these in-fills do not alter
R^{k+1}
because the only place to fill
R^{k+1}
is at the coast.

In
(1^{\prime})
we have a forked river
R^{k}_{1}
as in Definition
5.0.18
.
Note that
R^{k}_{1}
has one more coastal edge than the original river
R^{k}
but has the
same
riverbanks.
Thus the only in-fill on the top (or bottom) of the current continent is the unique sink in
R^{k}_{1}
.
In
(1^{\prime})
the distributary on the left riverbank has length one while the distributary to the right has length zero.

When we in-fill the sink of
R^{k}_{1}
, there are two possibilities, shown in
(2^{\prime})
and
(2)
, as the top edge of the landfill tetrahedron is blue or red.
In example
(2)
we obtain a river
R^{k+1}
which has been routed along the distributary (to the left) in
R^{k}_{1}
.
Again we claim that
c(R^{k+1})<c(R^{k})
.
Firstly, in-fills disjoint from
R^{k+1}
do not alter it.
Also, while
R^{k}
and
R^{k+1}
have the same length, the height of the second-to-last fall of
R^{k+1}
is smaller than that of
R^{k}
.

In example
(2^{\prime})
we obtain a forked river
R^{k}_{2}
.
Note that the sink is further upstream while the total length of the distributaries has increased by one.
Again the only in-fill on the boundary of the continent is the sink of
R^{k}_{2}
.
At that point we will either obtain
(3)
, a river with lower complexity, or
(3^{\prime})
, a forked river where the sink has moved upstream and one of the distributaries is one triangle larger.

Continuing in this way, if we do not make a river, then eventually the two distributaries contain all of the riverbank edges. This is shown in the forked river of subfigure
(8^{\prime})
. Here the sink has reached the source and again there is a unique in-fill. Depending on the colour of the upper edge of this in-fill, we arrive at one of the two rivers shown in subfigures (A) or (B). In this case our continent now contains
t
. The channelisation process may continue beyond (A) or (B), but by Lemma
5.0.12
it eventually terminates and gives a convex continent.

We now consider the general case.

Proof 5.0.20

(Proof of Lemma
5.0.17
)
.

Recall that
f
is the upper face of
C_{n}^{0}=C_{n}
which we must layer onto.
Recall that
C^{k+1}_{n}
is the result of channelisation of the pair
(C^{k}_{n},f)
.
By hypothesis, the face
f
is contained in the upper landscapes of both
C^{k}_{n}
and
C^{k+1}_{n}
.
Thus
R^{k}
and
R^{k+1}
are both defined.

Set
C^{k}_{n,0}=C^{k}_{n}
and let
C^{k}_{n,i}
be the continent obtained after attaching the first
i
landfills of this
(k+1)^{\rm st}
channelisation.
Only the first landfill is coastal; the rest are in-fill.
We define
R^{k}_{i}
to be the forked river for the pair
(C^{k}_{n,i},f)
.

Claim 2
.

(1)

The mouth(s) of
R^{k}_{i}
are coastal edges.

(2)

Suppose that
R^{k}_{i}
contains a sink. Then
\ell(R^{k}_{i})=\ell(R^{k})+1
. Also, the riverbanks of
R^{k}_{i}
are the same as those of
R^{k}
. Also,
R^{k}\cap R^{k}_{i}
is the sub-river of
R^{k}
with source
f
that ends at the sink of
R^{k}_{i}
.

(3)

Suppose
i>0
and
R^{k}_{i}
does not contain a sink. Then
\ell(R^{k}_{i})\leq\ell(R^{k})
and
R^{k}_{i}=R^{k+1}
.

Claim
2
proves Lemma
5.0.17
, as follows. By (
3
) the length of
R^{k+1}
is no greater than that of
R^{k}
. If it is shorter we are done; if it is the same length then by (
2
) the in-fill giving
R^{k+1}
has reduced the height of one fall without altering the heights further upstream.

Proof 5.0.21

(Proof of Claim
2
)
.

If
i=0
then we only need check (
1
) for
R^{k}_{0}=R^{k}
. This holds because
C^{k}_{n}
is convex. We now induct on
i
, starting with the base case of
i=1
.

So suppose that
i=1
.
Let
f^{\prime}
be the coastal triangle of
R^{k}_{0}
.
Let
t^{\prime}
be the tetrahedron immediately above
f^{\prime}
.
Thus
C^{k}_{n,1}=C^{k}_{n,0}\cup t^{\prime}
.
If
f^{\prime}=f
then
f
is not a face of the upper landscape of
C^{k+1}_{n}
, contrary to hypothesis.
Thus
f^{\prime}\neq f
.
Thus there is an edge
e^{\prime}
of
f^{\prime}
that is neither on the riverbank nor on the coast.
There are four cases to check as the riverbank edge of
f^{\prime}
is either blue or red and
as the upper edge of
t^{\prime}
is either blue or red.
These are very similar to the general case discussed immediately below, so we omit them.
(See Figure
5.11
, but delete the triangle attached to
f^{\prime}
in the first row of figures.)

Suppose now that
i>1
.
If
R^{k}_{i}
contains no sink then no future infill in the
k^{\rm th}
channelisation will alter
R^{k}_{i}
.
That is,
R^{k+1}=R^{k}_{i+1}=R^{k}_{i}
and there is nothing to prove.
Suppose instead that
R^{k}_{i}
contains a sink.
We define
f^{\prime}
to be the triangle of
R^{k}_{i}
immediately before the sink.
Let
t^{\prime}
be the tetrahedron immediately above
f^{\prime}
.
As before, if
f^{\prime}=f
then
f
does not appear in the upper landscape of the next continent, contrary to hypothesis.
So
f^{\prime}\neq f
.
Let
e^{\prime}
be the edge of
f^{\prime}
that is neither on the riverbank nor equal to the sink.
There are again four possibilities according to the colours of the riverbank edge of
f^{\prime}
and the upper edge of
t^{\prime}
.
See Figure
5.11
.

\labellist
\hair

2pt

\pinlabel
(1) [r] at 115 175

\pinlabel
(1^{\prime})
at 250 180

\pinlabel
f^{\prime}
at 132 310

\pinlabel
e^{\prime}
[r] at 106 280

\endlabellist

(
a
)
Landfilling along the blue riverbank.

\labellist
\hair

2pt

\pinlabel
(2^{\prime})
[r] at 115 170

\pinlabel
(2) at 255 170

\pinlabel
f^{\prime}
at 132 250

\pinlabel
e^{\prime}
[r] at 106 280

\endlabellist

(
b
)
Landfilling along the red riverbank.

Figure 5.11.
The two possible results of attaching
t^{\prime}
to
f^{\prime}
, depending on the colour of its uppermost edge.
The riverbank is shown shaded in brown.
The sink, covered by the landfill tetrahedron, can be of either colour.
Since the results are the same in each case, we here colour that edge black.

Suppose that
f^{\prime}
has an edge on the blue riverbank and the upper edge of
t^{\prime}
is blue.
Then, as shown in Figure
5.11a
(1), after attaching
t^{\prime}
we have that
R^{k}_{i+1}
is a river.
We deduce that the mouth of
R^{k}_{i+1}
is the mouth of the distributary meeting the red riverbank of
R^{k}_{i}
, which is coastal by induction.
This proves (
1
) in this case.
Since
R^{k}_{i+1}
is a river, (
2
) holds vacuously.
Note that exactly one of the two upper faces of
t^{\prime}
are contained in
R^{k}_{i+1}
.
Thus
R^{k}_{i+1}
is at least one triangle smaller than
R^{k}_{i}
.
Applying (
2
) to
R^{k}_{i}
we deduce that
\ell(R^{k}_{i+1})\leq\ell(R^{k})
.
This proves (
3
) in this case.

Suppose that
f^{\prime}
has an edge on the blue riverbank and the upper edge of
t^{\prime}
is red.
Then, as shown in Figure
5.11a
(1), after attaching
t^{\prime}
we have that
R^{k}_{i+1}
is again a forked river.
Both lower faces of
t^{\prime}
belonged to
R^{k}_{i}
and both upper faces of
t^{\prime}
belong to
R^{k}_{i+1}
.
Thus these forked rivers have the same number of triangles.
Likewise they have the same boundaries and so have the same riverbanks and mouths.
The distributary meeting the blue riverbank has grown by one triangle and the sink of
R^{k}_{i+1}
is one triangle further upstream.
This proves the induction hypotheses in this case.

The remaining two cases (where
f^{\prime}
has a red riverbank edge) are the same, switching sides and colours correctly.

This completes the proof of Claim
2
.

This completes the proof of Lemma
5.0.17
.

This completes the proof of Proposition
5.0.2
.

5.12.
No parallel edges

With Corollary
5.0.3
in hand we may now give a few global constraints on the combinatorics of veering triangulations.

Lemma 5.0.22
.

Suppose that
M
is a three-manifold equipped with a veering triangulation
\mathcal{V}
.
Then any pair of cusps of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
are connected by at most one edge.

Unlike Theorem
2.6
, the assumption of tautness alone does not suffice to prove Lemma
5.0.22
.

Proof 5.0.23

(Proof of Lemma
5.0.22
)
.

Fix cusps
c
and
d
of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
.
Suppose that
e
is an edge of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
connecting them.
Corollary
5.0.3
gives a layering
\mathcal{K}=(K_{i})
of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
.
Let
\tau^{i}
and
\tau_{i}
be the upper and lower tracks, respectively, of
K_{i}
.
For all
i
we define
P_{i}\subset K_{i}
as follows.
If there is an edge
e_{i}
in
K_{i}
connecting
c
to
d
then
P_{i}=e_{i}
.
If there is no such edge then we apply Lemma
4.0.2
and let
P_{i}
be the strip of triangles connecting
c
and
d
.

Let
t
be the tetrahedron immediately below
e
.
Let
K_{p}
be the lowest layer of
\mathcal{K}
containing the upper faces of
t
.
We deduce that
\tau^{p}
has a small switch at
e
.
Recall that, as shown in Figure
5.5a
, the track
\tau^{i-1}
is obtained from
\tau^{i}
by folding at exactly one small switch.
Inducting downwards, we have that for all
i<p

•

P_{i}
is a strip of at least two triangles,

•

the track-cusps of
\tau^{P_{i}}
, in the first and last triangles of
P_{i}
, point away from
c
and
d
, respectively, and

•

these track-cusps are connected by a train interval in
\tau^{P_{i}}
.

Thus there is no edge connecting
c
and
d
below
K_{p}
.

Now suppose that
t^{\prime}
is the tetrahedron immediately above
e
.
Let
K_{q}
be the highest layer of
\mathcal{K}
containing the lower faces of
t^{\prime}
.
We now repeat the previous argument replacing
K_{p}
by
K_{q}
and upper train tracks with lower.

We deduce that, for
i<p
and for
i>q
, the layer
K_{i}
does not have an edge connecting
c
and
d
.
Thus
p\leq q
.
From Lemma
5.0.8
we deduce that
p
is in fact strictly less than
q
.
Finally note that
e
lies in
K_{j}
if
p\leq j\leq q
.
This is because
e
can only be removed from
K_{j}
by flipping across
t
or
t^{\prime}
.
Since
K_{j}
is a copy of the Farey tessellation (Lemma
4.0.2
) it contains no bigons.
That is,
e
is the only edge in
K_{j}
connecting
c
to
d
.

Remark 5.0.24
.

When the triangulation
\mathcal{V}
is finite there is an alternative argument using strict angle structures
[
28
,
17
]
and combinatorial area
[
29
, Theorem 6.1]
.

5.13.
Train routes wiggle

We next control the train rays of the upper and lower tracks, in the presence of a veering triangulation.

Definition 5.0.25
.

Suppose that
K
is a layer of a layering.
Fix an orientation on
{\widetilde{M}}
and a transverse orientation on
{\widetilde{B}}
.
This induces an orientation on
K
.
A train ray in
\tau^{K}
travels through the triangles of
K
, entering through one edge of each triangle and exiting through the edge either to the left or the right.
We refer to the former as a
left turn
and the latter as a
right turn
.

Lemma 5.0.26
.

Suppose that
K
is a layer of a layering and
\rho
is a train ray in the upper track
\tau^{K}
on
K
.
Then
\rho
turns both left and right infinitely often.
The same is true for a train ray in the lower track
\tau_{K}
.

(
a
)
An upper train track that carries a left-turning ray that crosses only blue edges.

(
b
)
An upper train track that carries a left-turning ray that crosses only red edges (left). The corresponding lower track (right).

Figure 5.14.

Proof 5.0.27
.

Suppose, for a contradiction, that
\rho
is a train ray carried by
\tau^{K}
that turns left forever.
Consulting Figure
5.4
, we see that
\rho
can cross a face of
K
and travel from a blue edge to a blue edge, from a blue edge to a red edge, or from a red edge to a red edge, but that it can never travel from a red edge to a blue edge.
Replacing
\rho
by a sub-ray, we may assume that either
\rho
crosses only blue edges or
\rho
crosses only red edges.
Let
P\subset K
be the union of triangles that
\rho
passes through.
The two possibilities are shown in Figure
5.14
.

Suppose that we are in the first case, shown in Figure
5.14a
.
There are no sinks of
\tau^{P}
in
K
meeting any edge (interior or boundary) of
P
.
Thus it is impossible to layer a tetrahedron onto any triangle of
P
.
Therefore
K
is not a layer of a layering, a contradiction.

Suppose that we are in the second case, shown in Figure
5.14b
.
Here we instead consider the lower train track
\tau_{P}
.
We conclude that it is impossible to layer a tetrahedron onto the bottom of
P
, and again we reach a contradiction.

Similar arguments apply to train rays in
\tau^{K}
that turn right forever, and train rays in
\tau_{K}
that turn in either direction forever.

Chapter 6
Branched surfaces and branch lines

Here, following ideas of Agol, given
M
, an oriented three-manifold equipped with a transverse veering triangulation
\mathcal{V}
, we construct the upper and lower branched surfaces
B^{\mathcal{V}}
and
B_{\mathcal{V}}
.
Their branch loci play an important role in Chapter
8
.

6.1.
Branched surfaces

Again, see
[
7
, Section 6.3]
for a reference on branched surfaces.

We construct the
upper branched surface

B^{\mathcal{V}}
in two stages.
First, fix a tetrahedron
t
in
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
.
Let
L
be the two-triangle landscape above
t
and
L^{\prime}
the two-triangle landscape below.
Note that
\tau^{L}
, the upper track for
L
, is obtained from the upper track for
L^{\prime}
by doing a single split.
If we perform this split continuously then it sweeps out a two-complex
B^{t}
, shown in Figure
6.2a
.

The two-complex
B^{t}
is not quite a branched surface;
at the unique vertex the two one-cells are tangent rather than transverse.
We abuse terminology and nonetheless call
B^{t}
the
upper
branched surface, in
t
, in
normal position
.
We use this terminology because the sectors of
B^{t}
are three
normal disks
:
two triangles and a quadrilateral.
The track-cusps sweep along the two branch components of
B^{t}
;
each branch interval is a normal arc in a lower face of
t
.
The three sectors and two branch intervals meet at the midpoint of the bottom edge of
t
;
this is the unique vertex of
B^{t}
.
Again, see Figure
6.2a
.

We now remedy the failure of
B^{t}
to be a branched surface.
We fold
B^{t}
in a small regular neighbourhood of its branch intervals.
We call the result the
dual position
of
B^{t}
because, after folding,
B^{t}
is isotopic to the dual two-skeleton for
t
.
See Figure
6.2b
;
note how the branch locus is no longer contained in the lower faces of
t
.

(
a
)
Normal position.

(
b
)
Dual position.

Figure 6.2.
Two positions of the upper branched surface in a tetrahedron.

We now push
B^{t}
down to
M
.
We define
B^{\mathcal{V}}\subset M
, the
upper branched surface
in
normal position
or in
dual position
, to be the union
\bigcup_{t\in\mathcal{V}}B^{t}
where the latter are also in normal or dual position.

Remark 6.0.1
.

We justify giving a single name,
B^{\mathcal{V}}
, to the two positions with the following claim:
the normal and dual positions of
B^{\mathcal{V}}
are isotopic in
M
.
To see this, start with
B^{\mathcal{V}}
in normal position.
Now isotope
B^{\mathcal{V}}
slightly upwards.
This makes the branch locus transverse to
B(\mathcal{V})
.
We may then isotope a bit further, to make the branch locus self-transverse.
The result is
B^{\mathcal{V}}
in dual position.
Again, see Figure
6.2b
.

We will almost always draw
B^{\mathcal{V}}
in normal position.
We define
B_{\mathcal{V}}
similarly, in both its normal and dual position,
by using the lower tracks
\tau_{L}
and
\tau_{L^{\prime}}
to build
B_{t}
, and so on.

Remark 6.0.2
.

The branched surfaces
B^{\mathcal{V}}
and
B_{\mathcal{V}}
(in dual position) are both isotopic to the dual two-skeleton of
\mathcal{V}
.
It follows that
B^{\mathcal{V}}
and
B_{\mathcal{V}}
are isotopic two-complexes (that is, ignoring their branched surface structures).
Again, see Figure
6.2b
.

6.3.
Branch lines

The branched surface
B^{\mathcal{V}}\subset M
cuts
M
into a disjoint union of
upper cusp neighbourhoods
.
Each of these is homeomorphic to a torus (or annulus, or plane) crossed with a ray.
Suppose that
\check{c}
is a cusp of
\mathcal{V}
and
\check{N}
is the corresponding upper cusp neighbourhood.
We will abuse notation slightly and identify
\check{N}
with its closure in the induced path metric; this makes
\check{N}
homeomorphic to a torus (or annulus, or plane) cross an interval.
The inner boundary of
\check{N}
receives a piecewise smooth structure from
B^{\mathcal{V}}
.
The locus of non-smooth points in
\partial\check{N}
is the union of
upper branch loops (or lines)
for
\check{N}
.
We orient the upper branch loops (or lines) so that, with
B^{\mathcal{V}}
in dual position, the orientation on an upper branch loop (or line) agrees with the transverse orientation on
B(\mathcal{V})
.

Suppose that
\check{S}
is an upper branch loop (or line) in
\check{N}
.
Let
c
be an elevation of
\check{c}
to
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
.
Let
N^{c}
be the corresponding elevation of
\check{N}
.
Let
S\subset N^{c}
be an elevation of
\check{S}
.
We call
S
an
upper branch line
.

Lemma 6.0.3
.

Branch lines are in fact lines – not intervals, rays, or loops.

Proof 6.0.4
.

With notation as immediately above:
\check{S}
cannot be an interval or a ray as it has no boundary.
Thus
\check{S}
is either a loop or a line.
Consulting Figure
6.2a
we find that we may orient
\check{S}
to everywhere agree with the given co-orientation.

There are now two cases as
\check{S}
is a loop or a line.
If it is a line then
S
is as well and we are done.
If it is a loop then it is
vertical
in sense of
[
53
, Definition 2.1]
.
Applying
[
53
, Theorem 3.2]
we find that
\check{S}
is essential in
\pi_{1}(M)
.

Definition 6.0.5
.

We say two upper branch lines
S
and
T
in
N^{c}
are
adjacent
if they are not separated by a third branch line in
N^{c}
.

Figure 6.4.
The boundary of an upper cusp neighbourhood
N^{c}
:
the component of
{\widetilde{M}}-{\widetilde{B}}^{\mathcal{V}}
containing the cusp
c
.
The black lines represent the branch lines in
N^{c}
.

We now show that Figures
6.4
and
6.5
give an accurate depiction of an upper cusp neighbourhood.

\labellist
\hair

2pt

\pinlabel
c
[t] at 137 90

\endlabellist

Figure 6.5.
The strip of triangles
K(c)
in the layer
K
meeting the cusp
c
.
The complementary component of the train track
\tau^{K(c)}
meeting
c
is the intersection of
N^{c}
and
K
.
The track-cusps in the interior of
N^{c}
are shaded grey.

Lemma 6.0.6
.

Suppose that
c
is a cusp of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
.
Let
(S_{i})
be the branch lines on the boundary of
N=N^{c}
.
Fix
K
, a layer of some layering
\mathcal{K}
of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
.

(1)

For each
i
, there is a unique track-cusp
s_{i}
of
S_{i}
meeting
K
.

(2)

The upper branch lines in
N
, equipped with the adjacency relation, are indexed by
\mathbb{Z}
.

(3)

The intersection
N\cap K
is contained in the faces of
K
incident to
c
.
The track-cusp
s_{i}
points away from
c
, in the face that contains
s_{i}
and meets
c
.

(4)

The strip in
\partial N
cobounded by
S_{i}
and
S_{i+1}
meets
K
in a train interval running from
s_{i}
to
s_{i+1}
.

(5)

The cusp neighbourhood
N
is homeomorphic to
(N\cap K)\times\mathbb{R}
.

Proof 6.0.7
.

Fix a branch line
S_{i}
and fix a layer
K
.
Fix also a track-cusp
s^{\prime}
of
S_{i}
.
The track-cusp
s^{\prime}
lies in some face
f^{\prime}
, which lies in some layer
K^{\prime}
.
We now induct on the number of layers between
K
and
K^{\prime}
to find some track-cusp
s
of
S_{i}
lying in
K
.
This proves the existence claimed in (
1
).

We now prove uniqueness.
Let
s
and
s^{\prime}
be adjacent track-cusps of
S_{i}
, with
s^{\prime}
below
s
.
Let
f
and
f^{\prime}
be the faces of
{\widetilde{B}}
that contain
s
and
s^{\prime}
respectively.
Consulting Figure
6.2a
, we see that
f
and
f^{\prime}
are upper and lower faces of a tetrahedron
t
.
Let
e
be their shared edge.
The dihedral angle between
f
and
f^{\prime}
at
e
is zero.
Since the circular order
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{V}}
is compatible (Definition
2.0.13
), the faces
f
and
f^{\prime}
cannot lie in a single layer.

Let
L
be the lowest layer of
\mathcal{K}
containing
f
.
By the above paragraph,
f^{\prime}
is below
L
.
Recall that
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
by Proposition
5.0.2
and Lemma
4.0.21
.
Thus
L
separates
f^{\prime}
from all layers above
L
.
Thus no layer can contain
s^{\prime}
and a track cusp of
S_{i}
above
s^{\prime}
.
This proves uniqueness and thus we have (
1
).

By (
1
), every branch line
S_{i}
in the boundary of
N
meets
K
in a track-cusp
s_{i}
.
Let
K(c)
be the strip of triangles in the layer
K
meeting the cusp
c
.
Thus
N\cap K
is contained in
K(c)
.
Thus all of the
s_{i}
lie in
K(c)
.
By Lemma
5.0.26
, the track-cusps
(s_{i})
exit both ends of
K(c)
, giving (
2
) and (
3
).

The strip in
\partial N
cobounded by
S_{i}
and
S_{i+1}
meets
K
transversely within
\tau^{K}
.
The endpoints are the switches contained in
s_{i}
and
s_{i+1}
, giving (
4
).
See Figure
6.5
.

Suppose that
K^{\prime}
is the layer of
\mathcal{K}
immediately above
K
.
Move
K
and
K^{\prime}
slightly apart, keeping both carried by the branched surface
{\widetilde{B}}
.
Let
t
be the tetrahedron between
K
and
K^{\prime}
.
If
t\cap N
is empty then
K
and
K^{\prime}
cobound a product region
P\mathrel{\cong}(N\cap K)\times[0,1]
.
The product
P
is in turn carried by
{\widetilde{B}}
.
Suppose instead that
t\cap N
is non-empty.
Away from
t
the layers again cobound a product region
P_{0}
.
Let
Q_{t}
be a small closed neighbourhood of
t
.
Inside of
Q_{t}\cap N
the layers
K
and
K^{\prime}
cobound a small neighbourhood
P_{t}
of
t\cap N
.
Consulting Figure
6.2a
we see that
P_{t}
is again a product region.
We form
P=P_{0}\cup P_{t}
.
Again
P\mathrel{\cong}(N\cap K)\times[0,1]
and we have proven (
5
).
See Figure
6.4
.

Remark 6.0.8
.

Lemma
6.0.6
(
2
) is a version of
[
17
, Observation 2.3]
in our context.
When
M
is compact, the branch loops in
M
equip every component of
\partial M
with a
branch slope
.
Similar slopes are called the
ladderpole slopes
in
[
17
, Observation 2.4]
.
We give more details in Section
A.2
.

More generally, the branch loops are similar to the

parabolic locus
of
pared manifolds

[
42
, Definition 4.8]
,
the
degeneracy slopes
of
[
20
, page 62]
,
and the
maw loops
of
[
43
, page 27]
.

Corollary 6.0.9
.

Suppose that
S
is an upper branch line of
{\widetilde{B}}^{\mathcal{V}}
in
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
.
Then every subray of
S
meets the lower faces of some toggle tetrahedron.
In particular every subray of
S
meets both red and blue edges.
A similar statement holds for the lower branch lines of
{\widetilde{B}}_{\mathcal{V}}
.

Proof 6.0.10
.

Let
R\subset S
be an ascending subray.
Thus
R
passes through an infinite sequence
(s_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
of track-cusps.
Let
f_{i}
be the face containing
s_{i}
.
Let
t_{i}
be the tetrahedron immediately above
f_{i}
.
Suppose, for a contradiction, that
t_{i}
is always a fan tetrahedron.
It follows that all of the
t_{i}
are same colour and attached to a single edge of the opposite colour.
See Figure
5.5
.
But this contradicts the finiteness of edge degrees.

A similar argument applies when
R
is a descending ray or is contained in a lower branch line.

6.6.
Sectors

We now consider the various ways the normal quadrilaterals and triangles of
B^{\mathcal{V}}
can meet.
See Figure
5.5a
.
Recall that
\mathcal{V}
endows the faces and edges of
B(\mathcal{V})
with a co-orientation.

Lemma 6.0.11
.

Suppose that
D
is a sector of
B^{\mathcal{V}}
(in normal position). Then
D
is a disk which

•

contains exactly one normal quadrilateral,

•

contains exactly two (possibly empty) collections of normal triangles above the normal quadrilateral (to its left and right),

•

has exactly one
upper
(
lower
)
vertex
where the co-orientation points out of (into)
D
, at the top (bottom) of the normal quadrilateral,

•

has exactly two
cusp vertices
where
\partial D
has a cusp with respect to the co-orientation, and

•

has an
under-side vertex
, where the co-orientation points into
D
, at the bottom of each normal triangle.

Proof 6.0.12
.

Considering Figure
6.2a
, we see that in each tetrahedron, the branched surface consists of one normal quadrilateral and two normal triangles.
Both bottom edges of the quadrilateral, and precisely one bottom edge of each triangle, lies in the branch locus. Thus these bottom edges form part of the boundary of the containing sector.
The upper edges of the normal disks are either in the boundary of the sector or are glued to the free bottom edge of some triangle.
The gluings between the normal disks inside of a sector are given in Figure
5.5a
.
The desired properties follow.

See Figure
6.7
for a fairly generic example of a sector of
B^{\mathcal{V}}
.

(
a
)

(
b
)

Figure 6.7.
Any sector of
B^{\mathcal{V}}
(in normal position) contains exactly one normal quadrilateral and some (perhaps zero) normal triangles.
The boundary of the sector is drawn in black, while internal boundaries between normal disks are drawn in green.
In Figure
6.7a
we draw a sector in a style that emphasises the smoothing coming from
B(\mathcal{V})
.
In Figure
6.7b
we draw the same sector in a polygonal style, without smoothing.

6.8.
Leaves

We refer to
[
7
, Definitions 6.9 and 6.14, page 215]
for the definitions of
surface laminations
in three-manifolds, their
leaves
,
essential laminations
, and how branched surfaces
carry
laminations.

Suppose that
\mathcal{V}
is a transverse veering triangulation.
Let
B^{\mathcal{V}}
be the resulting upper branched surface (the discussion is similar for
B_{\mathcal{V}}
).

Remark 6.0.13
.

The upper and lower branched surfaces
B^{\mathcal{V}}
and
B_{\mathcal{V}}
are
laminar
in the sense of Tao Li
[
39
, Definition 1.4]
.
Thus they carry essential laminations.
However more is true.
Since
\mathcal{V}
is veering, there is a canonical pair of laminations
\Sigma^{\mathcal{V}}
and
\Sigma_{\mathcal{V}}
fully carried by
B^{\mathcal{V}}
and
B_{\mathcal{V}}
respectively.
Furthermore,
\Sigma^{\mathcal{V}}
and
\Sigma_{\mathcal{V}}
are the
only
laminations fully carried by
B^{\mathcal{V}}
and
B_{\mathcal{V}}
, up to Denjoy operations.
See Sections
8.12
and
8.15
.

When
\mathcal{V}
is not finite, we still have canonical laminations
\Sigma^{\mathcal{V}}
and
\Sigma_{\mathcal{V}}
.
These are needed in order to complete our programme of classifying pseudo-Anosov flows (without perfect fits).

We draw the sectors in the style of Figure
6.7b
.
This leaf
\sigma
is fully carried by the upper branched surface for the veering triangulation of the figure-eight knot complement given in Figure
3.2
.
There are two kinds of sector, here coloured light blue and light red according to the colour of the dual edge, as in Remark
6.0.2
.

Any leaf
\sigma
of any lamination
\Sigma
carried by
B^{\mathcal{V}}
(or
B_{\mathcal{V}}
) inherits a decomposition into sectors.
For an example, see Figure
6.9
.
Here
\sigma
is fully carried by the upper branched surface for the veering triangulation of the figure-eight knot complement, given in Figure
3.2
.

)

)

)

A leaf carried by the upper branched surface is patterned by the branch lines of the branched surface in one of these three ways in the vicinity of a vertex of the branched surface (compare with Figure
6.2b
).

Suppose that
\sigma
is a leaf of any lamination
\Sigma
carried by
B^{\mathcal{V}}
(say).
The decomposition of
B^{\mathcal{V}}
into sectors induces a cellulation of
\sigma
.
In this cellulation, drawn in the style of Figure
6.7b
, the two-cells are diamonds (possibly with many vertices on their lower sides) while the vertices all have degree three or four.
We call these vertices, respectively,
T-junctions
or
X-junctions
.
See Figure
6.10
.

Chapter 7
The veering circle

In this section we build the
veering circle

S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
.
Here is the desired statement.

Theorem 7.0.0
.

Suppose that
M
is an oriented three-manifold equipped with a transverse veering triangulation
\mathcal{V}
.
Then the order completion of
(\Delta_{\mathcal{V}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{V}})
is a circle
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
with the following properties.

(1)

The action of
\pi_{1}(M)
on
\Delta_{\mathcal{V}}
extends to give a continuous, faithful, orientation-preserving action on
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
.

(2)

If
\mathcal{V}
is finite then all orbits are dense.

7.2.
Arcs at infinity

We introduce several pieces of notation.

Definition 7.0.1
.

Suppose that
M
is a three-manifold equipped with a transverse veering triangulation
\mathcal{V}
.
Suppose that
a
and
b
are cusps in
\Delta_{\mathcal{V}}
.
Define

[a,b]_{\Delta}^{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\curvearrowleft$}}}=\{c\in\Delta_{\mathcal{V}}\mathbin{\mid}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{V}}(a,c,b)\geq 0\}

This is the
arc
in
\Delta_{\mathcal{V}}
that is anticlockwise of
a
and clockwise of
b
.

Now fix an edge
e
of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
.
Orient
e
by ordering the cusps
c
and
c^{\prime}
at the ends of
e
.
This, together with the transverse taut structure on
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
determines a co-orientation of
e
using the right-hand rule.
We set
\Delta(e)=[c^{\prime},c]_{\Delta}^{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\curvearrowleft$}}}
.
Note that
e
and
\Delta(e)
have the same endpoints.
The co-orientation on
e
points towards the arc
\Delta(e)
.
See Figure
7.3
.

\labellist
\hair

2pt

\pinlabel
c
at 13 450

\pinlabel
c^{\prime}
at 565 450

\pinlabel
e
at 220 340

\pinlabel
\Delta(e)
at 220 610

\endlabellist

\begin{array}[]{c}\includegraphics[height]{Figures/cooriented_edge}\end{array}

Figure 7.3.
The arc
\Delta(e)
(drawn dashed) in
\Delta_{\mathcal{V}}
corresponding to a co-oriented edge
e
.
In Section
7.4
, we take the order completion of
\Delta_{\mathcal{V}}
to obtain
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
.
The closure of
\Delta(e)
is the arc
A(e)\subset S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
.

Suppose that
K
is a layer of a layering of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
.
The edges in
K
make it into a copy of the Farey tessellation (Lemma
4.0.2
).
The dual graph is an infinite trivalent tree
T
.
We record the following fact.

Lemma 7.0.2
.

Suppose that
\rho
is a ray in
T
.
Let
(e_{n})
be the edges crossed by
\rho
.
Then
\rho
turns left and right infinitely often if and only if the nested intersection
\bigcap\Delta(e_{n})
is empty.

Proof 7.0.3
.

Suppose that
\rho
turns left and right infinitely often.
Suppose that
c
is a cusp of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
.
Let
H
be the line in
T
adjacent to
c
.
Thus no sub-ray of
\rho
is contained in
H
.

Let
e_{m}\in(e_{n})
be the edge which has, among all edges of
(e_{n})
closest to
H
, the largest index.
Thus, either
e_{m}
is the last edge (that
\rho
crosses) that meets
c
, or
e_{m}
is the second edge (that
\rho
crosses) of a Farey triangle, the third edge of which separates
\rho
from
c
.
In either case,
c
is not in the arc
\Delta(e_{m+1})
.

On the other hand, suppose that
\rho
turns right only finitely many times.
Thus there is a cusp
c
so that infinitely many of the
e_{n}
meet
c
.
Note that
\rho
turns left through all such
e_{n}
.
Appealing to the combinatorics of the Farey tessellation, we deduce that

\bigcap\Delta(e_{n})=\{c\}
.

Suppose that
\rho
is a train ray in the upper track
\tau^{K}
.
Let
(e_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be the sequence of co-oriented edges crossed by
\rho
;
here the co-orientation of
e_{n}
agrees with the orientation of
\rho
.
Note that the arc
\Delta(e_{n+1})
is contained in, and shares exactly one endpoint with, the arc
\Delta(e_{n})
.

Corollary 7.0.4
.

Let
(e_{n})
be the edges crossed by a train ray
\rho
(in some layer).
Then the nested intersection
\bigcap\Delta(e_{n})
is empty.

Proof 7.0.5
.

This follows from Lemmas
5.0.26
and
7.0.2
.

Suppose that
S
is an upper branch line.
Let
(s_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
be the track-cusps of
S
, where
s_{n+1}
is the track-cusp immediately above
s_{n}
.
Let
e_{n}
be the edge meeting
s_{n}
.
We co-orient
e_{n}
away from
s_{n}
.
Note that
\Delta(e_{n+1})
is contained in, and shares exactly one endpoint with,
\Delta(e_{n})
.
See Figure
5.5a
for examples.

Lemma 7.0.6
.

Let
(e_{n})
be the edges crossed by the branch line
S
. Then the nested intersection
\bigcap\Delta(e_{n})
is empty.

The proof of Lemma
7.0.6
is more difficult than that of Corollary
7.0.4
. This is because a branch line, unlike a train ray, is never contained in a single layer of a layering, by Lemma
6.0.6
(
1
).

Proof 7.0.7

(Proof of Lemma
7.0.6
)
.

Fix a cusp
c
of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
.
By Proposition
5.0.2
there is a continent
C
which meets both
S
and
c
.
By Lemma
5.0.12
we can assume that
C
is convex.
We define a sequence of convex continents
(C_{n})
as follows.

Let
C_{0}=C
.
Let
L_{n}
be the upper landscape of
C_{n}
, let
s_{n}
be the track-cusp of
L_{n}
meeting
S
, and let
f_{n}\subset L_{n}
be the face containing
s_{n}
.
Note that the edge of
f_{n}
meeting
s_{n}
is one of the edges associated to
S
.
Choosing indices correctly, we arrange that
e_{n}
(the edge crossed by
S
, defined above) lies in
f_{n}
.
Note that
e_{n}
is co-oriented away from
f_{n}
.

Let
\tau^{n}
be the upper track for
L_{n}
and let
R_{n}\subset L_{n}
be the maximal river with
f_{n}
as its source.
Since
C_{n}
is convex, the mouth,
e^{\prime}_{n}
say, of
R_{n}
is coastal.
We co-orient
e^{\prime}_{n}
away from
C_{n}
.
Let
c^{\prime}_{n}
and
d^{\prime}_{n}
be the cusps at the endpoints of
e^{\prime}_{n}
meeting the left and right riverbanks of
R_{n}
, respectively.
To obtain
C_{n+1}
we channelise repeatedly until the face
f_{n}
is covered;
by Lemma
5.0.17
this process terminates.

The proof of the following claim is omitted;
it is similar to the analysis of forked rivers in Lemma
5.0.17
.
See in particular Figure
5.10
(A) and (B).

Claim
.

For all
n>0
, the interval
\Delta(e_{n})
shares an endpoint with
\Delta(e^{\prime}_{n})
which is not a cusp of
C_{n-1}
.
This new shared endpoint is either
c^{\prime}_{n}
or
d^{\prime}_{n}
as the edge
e_{n}
is blue or red. ∎

By Corollary
6.0.9
, both colours appear amongst the edges
(e_{n})
.
Thus, for sufficiently large
m
, neither
c^{\prime}_{0}
nor
d^{\prime}_{0}
lies in the interval
\Delta(e_{m})
.
We deduce that no cusp of
C_{0}
lies in
\Delta(e_{m})
.
In particular,
c
does not lie in
\bigcap\Delta(e_{n})
.

7.4.
Completing the cusps

Suppose that
\Delta
is a countable set and
\mathcal{O}
is a dense circular order in the sense of Definition
2.0.3
.
The
order completion
of the pair
(\Delta,\mathcal{O})
is homeomorphic to
S^{1}
.
See
[
60
, Proposition 2.1.7]
or
[
7
, Theorem 2.47]
.

Definition 7.0.8
.

Suppose that
M
is a three-manifold equipped with a transverse veering triangulation
\mathcal{V}
.
Let
\Delta_{\mathcal{V}}
be the set of cusps of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
.
Let
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{V}}
be the dense circular order given by Theorem
5.2
.
Thus the order completion of
(\Delta_{\mathcal{V}},\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{V}})
is a circle
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
;
we call this the
veering circle
.

Definition 7.0.9
.

For
x,y\in S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
we define
[x,y]^{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\curvearrowleft$}}}
to be the arc of
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
anticlockwise of
x
and clockwise of
y
.

Note that if
x
and
y
are cusps then this arc is the closure of
[x,y]_{\Delta}^{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\curvearrowleft$}}}
.
Similarly, we define
A(e)\subset S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
to be the closure of
\Delta(e)
.
See Figure
7.3
.

We have the following consequence of Corollary
7.0.4
and Lemma
7.0.6
.

Corollary 7.0.10
.

Suppose that
(e_{n})
is the sequence of edges of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
crossed by either

•

a train ray
\rho
(in some layer) or

•

a branch line
S
.

Then the intersection
\bigcap A(e_{n})
is a single non-cusp point of
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
. ∎

We call this point the
endpoint at infinity
and it is denoted by
\partial\rho
or
\partial S
, respectively.
An oriented train line
\sigma
(in some layer) has two endpoints at infinity, denoted
\partial_{\pm}\sigma
.

Remark 7.0.11
.

Suppose that
K
is a layer of a layering of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
.
Applying Lemma
4.0.2
, the triangulation of
K
is a copy of the Farey tessellation.
Fix
x\in S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
.
We now find a closed neighbourhood basis, about
x
, in
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
.

•

Suppose that
x
is not a cusp.
Transversely orient all edges
e
, of
K
, towards
x
.
By Lemma
7.0.2
, the resulting arcs
\{A(e)\}
give the desired basis.

•

Suppose that
x
is a cusp.
Suppose that
e
and
e^{\prime}
are edges of
K
meeting
x
that are co-oriented away from each other.
Now the unions
\{A(e)\cup A(e^{\prime})\}
give the desired basis.

Proof 7.0.12

(Proof of Theorem
7.1
(
1
))
.

Fix
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(M)
.
Suppose that
a,b\in\Delta_{\mathcal{V}}
are distinct cusps.
Since
\gamma
preserves the circular order on
\Delta_{\mathcal{V}}
(by Theorem
5.2
) the image of
[a,b]^{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\curvearrowleft$}}}\cap\Delta_{\mathcal{V}}
is
[\gamma a,\gamma b]^{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\curvearrowleft$}}}\cap\Delta_{\mathcal{V}}
.
This implies that
\gamma
is well-defined on the non-cusp points of
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
.
and thus sends arcs to arcs. Thus
\gamma
is continuous
and orientation preserving, and so is
\gamma^{-1}
.
Thus
\gamma
is a homeomorphism.
Finally, the action is faithful because
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
contains
\Delta_{\mathcal{V}}
.

7.5.
Parabolics

In this section, we assume that
\mathcal{V}
is finite.
For the proof of Theorem
7.1
(
2
), we must analyse the action of cusp stabilisers in
\pi_{1}(M)
.
Suppose that
\check{c}
is a cusp of
\mathcal{T}
.
Let
\check{N}\subset M
be the upper cusp neighbourhood of
\check{c}
.
Fix a basepoint
\check{p}\in\check{N}
.
We lift to obtain a cusp
c\in\Delta_{\mathcal{V}}
and a basepoint
p\in N=N^{c}
.
This gives an isomorphism between
\pi_{1}(M)
and the deck group of
{\widetilde{M}}
, and thus an isomorphism between the
\operatorname{Stab}(c)
and
\pi_{1}(\check{N})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
We suppress this from our notation.

Definition 7.0.13
.

Let
\beta\in\operatorname{Stab}(c)
be the based homotopy class whose free homotopy class contains any (thus all) oriented branch loops
\check{S}
in
\partial\check{N}
.
In an abuse of notation we again call
\beta
the
branch slope
.

By Lemma
6.0.6
(
2
) we may index the branch lines of
N=N^{c}
, say
(S_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
, so that
S_{i}
and
S_{i+1}
are adjacent on
\partial N
.
We choose the indexing so that the
S_{i}
march anticlockwise around
N
as viewed from above.
We have the following.

Lemma 7.0.14
.

(1)

S^{1}(\mathcal{V})-\{c\}=\bigcup_{i}\,[\partial S_{i},\partial S_{i+1}]^{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\curvearrowleft$}}}

(2)

Suppose that
\gamma\in\operatorname{Stab}(c)-{\langle\beta\rangle}
, where
\beta
is the branch slope.
Then there is a non-zero integer
k
so that, for all
i
, we have
\gamma(\partial S_{i})=\partial S_{i+k}
.
Also, if
x
is any point of
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
then
\gamma^{n}(x)
converges to
c
as
n
tends to infinity.

Figure 7.6.
The points
(\partial S_{i})
converge to
c
as
i
tends to infinity. Compare with Figure
6.5
.

Proof 7.0.15
.

Suppose that
K
is a layer of a layering of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
.
By Lemma
6.0.6
(
1
) there is a unique track-cusp
s_{i}
of
S_{i}
contained in
K
.
By Lemma
6.0.6
(
3
), the track-cusp
s_{i}
meets an edge
e_{i}\subset K
which we co-orient away from
c
.
See Figure
6.5
.
By Corollary
7.0.10
we deduce that
\partial S_{i}\in A(e_{i})
.
From Remark
7.0.11
and Lemma
6.0.6
(
4
) we deduce that the points
\partial S_{i}
tend to
c
as
i
tends to infinity.
See Figure
7.6
.
This proves (
1
).

Since
\gamma
is not a power of
\beta
, it crosses
\beta
.
Suppose that there are
n
branch loops in
\partial\check{N}
.
Let
m
be the algebraic intersection number between
\gamma
and
\beta
.
Set
k=mn
.
We now deduce (
2
) from (
1
).

Proof 7.0.16

(Proof of Theorem
7.1
(
2
))
.

Fix any point
x\in S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
.
Also, fix a cusp
c\in\Delta_{\mathcal{V}}
.
Suppose that
\gamma\in\operatorname{Stab}(c)
is not a power of the branch slope.
By Lemma
7.0.14
(
2
) the sequence
\gamma^{n}(x)
converges to
c
.
Thus the orbit of
x
accumulates on
\Delta_{\mathcal{V}}
;
the latter is dense in
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
by construction.

Chapter 8
Laminations

In this section we build the
upper lamination

\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}
in
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
.
The
lower lamination

\Lambda_{\mathcal{V}}
is constructed similarly.

Theorem 8.0.0
.

Suppose that
M
is a three-manifold equipped with a transverse veering triangulation
\mathcal{V}
.
Then there is a lamination
\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}
in
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
with the following properties.

(1)

The lamination
\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}
is
\pi_{1}(M)
–invariant.

(2)

The lamination
\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}
suspends to give a
\pi_{1}(M)
–invariant lamination
{\widetilde{\Sigma}}^{\mathcal{V}}
in
{\widetilde{M}}
; this descends to
M
to give a lamination
\Sigma^{\mathcal{V}}
which

(a)

is fully carried by the upper branched surface
B^{\mathcal{V}}
,

(b)

has only plane, annulus, and Möbius band leaves, and

(c)

is essential.

(3)

Suppose that
\Sigma
is a lamination fully carried by
B^{\mathcal{V}}
.
Then, after collapsing parallel leaves,
\Sigma
is tie-isotopic to
\Sigma^{\mathcal{V}}
.

There is also a lamination
\Lambda_{\mathcal{V}}
with the corresponding properties with respect to
B_{\mathcal{V}}
.

We prove this in parts, giving the necessary definitions as we proceed.

8.2.
Laminations in the circle

Following Thurston
[
58
, page 187]
, we define
\mathcal{M}
to be the
Möbius band past infinity
:
that is,
S^{1}\times S^{1}
, minus the diagonal, and quotiented by the symmetry that interchanges the two factors.
A
leaf at infinity

\lambda=\lambda(a,b)
is the point of
\mathcal{M}
with
endpoints

a
and
b
.

Suppose that
\mu=\lambda(c,d)
is another leaf.
We say that
\lambda
and
\mu
are
asymptotic
if they share an endpoint.
We say that
\lambda
and
\mu
are
linked
if

•

the points
a
,
b
,
c
, and
d
are all distinct and

•

the points
c
and
d
lie in different components of
S^{1}-\{a,b\}
.

We say that
\lambda
and
\mu
are
unlinked
if they are not linked.

Definition 8.0.1
.

A
lamination at infinity

\Lambda
in
S^{1}
is a closed subset of
\mathcal{M}

where no pair of leaves
\lambda,\mu\in\Lambda
are linked.

Recall that
S^{1}
is the Gromov boundary of the hyperbolic plane
\mathbb{H}^{2}
and that
\mathcal{M}
parametrises the space of unoriented bi-infinite geodesics in
\mathbb{H}^{2}
.
This justifies the terminology of Definition
8.0.1
.

8.3.
From cusps to train lines

Fix
c
a cusp, set
N=N^{c}
, and let
S
be an upper branch line of
N
.
Suppose that
s
is a track-cusp of
S
.
Suppose that
f
is the face of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
containing
s
.
Note that
c
is a vertex of
f
.
We define a
connecting arc

\ell^{f}(c,s)\subset f
to be

•

a smooth arc in
f
,

•

connecting
c
to the switch in
s
, and

•

meeting
\tau^{f}
only at that switch.

We take
\ell^{f}(s,c)=\ell^{f}(c,s)
.
If
K
is a layer containing
f
, then we set
\ell^{K}(c,s)=\ell^{f}(c,s)
.
See Figure
8.4
.

\labellist
\hair

2pt

\pinlabel
c
at -5 0

\pinlabel
s
at 73 37

\endlabellist

Figure 8.4.
The connecting arc (dotted) from the cusp
c
to the track-cusp
s
.

Definition 8.0.2
.

Suppose that
K
is a layer of a layering.
Lemma
6.0.6
(
1
) implies that the upper branch line
S
meets
K
in a track-cusp, say
s
.
We define
\ell^{K}(s,S)\subset K
, the
cusp train ray
from
s
to
\partial S
, to be a train ray carried by
\tau^{K}
starting at
s
and with endpoint at infinity
\partial S
.

We define
\ell^{K}(c,S)\subset K
, the
cusp line
from
c
to
\partial S
, to be the union
\ell^{K}(c,S)=\ell^{K}(c,s)\cup\ell^{K}(s,S)
.

Lemma 8.0.3
.

Suppose that
c\in\Delta_{\mathcal{V}}
is a cusp.
Suppose that
K
is a layer of a layering
\mathcal{K}
.
Suppose that
S
is an upper branch line in the boundary of
N^{c}
.
Then a cusp line
\ell^{K}(c,S)
exists.
It is properly embedded in
K
and is uniquely determined (up to isotopy of the connecting arc) by
c
and
S
.

Proof 8.0.4
.

Choose indices in
\mathcal{K}=(K_{i})
so that
K_{0}=K
.
We define
\tau^{i}
to be the upper track for
K_{i}
.
Also, applying Lemma
6.0.6
(
1
),
we choose the indexing of the track-cusps of
S
so that
s_{i}
is the track-cusp of
S
in
K_{i}
.
Note that here we allow
s_{i+1}=s_{i}
.

Let
\Phi^{i}\colon\tau^{i+1}\to\tau^{i}
be the
carrying map
;
it is the identity everywhere except at one watershed which is folded by
\Phi^{i}
to produce a sink.
See Figure
6.2
.
Note that
\Phi^{i}
sends switches to switches.
We extend
\Phi^{i}
to obtain a map from
K_{i+1}
to
K_{i}
.
It is a homeomorphism on all components of
K_{i+1}-\tau^{i+1}
except for two.
In those two it crushes two track-cusps which are then carried by
\tau^{i}
.
In particular, if
s_{i+1}\neq s_{i}
then
\Phi^{i}(s_{i+1})\neq s_{i}
.
Again, see Figure
6.2
.

Fix
i<j
and define
\Phi^{i,j}\colon\tau^{j}\to\tau^{i}
by composing carrying maps.
When
i=j
we take
\Phi^{i,i}
to be the identity map.

For each
i
, fix a connecting arc
\ell^{i}=\ell^{i}(c,s_{i})\subset K_{i}
.
We arrange matters so that
\Phi^{i,i+1}(\ell^{i+1})
contains
\ell^{i}
.
Set
\ell^{i,j}=\Phi^{i,j}(\ell^{j})
.
Thus, for any
i\leq j\leq k
we have
\ell^{i,j}\subset\ell^{i,k}
.
Furthermore, the containment is proper whenever
s_{k}\neq s_{j}
.

Set

\ell^{i}(c,S)=\bigcup_{i\leq j}\ell^{i,j}\quad\text{and}\quad\ell^{i}(s_{i},S)=\ell^{i}(c,S)-\ell^{i}(c,s_{i})

Claim
.

For all
i
, the union
\ell^{i}(c,S)
is a cusp line.
Moreover,
\ell^{i}(s_{i},S)
is a cusp train ray.

This, and setting
\ell^{K}(c,S)=\ell^{0}(c,S)
, proves the lemma.

We now prove the claim.
Since
\ell^{i}(s_{i},S)
is carried by
\tau^{i}
it is a one-manifold.
The non-compact end of
\ell^{i}(s_{i},S)
is properly embedded ray in
K_{i}
;
this is because it is an ascending union that does not stabilise and because
\tau^{i}
is properly embedded in
K_{i}
.
Since
\ell^{i}(c,S)
contains
\ell^{i}(c,s_{i})
the former is a properly embedded line in
K_{i}
.

All that is left is to show that the endpoint at infinity for
\ell^{i}(s_{i},S)
is
\partial S
.
Note that
\Phi^{i,j}(\ell^{j}(c,S))=\ell^{i}(c,S)
.
Thus
\ell^{i}(c,S)
and
\ell^{j}(c,S)
are identical in
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}

away from at most
j-i
tetrahedra (those between
K_{j}
and
K_{i}
).
We deduce that
\partial\ell^{i}(c,S)=\partial\ell^{j}(c,S)
for all
i
and
j
.

Take
e_{j}\subset K_{j}
to be the edge pointed at by the track-cusp
s_{j}
.
Recall that by Corollary
7.0.10
, the unique point of
\bigcap_{i\leq j}A(e_{j})
is
\partial S
.
Since the cusp train ray
\ell^{j}(s_{j},S)
crosses
e_{j}
, we deduce that
\partial\ell^{j}(s_{j},S)
lies in
A(e_{j})
.
We deduce that
\partial\ell^{i}(s_{i},S)=\partial S
.
This proves the claim and thus the lemma.

With notation as above, we define
\lambda(c,\partial S)
to be an
(upper) cusp leaf
associated to
S
.
We will abuse notation and write
\lambda(c,S)
for
\lambda(c,\partial S)
.

Lemma 8.0.5
.

Any pair of upper cusp leaves
\lambda(c,S)
and
\lambda(d,T)
are unlinked.

Proof 8.0.6
.

If
c=d
then
\lambda(c,S)
and
\lambda(c,T)
are asymptotic and thus unlinked.

Now assume that
c\neq d
.
Fix a layer
K
of a layering.
Let
\ell^{K}(c,s)
and
\ell^{K}(d,t)
be the associated connecting arcs.
These are disjoint.
This also holds for all layers above
K
;
applying the carrying maps we deduce that
\ell^{K}(c,S)
does not cross
\ell^{K}(d,T)
.

Lemma 8.0.7
.

Suppose that
S
and
T
are upper branch lines.
If
\partial S=\partial T
then
S=T
.

Proof 8.0.8
.

Let
(K_{i})
be a layering of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
. Let
\tau^{i}
be the upper track for
K_{i}
. Again, applying Lemma
6.0.6
(
1
), we index the track-cusps of
S
so that
s_{i}
lies in
K_{i}
; we define
t_{i}
similarly.

Let
\ell^{0}(s_{0},S)
and
\ell^{0}(t_{0},T)
be the cusp train rays given by Lemma
8.0.3
.
Since
\partial S=\partial T
, the cusp train rays eventually cross the same collection of edges in
K_{0}
.

If neither of
\ell^{0}(s_{0},S)
and
\ell^{0}(t_{0},T)
is contained in the other, then their union is a sub-tree
Y\subset\tau^{0}
.
Note that
Y
has a single trivalent vertex, smoothed according to
\tau^{0}
.
There is a track-cusp, say
u_{0}
, at this vertex.
Let
U
be the branch line running through
u_{0}
.
Let
a
,
b
and
c
be the cusps associated to
S
,
T
, and
U
.
Appealing to Lemma
8.0.5
, we find that the cusp leaf
\lambda(c,U)
does not link either
\lambda(a,S)
or
\lambda(b,T)
.
Thus
\partial S=\partial U=\partial T
and so
\ell^{0}(u_{0},U)\subset\ell^{0}(t_{0},T)
.

Relabelling if necessary, we thus restrict to the case where
\ell^{0}(s_{0},S)\subset\ell^{0}(t_{0},T)
.
If they are equal, we are done.
For a contradiction, suppose that they are not equal.
Consulting Figure
5.5a
, we deduce that
\ell^{i}(s_{i},S)\subset\ell^{i}(t_{i},T)
for all
i
.
Thus, the track-cusps
t_{i}
follow behind the track-cusps
s_{i}
forever.
Let
\ell^{i}(t_{i},s_{i})=\ell^{i}(t_{i},T)-\ell^{i}(s_{i},S)
be the train interval in
\tau^{i}
connecting
t_{i}
to
s_{i}
.

For each train track
\tau^{i}
, we measure the
trailing distance

d_{i}
, as follows.
This is the number of track-cusps incident to
\ell^{i}(t_{i},s_{i})
(including
s_{i}
but excluding
t_{i}
) that point in the same direction as
s_{i}
.
Figure
8.5
shows an example where
d_{i}
equals four.

at -5 30

\pinlabel

\pinlabel

at 184 20

\endlabellist

Figure 8.5.
Counting the distance between track-cusps
t_{i}
and
s_{i}
.

We claim that
d_{i+1}\leq d_{i}
.
To see this, note that as we move up through the layers, track-cusps move by splitting past each other.
Thus, track-cusps pointing in the same direction as
s_{i}
may leave
\ell^{i}(t_{i},s_{i})
but no such track-cusps may enter.

Thus, above some layer, say
K_{j}
, the trailing distance
d_{i}
becomes constant.
So for
i>j
all of the track-cusps in
\ell^{i}(t_{i},s_{i})
follow
s_{i}
forever.
We now consider the branch line
V
with track-cusps
v_{i}
that follow immediately behind
s_{i}
in
\ell^{i}(t_{i},s_{i})
; that is, at trailing distance one.

\endlabellist

(

)
A strip of triangles containing
\ell^{i}(v_{i},s_{i})
.

[br] at 55 85

\endlabellist

(
b
)
Before landfilling a tetrahedron
t
above
s_{i}
.

\labellist
\hair

2pt

\pinlabel
s_{i+1}
[br] at 65 52

\endlabellist

(
c
)
If the upper edge of
t
is blue.

\labellist
\hair

2pt

\pinlabel
s_{i+1}
[br] at 105 77

\endlabellist

(
d
)
If the upper edge of
t
is red.

Figure 8.6.

Let
P_{i}
be the minimal strip of triangles containing
\ell^{i}(v_{i},s_{i})
.
Breaking symmetry, we assume that
\ell^{j}(v_{j},s_{j})
enters the face containing
s_{j}
through a red edge.
Figure
8.6a
shows an example of what
P_{j}
could look like.

For
i\geq j
, as we move from layer
K_{i}
to
K_{i+1}
there are three possibilities; the tetrahedron
t
between them either:

•

misses
P_{i}
,

•

is layered on the edge that
s_{i}
points at, or

•

is layered on the edge that
v_{i}
points at.

The tetrahedron
t
cannot meet any other edges of
P_{i}
as they are not sinks.

If we layer onto the edge meeting
s_{i}
, as shown in Figure
8.6b
, then there are two possibilities, depending on the colour of the upper edge of
t
.
These are shown in Figures
8.6c
and
8.6d
.
Either the length of
P_{i+1}
equals that of
P_{i}
or goes up by one, with the new triangle being a majority red triangle.
In this case,
P_{i+1}
has one more internal red edge than
P_{i}
.

If we layer onto the edge meeting
v_{i}
, then the track-cusp
v_{i}
splits past the first track-cusp pointing backwards along
P_{i}
.
Thus
P_{i+1}
is one triangle shorter than
P_{i}
.
Note then, that there is no way for
P_{i+1}
to have more internal blue edges than
P_{i}
has.
Thus the branch line
V
visits only a finite number of blue edges.
This contradicts Corollary
6.0.9
.

8.7.
Building the laminations

Suppose that
S
and
T
are adjacent upper branch lines lying in
N=N^{c}
.
Fix a layer
K
of a layering; let
s
and
t
be the upper track-cusps in
K
meeting
S
and
T
respectively (Lemma
6.0.6
(
1
)).
Since
S
and
T
are adjacent, there is a train interval
\ell^{K}(s,t)\subset\tau^{K}\cap\partial N
connecting
s
to
t
(Lemma
6.0.6
(
4
)).
See Figure
6.5
.

Definition 8.0.9
.

The
boundary train line
in
K
between
S
and
T
is

\ell^{K}(S,T)=\ell^{K}(S,s)\cup\ell^{K}(s,t)\cup\ell^{K}(t,T)

The
(upper) boundary leaf
is
\lambda(S,T)=\lambda(\partial S,\partial T)
.
Suppose now that
R
is the other branch line in
N
adjacent to
S
.
Then
\lambda(R,S)
is
adjacent
to
\lambda(S,T)
.

Definition 8.0.10
.

Let
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{V})
be the Möbius band past infinity for
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
, as defined in Section
8.2
.
We define
\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}\subset\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{V})
, the
upper lamination
for
\mathcal{V}
, to be the closure of the union of upper boundary leaves:

\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}={\overline{\cup\,\lambda(S,T)}}

Here the pairs
(S,T)
range over all adjacent upper branch lines.
We equip
\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}\subset\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{V})
with the subspace topology.
Non-boundary leaves of
\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}
are called
interior leaves
.
Similarly, we define
\Lambda_{\mathcal{V}}
using the lower boundary leaves.

Remark 8.0.11
.

We justify the names
boundary
and
interior
, for leaves of the laminations
\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}
and
\Lambda_{\mathcal{V}}
, in Lemma
8.0.16
.

Definition 8.0.12
.

Any leaf
\lambda\in\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}
separates
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
into two components, which we call the two
sides
of
\lambda
.

Here we gather together the basic properties of
\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}
.

Lemma 8.0.13
.

The upper lamination
\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}
has the following properties.

(1)

It is a lamination.

(2)

It is
\pi_{1}(M)
–invariant.

(3)

For any leaf
\lambda\in\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}
and for any layer
K
of any layering,
there is a train line
\ell
carried by
\tau^{K}
with the same endpoints as
\lambda
.

(4)

For any layer
K
of any layering and for any branch
b
of
\tau^{K}
there is a leaf
\lambda\in\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}
so that the corresponding train line runs along
b
.

(5)

No endpoint of any leaf is a cusp.

(6)

A pair of distinct leaves
\lambda,\lambda^{\prime}\in\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}
share an endpoint if and only if they are adjacent boundary leaves.

Replacing upper by lower, we obtain the same properties for
\Lambda_{\mathcal{V}}
.

Proof 8.0.14
.

(1)

Being linked is an open property for pairs of leaves in
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{V})
.
So it suffices to prove that any pair of upper boundary leaves
\lambda(S,T)
and
\lambda(U,V)
are unlinked.
We fix a layer
K
and argue that the associated boundary train lines
\ell^{K}(S,T)
and
\ell^{K}(U,V)
are unlinked. Suppose that
S,T\subset N^{c}
and
U,V\subset N^{d}
.
We consider the unions
\ell^{K}(S,c)\cup\ell^{K}(c,T)
and
\ell^{K}(U,d)\cup\ell^{K}(d,V)
.
If
c=d
then we are done by the definition of adjacency and by Lemma
6.0.6
(
2
).
If
c\neq d
then, by Lemma
8.0.5
, the set
\{\partial U,d,\partial V\}
is contained in exactly one of the components of
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})-\{\partial S,c,\partial T\}
.

(2)

Suppose that
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(M)
is a deck transformation.
Suppose that
S
and
T
are upper branch lines.
Then
S
and
T
are adjacent if and only if
\gamma(S)
and
\gamma(T)
are adjacent.
Thus the collection of upper boundary leaves is
\pi_{1}(M)
–invariant and hence so is its closure.

(3)

If
\lambda=\lambda(S,T)
is a boundary leaf,
then we take
\ell=\ell^{K}(S,T)
to be the associated boundary train line.

Suppose instead that
\lambda
is an interior leaf.
Orient
\lambda
and let
\partial_{-}\lambda
and
\partial_{+}\lambda
be its endpoints.
Fix a sequence
(\lambda_{n})
of oriented upper boundary leaves converging (respecting orientation) to
\lambda
.
Pass to a subsequence to ensure that
\partial_{+}\lambda_{n}
converges to
\partial_{+}\lambda
monotonically from one side.
Applying (
1
), we deduce that
\partial_{-}\lambda_{n}
converges to
\partial_{-}\lambda
monotonically and from the same side.
Let
\ell_{n}\subset\tau^{K}
be the boundary train line associated to
\lambda_{n}
.

We now apply Remark
7.0.11
:
pick nested neighbourhood bases of
\partial_{-}\lambda
and
\partial_{+}\lambda
respectively.
If either is a cusp then its bases require a pair of edges;
we only keep those edges on the same side as the
\lambda_{n}
.
This gives us two collections of “nested” edges
(e^{-}_{i})
and
(e^{+}_{j})
.
We pass to subsequences repeatedly (both of the
\lambda_{n}
and of the
e^{\pm}_{i}
) to arrange that
\lambda_{j}
links both
e^{-}_{j}
and
e^{+}_{j}
.
Recall that
\tau^{K}
is a tree.
Thus the boundary train line
\ell_{j}
crosses
e^{-}_{j}
and
e^{+}_{j}
;
let
L_{j}
be the resulting unique train interval
L_{j}
running from
e^{-}_{j}
to
e^{+}_{j}
.
Note that
L_{j}
is a subinterval of all
\ell_{k}
for
k\geq j
.
Also, since the two collections of edges are each nested, we have
L_{j}\subset L_{j+1}
.
Thus
\ell=\bigcup_{j}L_{j}
is the desired train line.

(4)

By Lemma
6.0.6
there are upper branch lines
S
and
T
so that
b
lies on the boundary train line
\ell^{K}(S,T)
.
The desired leaf of
\Lambda^{K}
is then
\lambda(S,T)
.

(5)

This follows from property (
3
) and Corollary
7.0.10
.

(6)

The backwards direction follows from the definition of adjacency given in Definition
8.0.9
.

For the forward direction, suppose that
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
are distinct leaves of
\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}
that share a single common endpoint,
x
.
Let
y
and
y^{\prime}
be the other endpoints of
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
, respectively.

Suppose that both
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
are boundary leaves.
Lemma
8.0.7
implies that there is a branch line
S
so that
x=\partial S
.
Thus
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
are adjacent.

Suppose instead that
\lambda
is an interior leaf.
Let
K
be a layer of a layering of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
.
By (
3
), there are train lines
\ell
and
\ell^{\prime}
carried by
\tau^{K}
with the same endpoints as
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
respectively.
Thus the union of
\ell
and
\ell^{\prime}
forms a sub-tree
Y\subset\tau^{K}
with a single trivalent vertex, smoothed according to
\tau^{K}
.
See Figure
8.8
.

\hair

2pt

\pinlabel

at -5 50

\pinlabel

at 15 15

\pinlabel

at 12 43

\pinlabel

at 118 43

\pinlabel

at 109 -5

\pinlabel

[l] at 175 50

\endlabellist

Figure 8.8.
One possible picture for an asymptotic pair of train lines.

There is a track-cusp
s
at the vertex of
Y
.
Let
S
be the branch line containing
s
and let
c
be its associated cusp.
The points
y
and
y^{\prime}
are on opposite sides of
\ell^{K}(c,S)
.
Let
S^{\prime}
be the branch line adjacent to
S
on the side containing
y
.
Then
\ell^{K}(S,S^{\prime})
is a boundary train line.
By (
1
), it does not link either
\ell
or
\ell^{\prime}
.
Thus
\ell^{K}(S,S^{\prime})
has one endpoint at
x=\partial S
and the other at
\partial S^{\prime}
.
Since
\lambda
is not a boundary leaf, we have that
\ell\neq\ell^{K}(S,S^{\prime})
.
Thus, the
\partial S^{\prime}
is not equal to
y
.
We deduce that in
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
, the point
\partial S^{\prime}
is separated from
x
by
\{y,y^{\prime}\}
.

As in the above argument, the union of
\ell
and
\ell^{K}(S,S^{\prime})
forms a sub-tree giving a new track-cusp,
t
.
Let
T
be the branch line passing through
t
.
Again,
x=\partial T
.
This implies that
\partial S=\partial T
.
Since
s\neq t
, Lemma
6.0.6
(
1
) implies that
S\neq T
.
This contradicts Lemma
8.0.7
.

Proof 8.0.15

(Proof of Theorem
8.1
(
1
))
.

Parts (
1
) and (
2
) of Lemma
8.0.13
give the desired statement.

We now justify the names of boundary and interior leaves of
\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}
.
The following lemma proves that
\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}
is
perfect
: closed and without isolated leaves.

Lemma 8.0.16
.

For either side of an interior leaf
\lambda\in\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}
, there is a sequence of boundary leaves
\lambda_{k}
that converge to
\lambda
from that side.
A boundary leaf
\lambda\in\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}
is also a limit of boundary leaves
\lambda_{k}
, but only on the side not containing its cusp.
Similar properties hold for
\Lambda_{\mathcal{V}}
.

Proof 8.0.17
.

Let
K
be a layer of a layering.
Let
\ell
be the oriented train line carried by
\tau^{K}
with the same endpoints as
\lambda
, as given by Lemma
8.0.13
(
3
).
Suppose that
s_{0}
is a track-cusp on the side of
\ell
from which we wish to approach.
This exists by Lemma
5.0.26
.
See Figure
8.9
.

\hair

2pt

s_{2}

\pinlabel
