\pinlabel
\partial S^{\prime}_{0}
at 47 2

\pinlabel
s_{0}
at 93 31

\pinlabel
\partial S_{0}
at 160 2

\pinlabel
\partial S^{\prime}_{1}
at 177 2

\pinlabel
s_{1}
at 160 31

\pinlabel
\partial S_{2}
at 226 2

\pinlabel
s_{3}
at 226 31

\endlabellist

Figure 8.9.
Approaching a leaf by a sequence of boundary leaves.

The track-cusp
s_{0}
is part of a branch line
S_{0}
.
As in the proof of Lemma
8.0.13
(
6
), the track-cusp
s_{0}
gives us a pair of asymptotic boundary train lines both ending at
\partial S_{0}
.
Let
\ell_{0}
be the one of these that separates
s_{0}
from
\ell
.
Note that
\ell_{0}
is not equal to
\ell
, either because
\lambda
is not a boundary leaf or because
\lambda
is a boundary leaf whose cusp is on the other side from
s_{0}
.
Since the train lines
\ell
and
\ell_{0}
both run past
s_{0}
, their intersection is non-empty.
By Lemma
8.0.13
(
6
) the intersection is a train interval.
Let
s_{1}
be the track-cusp at the end
\ell\cap\ell_{0}
pointing in the opposite direction to
s_{0}
.

Suppose that
k>0
.
By induction, we have the following:

•

a track-cusp
s_{k}
(in a branch line
S_{k}
) meeting
\ell
on the desired side and

•

a boundary train line
\ell_{k-1}
that passes through the switch of
s_{k}

such that

•

if
k
is even,
s_{k}
points in the same direction as
s_{0}
;
if
k
is odd,
s_{k}
points in the opposite direction,

•

the boundary train line
\ell_{k-1}
does not separate
s_{k}
from
\ell
, and

•

the boundary train line
\ell_{k-1}
is not asymptotic to
\partial S_{k}
.

For the induction step, let
\ell_{k}
be the boundary train line that ends at
\partial S_{k}
and that separates
s_{k}
from
\ell
.
By Lemma
8.0.13
(
1
), the new boundary train line separates
\ell_{k-1}
from
\ell
.
Thus we have
\ell\cap\ell_{k-1}\subset\ell\cap\ell_{k}
, and the latter is at least one branch of
\tau^{K}
longer than the former, near
s_{k}
.
Let
s_{k+1}
be the track-cusp at the end of the train interval
\ell\cap\ell_{k}
pointing in the opposite direction to
s_{k}
.
This completes the construction of
s_{k+1}
and
\ell_{k}
.

Since
s_{k+1}
points in the opposite direction to
s_{k}
, the first inductive property holds.
The second property holds by construction.
The third property holds by Lemma
8.0.13
(
6
).

Let
\lambda_{k}
be the leaf with the same endpoints as
\ell_{k}
.
Since the train intervals
\ell\cap\ell_{k}
grow in both directions, and end on the midpoints of edges of the Farey triangulation of
K
, by Remark
7.0.11
the leaves
\lambda_{k}
converge to
\lambda
.

We finally claim that a boundary leaf
\lambda(S,T)
cannot be approached from the side containing its cusp
c
.
This is because, by Lemma
8.0.5
, any other boundary leaf
\lambda^{\prime}
is contained in one of the three components of
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})-\{\partial S,c,\partial T\}
.

8.10.
Cantor cross-section

We now discuss the transverse structure of the upper lamination;
similar properties hold for the lower.

Definition 8.0.18
.

Fix distinct cusps
c\neq d
.
Let
\Lambda^{(c,d)}
be the leaves of
\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}
that separate
c
from
d
.
We equip
\Lambda^{(c,d)}
with the subspace topology.
We define a total order on
\Lambda^{(c,d)}
by taking
\lambda<^{(c,d)}\lambda^{\prime}
if the endpoints of
\lambda
separate
c
from some endpoint of
\lambda^{\prime}
.
See Figure
8.11
.
Thus
\lambda^{\prime}
separates some endpoint of
\lambda
from
d
.

Note that by Lemma
8.0.13
(
1
), the subspace topology on
\Lambda^{(c,d)}
agrees with the order topology coming from
<^{(c,d)}
.

\labellist
\hair

2pt

\pinlabel
c
[t] at 199 0

\pinlabel
\partial S^{\prime}
[tl] at 286 24

\pinlabel
\partial T
[bl] at 299 372

\pinlabel
d
[b] at 199 400

\pinlabel
\partial T^{\prime}
[br] at 132 384

\pinlabel
\partial S
[tr] at 59 61

\endlabellist

Figure 8.11.
Some of the boundary leaves (solid) in
\Lambda^{(c,d)}
. The corresponding cusp leaves are dotted.

Recall that
[x,y]^{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\curvearrowleft$}}}
is the closed arc in
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
anticlockwise of
x
and clockwise of
y
.
Let
\mathcal{C}\subset[0,1]
be the middle-thirds Cantor set equipped with its usual topology and total order.

Lemma 8.0.19
.

The subspace
\Lambda^{(c,d)}
is order isomorphic (and thus homeomorphic) to the Cantor set
\mathcal{C}
.
Thus interior leaves are dense in
\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}
.

Proof 8.0.20
.

By Lemma
7.0.14
(
1
) there is exactly one boundary leaf
\lambda(S,S^{\prime})
associated to
c
that lies in
\Lambda^{(c,d)}
.
Similarly, there is exactly one boundary leaf
\lambda(T,T^{\prime})
associated to
d
that lies in
\Lambda^{(c,d)}
.
These then are the minimum and maximum points of the order
<^{(c,d)}
.
Breaking the symmetry of the situation we assume that the order of these points in
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
is

c,\partial S^{\prime},\partial T,d,\partial T^{\prime},\partial S

See Figure
8.11
.

We deduce that any leaf of
\Lambda^{(c,d)}
has one endpoint in
[\partial S^{\prime},\partial T]^{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\curvearrowleft$}}}
and the other in
[\partial T^{\prime},\partial S]^{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\curvearrowleft$}}}
.
Thus
\Lambda^{(c,d)}
is closed in the Möbius band past infinity
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{V})
.
Also,
\Lambda^{(c,d)}
is contained in the quotient of
[\partial S^{\prime},\partial T]^{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\curvearrowleft$}}}\times[\partial T^{\prime},\partial S]^{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\curvearrowleft$}}}
lying in
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{V})
.
Thus
\Lambda^{(c,d)}
is compact.

We now recursively construct ternary codes for the leaves of
\Lambda^{(c,d)}
.
By Lemma
8.0.16
there is a pair of asymptotic upper boundary leaves
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
in
\Lambda^{(c,d)}
.
In fact there are countably many such (because there are countably many boundary leaves).
Choose an ordering of these pairs
((\lambda_{n},\lambda^{\prime}_{n})\mid n\in\mathbb{N})
.
Break symmetry and assume that
\lambda_{n}<^{(c,d)}\lambda^{\prime}_{n}
.

We now recursively build various clopen subintervals
I_{\omega}
of
\Lambda^{(c,d)}
where
\omega
ranges over finite ternary words.
Set
I_{\epsilon}=\Lambda^{(c,d)}
;
here
\epsilon
is the empty word.
At stage
n
, we have that
(\lambda_{n},\lambda^{\prime}_{n})
lies in the interior of some
I_{\omega}
previously produced.

We now define

I_{\omega 0}=\{\mu\in\Lambda^{(c,d)}\mathbin{\mid}\mu\leq^{(c,d)}\lambda_{n}\}\\
\quad\mbox{and}\quad I_{\omega 2}=\{\mu\in\Lambda^{(c,d)}\mathbin{\mid}\lambda^{\prime}_{n}\leq^{(c,d)}\mu\}

Each is clopen (and compact).

Now, for any leaf
\lambda\in\Lambda^{(c,d)}
we send it to the real number whose ternary expansion agrees with the subscripts of the nested sets
I_{\omega}
containing
\lambda
.
Note that
\lambda(S,S^{\prime})
and
\lambda(T^{\prime},T)
are sent to
\bar{0}
and
\bar{2}
(zero repeating and two repeating) respectively.
Similarly, the asymptotic pair of boundary leaves splitting
I_{\omega}
receive the codes
\omega\bar{0}
and
\omega\bar{2}
.
Conversely, for any ternary expansion
\omega
let
\omega[{:}n]
be the prefix of length
n
.
Then the nested intersection
\bigcap_{n}I_{\omega[{:}n]}
is a singleton by Lemma
8.0.16
and the fact that we eventually split along each pair of boundary leaves
(\lambda,\lambda^{\prime})
.
Thus the coding is a continuous order preserving injection, homeomorphic onto its image.

Since interior points are dense in the Cantor set
\mathcal{C}
we deduce that interior leaves are dense in
\Lambda^{(c,d)}
.
We deduce interior leaves are dense in
\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}
from this and Lemma
8.0.16
.

8.12.
Suspending and descending

Suppose that
e
is an edge of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
.
Suppose that
c
and
d
are the cusps meeting
e
.
Following Definition
8.0.18
, we define
\Lambda^{e}=\Lambda^{(c,d)}
to be the leaves of
\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}
that link
e
.
By Lemma
8.0.19
, the subspace
\Lambda^{e}
is homeomorphic to a Cantor set.

Now, for every edge
e
of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
, we place a copy
\mathcal{C}^{e}
of
\Lambda^{e}
along
e
.
We arrange matters so that if
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(M)
then
\gamma(\mathcal{C}^{e})=\mathcal{C}^{\gamma(e)}
.
Suppose that
f
is a face of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
with edges
e_{0}
,
e_{1}
, and
e_{2}
.
Breaking symmetry, suppose that
\Lambda^{e_{0}}=\Lambda^{e_{1}}\cup\Lambda^{e_{2}}
.
We connect corresponding points of
\mathcal{C}^{e_{0}}
to corresponding points of
\mathcal{C}^{e_{1}}
or
\mathcal{C}^{e_{2}}
using disjoint normal arcs
\mathcal{C}^{f}
in
f
.
We again arrange matters so that the arcs of
\mathcal{C}^{f}
are sent to the arcs of
\mathcal{C}^{\gamma(f)}
by an element
\gamma
of
\pi_{1}(M)
.
Finally, inside of every tetrahedron
t\in{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
, we choose a collection of normal disks
\mathcal{C}^{t}
spanning the normal curves on the boundary of
t
.
To prove that only normal disks are needed to span the curves in
\partial t
, we fix a layering, consider the layers
K
and
K^{\prime}
immediately below and above
t
, and apply Lemma
8.0.13
(
3
).
See Figure
6.2a
.
Again we do this equivariantly.
Let
{\widetilde{\Sigma}}^{\mathcal{V}}
be the union
\bigcup_{t}\mathcal{C}^{t}
.

Proof 8.0.21

(Proof of Theorem
8.1
(
2
))
.

The lamination
{\widetilde{\Sigma}}^{\mathcal{V}}
consists of planes.
Also,
{\widetilde{\Sigma}}^{\mathcal{V}}
is
\pi_{1}(M)
–invariant.
Thus it descends to give a
\pi_{1}
–injective lamination
\Sigma^{\mathcal{V}}
in
M
, carried by
B^{\mathcal{V}}
.
By Lemma
8.0.13
(
4
), this lamination is fully carried.
This proves Theorem
8.1
(
2a
).

Suppose that
\sigma
is leaf of
\Sigma^{\mathcal{V}}
.
Thus
\sigma
has a cellulation as a union of sectors;
see Sections
6.6
and
6.8
and the figures therein.
Suppose that
\sigma
is closed.
By the above
\sigma
has Euler characteristic zero, so it is a torus or Klein bottle.
We must now derive a contradiction.

We construct a smooth
upwards loop
in
\sigma
;
that is, a loop
\gamma
which exits each sector
s
it meets through the upper vertex of
s
.
By
[
53
, Theorem 3.2]
, the upwards loop
\gamma
is essential.
Thus
\sigma-\gamma
is an annulus or Möbius band.
Let
(P_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}
be the set of elevations of
\sigma-\gamma
to
{\widetilde{\sigma}}
.
Each of these is homeomorphic to
(0,1)\times\mathbb{R}
; the two boundary components are distinct elevations of
\gamma
.
Let
\mathcal{K}=(K_{j})
be a layering of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
.
For all
i
and
j
, define
\alpha(i,j)=P_{i}\cap K_{j}
.
Note that
\alpha(i,j)
is a properly embedded arc in
P_{i}
.
Also
\alpha(i,j)
and
\alpha(i,j+1)
cobound a rectangle in
P_{i}
.
Each
\alpha(i,j)
is carried by a smooth subarc of the one-skeleton of
\sigma
.
Thus each
\alpha(i,j)
receives a
normal length

\ell(i,j)
.
From Figure
6.7
we observe that
\ell(i,j+1)\leq\ell(i,j)
, with equality if and only if there are no normal triangles between
\alpha(i,j)
and
\alpha(i,j+1)
.
Since the fundamental group of
\sigma
acts cocompactly on
{\widetilde{\sigma}}
, we deduce that
\ell(i,j+1)=\ell(i,j)
for all
i
and
j
.
Thus there are no normal triangles in
{\widetilde{\sigma}}
.
We now reach a contradiction as in Corollary
6.0.9
;
let
Q
be a diagonal strip of quadrilaterals in
{\widetilde{\sigma}}
.
All quadrilaterals of
Q
are contained in tetrahedra meeting a single edge of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
.
This contradicts the finiteness of edge degrees.
We deduce that
\Sigma^{\mathcal{V}}
has no closed leaves.

Remark 8.0.22
.

When
\mathcal{V}
is finite, it admits a strict angle structure by
[
28
, Theorem 1.5]
or by
[
17
, Theorem 1.4]
.
So, in the finite case, this gives an alternate proof that there are no closed surfaces carried by
B^{\mathcal{V}}
.

We now show that
\sigma
is either a plane, annulus, or Möbius band.
Again we use the decomposition of
\sigma
into sectors (Section
6.8
).
Suppose that
\gamma=(\gamma_{i})_{i=0}^{K-1}
is an essential simple closed multicurve in the leaf
\sigma
.
Isotope
\gamma
inside of
\sigma
to be transverse to
\sigma^{(1)}
, the one-skeleton of
\sigma
.
We define the
length
of
\gamma
to be the number of points of intersection between
\gamma
and
\sigma^{(1)}
.
So
\sigma^{(1)}
cuts
\gamma
into a collection of arcs in each sector:
these are
maxima
,
minima
,
diagonals
, and
verticals
.
The maxima and minima are of two types: they either separate the vertices of the sector, or they do not.
We call the former
separating
and the latter
non-separating
.
See Figure
8.13
.

\labellist
\hair

2pt

\pinlabel
maxima [r] at 115 46

\pinlabel
diagonals [t] at 405 49

\pinlabel
verticals [b] at 115 272

\pinlabel

separating

minimum

[b] at 240 270

\pinlabel

minimum

[b] at 400 216

\endlabellist

(
a
)

(
b
)

Figure 8.13.
The various possible subarcs of
\gamma
meeting a fixed sector, drawn in the two styles of Figure
6.7
.
Verticals separate one cusp vertex from the upper and lower vertices of the normal quadrilateral.
Diagonals separate the upper vertex of the normal quadrilateral and one of the cusp vertices from the lower vertex of the normal quadrilateral and the other cusp vertex.
Maxima and minima do not separate the cusp vertices.

(
a
)

(
b
)

(
c
)

(
d
)

Figure 8.14.
Examples of arcs
\delta
and the corresponding ambient isotopies.
In each case the arc
\delta\subset\gamma_{k}
is drawn in green.
The result of the ambient isotopy is drawn with a dashed line.

Claim
.

The multicurve
\gamma=(\gamma_{i})_{i=0}^{K-1}
can be isotoped to have no minima.

Proof 8.0.23
.

Fix
k\geq 0
.
We assume that, for
i<k
, the curves
\gamma_{i}
have no minima.
We now perform a sequence of ambient isotopies of
\gamma
that fix the
\gamma_{i}
(for
i<k
) pointwise while reducing the following lexicographic complexity of
\gamma_{k}
:

(\mbox{number of minima of $\gamma_{k}$, length of $\gamma_{k}$})

If
\gamma_{k}
has no minima then we are done with
\gamma_{k}
.

The ambient isotopies of
\gamma
give
bigon moves
of
\gamma_{k}
that reduce complexity.
A bigon move is applied to a (perhaps long) subarc
\delta
of
\gamma_{k}
.

The simplest case is when
\delta
is a non-separating maximum or minimum.
We push
\delta
across a subarc of
\sigma^{(1)}
;
this does not increase the number of minima and reduces the length by two.
See Figure
8.14a
.

When we cannot apply the previous move, we take
\delta
to be an arc

•

starting with a separating minimum,

•

followed by a (possibly empty) sequence of diagonals fellow-travelling a smooth arc
\epsilon
in
\sigma^{(1)}
, and

•

ending either when
\epsilon
ends in a T-junction (Figure
8.14b
) or when
\delta
ends

–

with a vertical (Figure
8.14c
) or

–

immediately before a separating maximum (Figure
8.14d
).

For the subcases shown in Figures
8.14b
and
8.14c
we push
\delta
across
\epsilon
;
this does not increase the number of minima and reduces the length.
After applying all moves of the above types, we either have no minima in
\gamma_{k}
(and are done) or the only subarcs
\delta
are of the type shown in Figure
8.14d
(and thus
\gamma_{k}
has no verticals).
In particular, since
\epsilon
ends at an X-junction, the separating maximum does not separate the end of
\epsilon
from the cusp vertices of its sector.
(Because we are not in the case where
\epsilon
ends in a T-junction.)

All remaining cases lead to contradiction, as follows.
Pick one minimum and consider its two subarcs (heading in opposite directions),
\delta
and
\delta^{\prime}
.
First, assume that
\delta
and
\delta^{\prime}
do not end in the same maximum.
In this case, we repeatedly applying the bigon move shown in Figure
8.14d
while keeping
\gamma_{k}
fixed outside of a neighbourhood of
\delta\cup\delta^{\prime}
.
This either reduces the length or reduces the number of minima.
Finally, suppose that
\delta
and
\delta^{\prime}
end in the same maximum.
In this case, by repeatedly applying the bigon move shown in Figure
8.14d
, we either reduce the length, or find an infinite or periodic strip of quadrilaterals.
This gives a contradiction, again as in Corollary
6.0.9
.

Recall that
\sigma
is not closed.
If
\sigma
is not a plane, annulus, or Möbius band then we may choose a multicurve
\gamma
which cuts out of
\sigma
a compact subsurface
\sigma^{\prime}
of negative Euler characteristic.
Applying the claim immediately above, we may assume that
\gamma
has no minima and thus no maxima.
Thus
\gamma
is a union of diagonals and verticals.
Consider a component
s^{\prime}
of
\sigma^{\prime}-\sigma^{(1)}
, with
s^{\prime}
contained in the sector
s
.
Thus
s^{\prime}
is a component of
s-\gamma
.
Consulting Figure
8.13a
, we deduce that
s^{\prime}
is either

•

a
cusped bigon
(all of
s
),

•

a
boundary trigon
(contains a cusp vertex), or

•

a
rectangle
(contains no cusp vertices).

See
[
53
, Table 1]
.
Thus
\sigma^{\prime}
has Euler characteristic zero.
This is a contradiction.
This proves Theorem
8.1
(
2b
).

Finally, Lemma
6.0.6
(
5
) implies all complementary regions to
\Sigma^{\mathcal{V}}
are torus (or annulus, or plane) crossed with a ray, with paring locus on the inner boundary.
Thus
\Sigma^{\mathcal{V}}
is an
essential lamination
.
(See
[
7
, Definition 6.14]
.)
This completes the proof of Theorem
8.1
(
2c
).

8.15.
Uniqueness

To simplify the combinatorics, here we relax the transversality assumption on our paths.
That is, suppose that
\gamma\subset B^{\mathcal{V}}
is an oriented path.
In this section and the next we say that
\gamma
is an
ascending path
if

•

\gamma
enters every sector through a lower edge or a lower vertex and

•

\gamma
exits every sector through an upper edge or its upper vertex.

An ascending path
\gamma
is an
upwards path
if
\gamma
exits every sector through its upper vertex.

Remark 8.0.24
.

Suppose that
\sigma
is a leaf carried by the upper branched surface
B^{\mathcal{V}}
.
Suppose that
\gamma
is an ascending path that ends in
\sigma
.
Then after a small isotopy,
\gamma
is completely contained in
\sigma
.

Definition 8.0.25
.

Fix
M
a three-manifold and
B\subset M
a branched surface.
Let
N=N(B)
be a tie-neighbourhood of
B
.
A
tie-isotopy
is an ambient isotopy of
M
supported in
N
and which preserves the ties of
N
.

Suppose that
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
are laminations carried by
B
.
We say that leaves
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
of
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
are
parallel
if they are tie-isotopic.

Note that, by construction,
\Sigma^{\mathcal{V}}
has no (distinct) parallel leaves.

Lemma 8.0.26
.

Suppose that
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
are laminations fully carried by
B^{\mathcal{V}}
.
Then for any leaf
\sigma
of
\Sigma
there is a leaf
\sigma^{\prime}
of
\Sigma^{\prime}
so that
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
are parallel.

Proof 8.0.27
.

We first assume that
M\mathrel{\cong}\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
Fix
K
a layer of a layering of
\mathcal{V}
.
Let
\ell=\sigma\cap K
.
Fix a sector
E
of
B^{\mathcal{V}}
which meets
\ell
.

We have the following claim.

Claim
.

For every
n>0
there is a leaf
\sigma^{\prime}_{n}
of
\Sigma^{\prime}
so that

•

\sigma^{\prime}_{n}
crosses
E
and

•

the train lines
\ell
and
\ell^{\prime}_{n}=\sigma^{\prime}_{n}\cap K
fellow travel for at least
n
triangles of
K
, to either side of
E\cap K
.

Proof 8.0.28

(Proof of Claim)
.

Let
\gamma_{n}\subset\sigma
be the unique (up to isotopy) upwards path which begins in
E
and which crosses
n
sectors.
See Figure
6.10
.
Let
E_{n}
be the final sector that
\gamma_{n}
meets.

Since
\Sigma^{\prime}
is fully carried, there is a leaf
\sigma^{\prime}_{n}
of
\Sigma^{\prime}
crossing
E_{n}
.
By Remark
8.0.24
applied to
\gamma_{n}
, the leaf
\sigma^{\prime}_{n}
also crosses
E
.

By Lemma
6.0.6
(
1
), all branch lines meet
K
.
This includes the branch lines
(S_{i})
that meet
\gamma_{n}
.
Let
s_{i}
be the track-cusp of
S_{i}
contained in
K
.
Since
S_{i}
is a branch line, the lower component of
S_{i}-\gamma_{n}
is (after a small isotopy) contained in
\sigma
.
Equally well (after a different small isotopy)
S_{i}-\gamma_{n}
is contained in
\sigma^{\prime}_{n}
.
Since the lower component of
S_{i}-\gamma_{n}
contains
s_{i}
we deduce that
\ell=\sigma\cap K
and
\ell^{\prime}_{n}=\sigma^{\prime}_{n}\cap K
both cross the edge of
K
meeting
s_{i}
.
Since all of the
s_{i}
are distinct, and since there are
n
of them to each side of
E
, the claim follows.

Since
\Sigma^{\prime}
is a lamination, and since all of the
\sigma^{\prime}_{n}
meet
E
, we pass to a convergent subsequence of the leaves
\sigma^{\prime}_{n}
(in the Gromov-Hausdorff topology).
Thus
\Sigma^{\prime}
contains the limiting leaf
\sigma^{\prime}
.
Thus
\ell^{\prime}=\sigma^{\prime}\cap K
has the same endpoints in
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
as
\ell
.
This holds for all layers of the layering.
We deduce that
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
are tie-isotopic in the tie-neighbourhood
N(B^{\mathcal{V}})
, as desired.

If
M
is not homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
then we lift to
{\widetilde{M}}
and
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
.
By Lemma
4.0.21
we have that
{\widetilde{M}}
is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
The preimages of the laminations
\Sigma
and
\Sigma^{\prime}
are again fully carried by the preimage of
B^{\mathcal{V}}
.
So we apply the above argument and note that the tie-isotopy may be chosen to be equivariant, by inducting on the skeleta of
B^{\mathcal{V}}
.

Definition 8.0.29
.

Fix
M
a three-manifold and
B\subset M
a branched surface.
Suppose that
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
are distinct parallel leaves of a lamination
\Sigma
carried by
B
.
Then we may delete all leaves between
\sigma
and
\sigma^{\prime}
and tie-isotope
\sigma^{\prime}
to
\sigma
.
This tie-isotopy extends to give a new lamination
\Sigma^{\prime}
.
We say that
\Sigma^{\prime}
is obtained by
collapsing
the given parallel leaves.

Proof 8.0.30

(Proof of Theorem
8.1
(
3
))
.

Suppose that
\Sigma
is a lamination fully carried by
B^{\mathcal{V}}
.
From Lemma
8.0.26
we deduce that every leaf of
\Sigma
is tie-isotopic to some leaf of
\Sigma^{\mathcal{V}}
.
However distinct leaves of
\Sigma
may be parallel;
if so, this correspondence will not be one-to-one.
So we collapse maximal collections of parallel leaves in
\Sigma
.
Now the leaves of
\Sigma
are naturally in bijection with the leaves of
\Sigma^{\mathcal{V}}
.
Pick any order on the countably many boundary leaves of
\Sigma
.
Recalling that tie-isotopies are ambient, we tie-isotope boundary leaves of
\Sigma
, in order, to the corresponding boundary leaves of
\Sigma^{\mathcal{V}}
.
This completes the proof of Theorem
8.1
(
3
).

8.16.
An application

In the context of pseudo-Anosov homeomorphisms, Theorem
8.1
(
3
) gives a strong uniqueness result.
Before proving this, we require the following.

Lemma 8.0.31
.

Suppose that
M
is a three-manifold equipped with a finite transverse veering triangulation
\mathcal{V}
.
Suppose that
D
and
D^{\prime}
are sectors of
B^{\mathcal{V}}
.
Then there is an ascending path
\gamma\subset B^{\mathcal{V}}
that starts in
D
and ends in
D^{\prime}
.

Proof 8.0.32
.

The branch locus of
B^{\mathcal{V}}
, in dual position, is a four-valent graph with a cyclically ordered edge-ends at the vertices.
Since
\mathcal{V}
is finite, the branch locus of
B^{\mathcal{V}}
decomposes as a union of smooth circles;
each circle is an image of some branch line under the universal covering map.

By Remark
6.0.2
, there is some oriented path
\gamma
in
B^{\mathcal{V}}
that starts and ends in the interiors of
D
and
D^{\prime}
respectively.
Homotope an initial segment of
\gamma
, staying in
B^{\mathcal{V}}
, so that it initially exits
D
through its uppermost vertex
v
.
Similarly, we may make
\gamma
enter
D^{\prime}
through its lowest vertex
v^{\prime}
.
Let
\beta
be the subpath of
\gamma
between
v
and
v^{\prime}
.
Since the branch locus is isotopic to the dual one-skeleton (again by Remark
6.0.2
) we may homotope
\beta
, relative to its endpoints, into the branch locus.
Tighten
\beta
so that it is locally an embedding.

Consider a maximal arc
\beta^{\prime}
of
\beta
which is contained in a smooth circle
C
of the branch locus, and whose orientation disagrees with the given co-orientation.
We call
\beta^{\prime}
a
descending
arc.
We replace
\beta^{\prime}
by its (ascending) complement in
C
.
We now alternate between tightening and replacing descending arcs with ascending arcs, until all arcs are ascending.
This produces the desired ascending path.

Corollary 8.0.33
.

Suppose that
M
is a three-manifold equipped with a finite transverse veering triangulation
\mathcal{V}
.
Suppose that
\Sigma
is a non-empty lamination carried by
B^{\mathcal{V}}
.
Then
\Sigma
is fully carried.

Proof 8.0.34
.

Let
\sigma
be any leaf of
\Sigma
.
Let
D^{\prime}
be any sector crossed by
\sigma
.
Let
D
be any other sector of
B^{\mathcal{V}}
.
By Lemma
8.0.31
there is an ascending path
\gamma
in
B^{\mathcal{V}}
connecting
D
to
D^{\prime}
.
From Remark
8.0.24
we deduce that
\sigma
crosses
D
.
Thus
\Sigma
is fully carried.

Corollary 8.0.35
.

Suppose that
S
is a compact, connected, oriented surface.
Suppose that
f\colon S\to S
is a pseudo-Anosov homeomorphism.
Let
B^{f}
be the stable branched surface in the mapping torus
M_{f}
.
Let
\Sigma^{f}
be the suspension of the stable lamination for
f
.
Then any non-empty lamination
\Sigma
carried by
B^{f}
is tie-isotopic to
\Sigma^{f}
, after collapsing parallel leaves.

Proof 8.0.36
.

We remove the boundary of
M_{f}
and drill out the singular orbits of the suspension flow
\Phi_{f}
.
By
[
1
, Main Construction]
the resulting manifold
M
admits a veering triangulation
\mathcal{V}
;
furthermore,
B^{f}
is isotopic to
B^{\mathcal{V}}
and
\Sigma^{f}
is isotopic to
\Sigma^{\mathcal{V}}
.

Suppose that
\Sigma
is a non-empty lamination carried by
B^{f}
.
By Corollary
8.0.33
we have that
\Sigma
is fully carried.
By Theorem
8.1
(
3
) we have that, after collapsing parallel leaves,
\Sigma
is tie-isotopic to
\Sigma^{f}
.

Chapter 9
Interaction between the upper and lower laminations

With the upper and lower laminations in hand we may explore their interactions.

9.1.
No shared endpoints

As usual we fix
M
a three-manifold with a transverse veering triangulation
\mathcal{V}
.
We take
{\widetilde{M}}
to be the universal cover;
we take
{\widetilde{B}}
to be the preimage, in
{\widetilde{M}}
, of the horizontal branched surface
B(\mathcal{V})
.
If
L\subset{\widetilde{M}}
is a landscape carried by
{\widetilde{B}}

then we take
\tau^{L}
and
\tau_{L}
to be the induced upper and lower tracks.
Finally we have
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
the veering circle and
\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}
and
\Lambda_{\mathcal{V}}
the upper and lower laminations.

Lemma 9.0.1
.

No leaf of
\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}
is asymptotic to a leaf of
\Lambda_{\mathcal{V}}
.

Proof 9.0.2
.

For a contradiction, suppose that
\lambda=\lambda(x,y)
and
\mu=\lambda(y,z)
are leaves of
\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}
and
\Lambda_{\mathcal{V}}
respectively, which share a common endpoint
y\in S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
.

Fix
K
a layer of some layering. Let
\tau^{K}
and
\tau_{K}
be the upper and lower train tracks in
K
. Lemma
8.0.13
(
3
) gives us train lines
\ell
in
\tau^{K}
and
m
in
\tau_{K}
that have the same endpoints as
\lambda
and
\mu
, respectively. We orient
\ell
and
m
towards
y
. Since
\partial_{+}\ell=y=\partial_{+}m
, and applying Lemma
8.0.13
(
5
), there are subrays of
\ell
and
m
that cross an identical collection of edges in the triangulation of
K
. This collection of edges determines an infinite strip
P\subset K
of triangles. There are only two kinds of veering triangle, both shown in Figure
5.4
; from this we deduce that the internal edges (and the initial edge) of
P
are all the same colour. The boundary edges (other than the initial edge) are the opposite colour. Breaking symmetry, we assume that the interior edges of
P
are red. For an example, see Figure
9.2a
.

(
a
)
A strip of triangles
P_{0}
with blue boundary edges and red interior edges. This locally contains the fellow travelling upper and lower train lines.

(
b
)
Layering a fan tetrahedron on top of
P_{i}
.

(
c
)
Layering a toggle tetrahedron on top of
P_{i}
sends upper (green) train routes out of the strip.

Figure 9.2.
Layering a tetrahedron on top of a strip of triangles. The altered triangles are shaded in light grey. Where the upper and lower tracks coincide we draw a thicker grey arc.

Claim
.

Any in-fill tetrahedron
t
attached above
P
, to an interior edge of
P
, is a fan tetrahedron.

Proof 9.0.3
.

Let
K^{\prime}
be the landscape obtained from
K
by removing the bottom two triangles of
t
from
K
and replacing it with the top two triangles of
t
. Note that
K^{\prime}
is again a layer of some layering. We define
P^{\prime}\subset K^{\prime}
similarly. Applying Lemma
8.0.13
(
3
), the leaf
\lambda
determines a train line
\ell^{\prime}
in the track
\tau^{K^{\prime}}
.

Suppose that
t
is a toggle.
Thus
P^{\prime}
has an internal edge which is blue.
Since
\ell^{\prime}
cannot cross this edge, we have reached a contradiction.
See Figure
9.2c
.

Suppose that
t
is an in-fill fan tetrahedron attached above
P
, to an interior edge of
P
.
The resulting strip
P^{\prime}
again has red interior edges.
Also,
\tau^{P^{\prime}}
still carries
\ell^{\prime}
.
See Figure
9.2b
.

By Lemma
5.0.26
the rays
\ell\cap P
and
m\cap P
both turn right and left infinitely often. Thus there is an upper track-cusp
s
meeting an interior edge of
P
so that
s
points in the same direction as
\ell
and
m
. By the claim above, the branch line
S
passing through
s
meets only fan tetrahedra. This contradicts Corollary
6.0.9
.

9.3.
Crowns

Definition 9.0.4
.

We define
\Lambda^{c}\subset\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}
, the
upper crown
of the cusp
c
, to be the set of leaves
\lambda(S,T)
where here
S
and
T
range over all adjacent upper branch lines in the upper cusp neighbourhood
N^{c}
.
We define the
lower crown

\Lambda_{c}
similarly.
The endpoints of the boundary leaves of a crown are called its
tips
.

2pt

Figure 9.4.
Two upper crowns
\Lambda^{c}
and
\Lambda^{d}
.
The solid lines are boundary leaves and the dotted lines are cusp leaves.
Compare with Figure
8.11
.

By Lemma
6.0.6
(
2
) the boundary leaves of an upper crown
\Lambda^{c}
are adjacent in pairs and ordered by
\mathbb{Z}
.
By Lemma
7.0.14
(
1
) the tips of
\Lambda^{c}
accumulate (only) on
c
.
See Figure
9.4
.

Definition 9.0.5
.

Suppose that
\Lambda^{c}
and
\Lambda_{d}
are upper and lower crowns.

•

They are
unlinked
if no leaf of
\Lambda^{c}
links any leaf of
\Lambda_{d}
.

•

They
cross
if there are leaves
\lambda(R,S)
and
\lambda(S,T)
in
\Lambda^{c}
and leaves
\lambda(U,V)
and
\lambda(V,W)
in
\Lambda_{d}
so that these leaves of the crowns link and no others do.
See Figure
9.5a
.

•

They
interleave
if
c=d
and we may index the upper and lower branch lines
S_{i}
and
V_{i}
such that
\lambda(S_{i},S_{i+1})
links
\lambda(V_{i-1},V_{i})
and
\lambda(V_{i},V_{i+1})
.
See Figure
9.5b
.

(

)

(

)

Figure 9.5.
Crossing and interleaving crowns. We illustrate each leaf by drawing an arc through the unit disk joining the two points of the leaf. We indicate linking pairs of leaves with black or yellow dots as the pair comes from interleaving crowns or otherwise.

Lemma 9.0.6
.

The upper and lower crowns
\Lambda^{c}
and
\Lambda_{c}
interleave.

The analysis here is similar to, but more delicate than that in the proof of Lemma
6.0.6
(
3
).

Proof 9.0.7

(Proof of Lemma
9.0.6
)
.

Fix
K
a layer of a layering.
We walk in
K
anticlockwise about the cusp
c
.
As we do so, we pass a sequence
(e_{i})
of edges meeting
c
.
Let
f_{i}
be the triangle meeting
e_{i}
and
e_{i+1}
.
If
e_{i}
is red and
e_{i+1}
is blue then
f_{i}
contains an upper track-cusp pointing away from
c
.
If the colours are interchanged then
f_{i}
contains an lower track-cusp pointing away from
c
.
If
e_{i}
and
e_{i+1}
have the same colour then
f_{i}
does not contain a track-cusp pointing away from
c
.
See Figure
5.4
.
Thus the connecting arcs emanating from
c
alternate in the correct fashion.
By Lemma
4.0.2
the layer
K
is a copy of the Farey tessellation.
Thus we deduce that the cusp lines emanating from
c
also alternate.
Thus the upper and lower crowns interleave.
See Figure
9.5b
.

Before dealing with crossing crowns, we require the following.

Lemma 9.0.8
.

Suppose that
c
is a cusp and
R
and
S
are adjacent upper branch lines in
N^{c}
.
Suppose that
\mu
is a lower cusp leaf, a lower boundary leaf or a lower interior leaf.
Then
\mu
links at most one of the cusp leaves
\lambda(c,R)
and
\lambda(c,S)
.
The same statement holds swapping upper and lower.

Proof 9.0.9
.

Suppose for contradiction that
\mu
links both
\lambda(c,R)
and
\lambda(c,S)
.
Thus
\mu
is neither a cusp leaf based at
c
nor a boundary leaf of the crown
\Lambda_{c}
.
By Lemma
9.0.6
, there is a lower branch line
U
in
N_{c}
such that the cusp leaf
\lambda(c,U)
links the boundary leaf
\lambda(R,S)
.
We deduce that
\mu
also links the cusp leaf
\lambda(c,U)
.
If
\mu
is a cusp leaf then we have a contradiction to Lemma
8.0.5
.
Thus by Lemma
7.0.14
(
1
) the endpoints of
\mu
lie in distinct components of
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
minus the tips of the crown
\Lambda_{c}
.
Thus,
\mu
links exactly two boundary leaves of
\Lambda_{c}
.
This contradicts Lemma
8.0.13
(
1
).

(

)

Figure 9.6.
Crowns with distinct cusps that have linking boundary leaves must cross.

Lemma 9.0.10
.

Suppose that
c
and
d
are distinct cusps.
Then
\Lambda^{c}
and
\Lambda_{d}
are either unlinked or cross.

Recall that
[x,y]^{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\curvearrowleft$}}}
is the closed arc in
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
between
x
and
y
and anticlockwise of
x
.

Proof 9.0.11

(Proof of Lemma
9.0.10
)
.

By Corollary
7.0.10
, the cusps
c
and
d
are distinct from the tips of both crowns.
Also, by Lemma
9.0.1
, the tips of
\Lambda^{c}
are all distinct from the tips of
\Lambda_{d}
.

Suppose that there is a boundary leaf
\lambda(R,S)
of
\Lambda^{c}
that links some boundary leaf
\lambda(U,V)
of
\Lambda_{d}
.
Breaking symmetry, we assume that the points
\partial R,\partial U,\partial S,\partial V
lie on the circle in that anticlockwise order. See Figure
9.6a
. The cusp
c
is distinct from these points. Breaking symmetry we will assume that
c
lies in
[\partial R,\partial U]^{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\curvearrowleft$}}}
. See Figure
9.6b
. Let
T
be the branch line of
N^{c}
that is adjacent to
S
and not equal to
R
. Note that
\partial T
is on the opposite side of
\lambda(c,S)
from
\partial R
. Lemma
9.0.8
implies that
\partial T
cannot lie in
[\partial U,\partial S]^{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\curvearrowleft$}}}
. So
\partial T
lies in
[c,\partial U]^{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\curvearrowleft$}}}
. See Figure
9.6c
.

We now consider the location of the cusp
d
. Again by Lemma
9.0.8
the cusp
d
cannot lie in
[\partial R,\partial T]^{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\curvearrowleft$}}}
. Breaking symmetry, there are two cases. Either
d
lies in
[\partial T,\partial U]^{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\curvearrowleft$}}}
or
[\partial U,\partial S]^{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\curvearrowleft$}}}
.

Suppose that
d
lies in
[\partial T,\partial U]^{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\curvearrowleft$}}}
. See Figure
9.6d
. Let
W
be the branch line of
N_{d}
that is adjacent to
V
and not equal to
U
. Note that
\partial W
is on the opposite side of
\lambda(d,V)
from
\partial U
. Lemma
9.0.8
implies that
\partial W
cannot lie in
[\partial V,\partial T]^{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\curvearrowleft$}}}
. So
\partial W
lies in
[\partial T,d]^{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\curvearrowleft$}}}
.
See Figure
9.6e
.
Thus the crowns
\Lambda^{c}
and
\Lambda_{d}
cross, as desired.

The case where
d
instead lies in
[\partial U,\partial S]^{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\curvearrowleft$}}}
is easier; we omit it.

Chapter 10
The link space

We now give a direct construction of the
link space

\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
.
With this done, and after obtaining several important properties, we prove Theorem
10.18
.
That is, we show that link spaces are
loom spaces
, in the sense of
[
54
, Definition 2.11]
.

Remark 10.0.1
.

Here is one way to think of the link space construction, at least in the layered case.
The upper and lower laminations
\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}
and
\Lambda_{\mathcal{V}}
collapse to give a pair of dendrites.
The fundamental group of the fibre acts on their product.
Removing
\Delta_{\mathcal{V}}^{2}
and then taking the
Guirardel core

[
24
]
gives (a quotient of) the link space.

However, in the non-layered case the upper and lower laminations need not admit (projective) measures compatible with the action of
\pi_{1}(M)
or any obvious subgroup.
Thus the geometric steps of the construction must be replaced.
Also we do not have the same uniformity of the action.
Furthermore, we will need quite delicate topological control over
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
; in fact we will show it is a copy of the plane.
Also, we will require an in-depth combinatorial understanding of the
rectangles
in
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
in order to complete our programme of classifying pseudo-Anosov flows (without perfect fits).

Definition 10.0.2
.

Suppose that
M
is a three-manifold which is not necessarily orientable or compact.
Suppose that
\mathcal{T}
is a taut ideal triangulation of
M
.
Let
{\widetilde{T}}
be the induced taut ideal triangulation on the universal cover
{\widetilde{M}}
.
Fix an orientation on
{\widetilde{M}}
.
We say that
\mathcal{T}
is
locally veering
if
{\widetilde{T}}
has a transverse veering structure.

As an example, there is a locally veering triangulation of the Gieseking manifold with a single tetrahedron.
The double cover is the veering triangulation shown in Figure
3.2
.

Remark 10.0.3
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{V}
is a locally veering triangulation of a three-manifold
M
.
Since all of our constructions occur in the universal cover
{\widetilde{M}}
, we may fix an orientation of
{\widetilde{M}}
and a transverse veering structure on
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
.
We define
\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}
and
\Lambda_{\mathcal{V}}
with respect to these choices.
Making different choices may switch the laminations and may swap colours.

Definition 10.0.4
.

We define the
pair space
to be

\operatorname{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{V})=\big\{(\lambda,\mu)\in\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}\times\Lambda_{\mathcal{V}}\mathbin{\mid}\mbox{$\lambda$ and $\mu$ are linked}\big\}

The topology on
\operatorname{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{V})
is that of a subspace of a product.

We define an equivalence relation on points of
\operatorname{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{V})
as follows.
If
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
are asymptotic upper leaves and if
\mu
links both then
(\lambda,\mu)\sim(\lambda^{\prime},\mu)
.
We do the same for asymptotic lower leaves.
Finally we take the transitive closure.
Let
[(\lambda,\mu)]
be the equivalence class of
(\lambda,\mu)
.

Lemma 10.0.5
.

An equivalence class
[(\lambda,\mu)]
in
\operatorname{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{V})
has either one, two, four or infinitely many representatives.
These correspond exactly to the cases that:

•

the leaves
\lambda
and
\mu
are both interior,

•

one is interior and the other is boundary,

•

the leaves are boundary about different cusps, or

•

the leaves are boundary about a single cusp.

Proof 10.0.6
.

•

Suppose that
\lambda
and
\mu
are interior leaves. By Lemma
8.0.13
(
6
), the leaf
\lambda
has no asymptotic partner in
\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}
. The same holds for
\mu
.

•

Breaking symmetry, suppose that
\mu
is an interior leaf and
\lambda=\lambda(R,S)
is a boundary leaf. Let
c
be the cusp such that
N^{c}
contains
R
and
S
. Breaking symmetry again, the leaf
\mu
also links
\lambda(c,S)
. Let
T
be the other branch line adjacent to
S
. Applying Lemma
9.0.8
we have

[(\lambda,\mu)]=\{(\lambda(R,S),\mu),(\lambda(S,T),\mu)\}

•

Suppose that both
\lambda
and
\mu
are boundary leaves, adjacent to distinct cusps
c
and
d
. Thus by Lemma
9.0.10
, the crowns
\Lambda^{c}
and
\Lambda_{d}
cross, and
[(\lambda,\mu)]
consists of four pairs. See Figure
9.5a
.

•

Suppose that both
\lambda
and
\mu
are boundary leaves, both adjacent to the cusp
c
. Thus by Lemma
9.0.6
, the crowns
\Lambda^{c}
and
\Lambda_{c}
interleave, and
[(\lambda,\mu)]
consists of a countable collection of pairs. See Figure
9.5b
.

We call the last type of equivalence class a
cusp class
.

Definition 10.0.7
.

The
link space

\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
is the quotient of
\operatorname{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{V})
, minus the cusp classes. That is:

\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})\,\,=\,\,\operatorname{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{V})/{\sim}\,\,-\,\,\{\mbox{cusp classes}\}

We give
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
a topology by realising it as a subspace of a quotient.

Definition 10.0.8
.

We now define the
upper foliation

F^{\mathcal{V}}
of
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
.
It consists of two kinds of leaves. Suppose that
\lambda\in\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}
is an interior leaf. Then we take

\ell^{\lambda}=\big\{[(\lambda,\mu)]\in\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})\mbox{ for some $\mu\in\Lambda_{\mathcal{V}}$}\big\}

This is a
non-cusp leaf
of
F^{\mathcal{V}}
.

Suppose instead that
c
is a cusp and
S
is an upper branch line in
N^{c}
.
Let
\lambda(c,S)
be the corresponding upper cusp leaf.
By Lemma
6.0.6
(
2
), there are exactly two upper branch lines
S^{\prime}
and
S^{\prime\prime}
(in the boundary of
N^{c}
) that are adjacent to
S
.
Take
\lambda
to be one of
\lambda(S^{\prime},S)
or
\lambda(S,S^{\prime\prime})
.
Then we define

\ell^{S}=\big\{[(\lambda,\mu)]\in\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})\mathbin{\mid}\mbox{$\mu\in\Lambda_{\mathcal{V}}$ links $\lambda(c,S)$}\big\}

This is a
cusp leaf
of
F^{\mathcal{V}}
.
(Note that applying Lemma
9.0.8
twice shows that
\ell^{S}
does not depend on whether we chose
\lambda(S^{\prime},S)
or
\lambda(S,S^{\prime\prime})
above.)

We define the lower foliation
F_{\mathcal{V}}
similarly.

Theorem 10.0.8
.

Suppose that
M
is a three-manifold equipped with a locally veering triangulation
\mathcal{V}
.
Then we have the following.

(1)

The link space
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
is homeomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

(2)

F^{\mathcal{V}}
and
F_{\mathcal{V}}
are transverse foliations of
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
.

(3)

Non-cusp leaves are dense in each of
F^{\mathcal{V}}
and
F_{\mathcal{V}}
.

(4)

The induced action of
\pi_{1}(M)
on
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
is continuous and faithful.

Suppose additionally that
M
is orientable and
\mathcal{V}
is transverse veering.
Then we have the following.

(5)

There is a canonical orientation on
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
.

(6)

The induced action of
\pi_{1}(M)
on
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
is orientation preserving.

Before proving the theorem, we develop some structure.
If
M
is orientable, and
\mathcal{V}
is transverse veering, then there are induced orientations in the universal cover.
If not, then we appeal to Remark
10.0.3
to obtain such orientations in the universal cover.

Definition 10.0.9
.

A
rectangle

R
in
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
is an embedding of
(0,1)^{2}
into
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
that sends line segments parallel to the
y
–axis (
x
–axis) to arcs of the upper (lower) foliations.

Definition 10.0.10
.

Suppose that
R
is a rectangle in
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
.
Suppose that
(\ell_{i})
is a monotonic sequence of leaves of
R\cap F^{\mathcal{V}}
(or of
R\cap F_{\mathcal{V}}
), exiting
R
.
Then the set of accumulation points of
(\ell_{i})
is one of the four
sides
of
R
.

Let
{\overline{R}}
denote the closure of
R
in
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
.
As usual, we set
\partial R={\overline{R}}-{\operatorname{interior}}(R)={\overline{R}}-R
.
Note that the sides of a rectangle
R
lie in
\partial R
.
Note also that a side need not be connected.
Any point of
\partial R
lying in two sides is called a
material corner
of
R
.

Definition 10.0.11
.

Suppose that
R
is a rectangle in
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
.
Note that the closure of
R
, taken in
\operatorname{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{V})/{\sim}
, consists of
{\overline{R}}
, together with some number of cusp classes.
We say that such a cusp class
p
is an
ideal point
of
R
.
We say that
p
is in the
interior
of a side of
R
if it is accumulated by only that side.
We say that
p
is an
ideal corner
of
R
if it is accumulated by exactly two, adjacent, sides of
R
.

10.2.
Edge rectangles

We take up the task of building rectangles in
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
.

Definition 10.0.12
.

Fix an edge
e
of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
. Fix
(\lambda,\mu)\in\operatorname{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{V})
. We say that a pair
(\lambda,\mu)\in\operatorname{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{V})

links

e
if both
\lambda
and
\mu
link
e
. The
edge rectangle

\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
is defined to be

\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)=\{p\in\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})\mathbin{\mid}\mbox{every representative $(\lambda,\mu)$ of $p$ links $e$}\}

Lemma 10.0.13
.

Suppose that
e
is an edge of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
with endpoints at cusps
c
and
d
.

(1)

The edge rectangle
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
is a rectangle in the sense of Definition
10.0.9
.

(2)

The boundary of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
is contained in the union of four cusp leaves that alternatingly lie in
F^{\mathcal{V}}
and
F_{\mathcal{V}}
.
The closure of each side in
\operatorname{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{V})/{\sim}
is a closed interval connecting an ideal corner to a material corner.
The former are the cusp classes coming from
c
and
d
.

\hair

\pinlabel
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
at 289 36

Figure 10.3.
Left: a red edge
e
. Right: the edge rectangle
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
. The dotted, shrunken rectangle is intended to remind the reader that
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
does not include its boundary. We have coloured the material corners yellow and the ideal corners black.

Proof 10.0.14
.

Breaking symmetry, we suppose that
e
is coloured red. We orient
e
from
c
to
d
. Lemma
9.0.6
tells us that the crowns
\Lambda^{c}
and
\Lambda_{c}
interleave, as do the crowns
\Lambda^{d}
and
\Lambda_{d}
.

Claim
.

There are boundary leaves

\lambda(S,S^{\prime})\in\Lambda^{c},\quad\lambda(U,U^{\prime})\in\Lambda_{c},\quad\lambda(T,T^{\prime})\in\Lambda^{d},\quad\lambda(V,V^{\prime})\in\Lambda_{d}

so that each links the next, cyclically.

Proof 10.0.15
.

Fix a layer
K
of a layering, chosen so that
e
lies in
K
.
Let
P
be the maximal strip of majority red faces in
K
to the right of
e
.
See Figure
10.4
.
When
P
is finite, we add a single majority blue face
f^{\prime}
at the end of
P
.
In this case, let
b
be the cusp of
f^{\prime}
not meeting a red edge of
P
.

By construction, the boundary of
P
consists of one red edge,
e
, and the rest blue.
Recall that
\tau^{P}
and
\tau_{P}
are the upper and lower tracks in
P\subset K
.
Consider the blue edge
e^{\prime}\subset\partial P
adjacent to
c
.
Since
e
and all interior edges of
P
are red, the face of
P
meeting
e^{\prime}
contains a lower track-cusp
u
that points away from
c
.
Again see Figure
10.4
.
Let
U
be the branch line containing
u
.
Similarly, let
e^{\prime\prime}\subset\partial P
be the blue edge adjacent to
d
.
The face of
P
meeting
e^{\prime\prime}
contains an upper track-cusp
t
that points away from
d
.
Let
T
be the branch line containing
t
.

Partition the blue edges of
P
into two sets: those connected either to
c
or to
d
by a sequence of blue edges (not passing through
b
in the case that
P
is finite).
Call these the
c
–edges and the
d
–edges.
Due to the colouring of the edges of
P
, the cusp line
\ell_{K}(c,U)
cannot exit
P
through a
c
–edge.
Similarly,
\ell^{K}(d,T)
cannot exit
P
through a
d
–edge.
By Lemma
9.0.1
, at least one must exit
P
, and hence the cusp leaves
\lambda(c,U)
and
\lambda(d,T)
link.

Figure 10.4.
A possible (finite) strip of faces
P
starting at the edge
e
.

We make the same argument to the left of
e
.
This produces branch lines
S
in
N^{c}
and
V
in
N_{d}
so that
\lambda(d,V)
links
\lambda(c,S)
.
Thus the points

c,\partial T,\partial U,d,\partial S,\partial V

appear in
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
in that anticlockwise order.
Let
T^{\prime}
be the branch line in
N^{d}
, adjacent to
T
, chosen so that
c
lies in
[\partial T^{\prime},\partial T]^{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\curvearrowleft$}}}
.
We define
S^{\prime}
,
U^{\prime}
, and
V^{\prime}
similarly.
Applying Lemma
9.0.6
and Lemma
9.0.10
we deduce that the points

c,\partial S^{\prime},\partial T,\partial U,\partial V^{\prime},d,\partial T^{\prime},\partial S,\partial V,\partial U^{\prime}

appear in
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
in that anticlockwise order.
See Figure
10.5
.

Figure 10.5.
The upper and lower crowns for cusps at the two ends of an edge.

Recall that
e
meets the cusps
c
and
d
.
Set
\Lambda^{e}=\Lambda^{(c,d)}
as in Definition
8.0.18
.
Note that the leaves of
\Lambda^{e}
are exactly those of
\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}
that link
e
.
We define
\Lambda_{e}
in similar fashion.
By the claim immediately above, every
\lambda
in
\Lambda^{e}
links every
\mu
in
\Lambda_{e}
.
Thus
\Lambda^{e}\times\Lambda_{e}
is a subset of
\operatorname{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{V})
.
By Lemma
8.0.19
this is a product of two copies of the Cantor set
\mathcal{C}
.
Mapping this product to its quotient in
\operatorname{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{V})/{\sim}
is realised by the applying the Cantor function in each coordinate.
That is, we replace ternary expansions by binary by replacing all twos by ones.
Now, since
0.\bar{1}=1.\bar{0}
and applying Lemma
10.0.5
, pairs and four-tuples of points are identified in the desired fashion.
Thus the image in
\operatorname{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{V})/{\sim}
is homeomorphic to the closed square
[0,1]^{2}
.

Note, however, that
\lambda(S,S^{\prime})
is smallest in
(\Lambda^{e},<^{e})
.
So, its adjacent boundary leaf in
\Lambda^{c}
, sharing the endpoint
\partial S
, does not link
e
.
Thus any point
p\in\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
, having a representative of the form
(\lambda(S,S^{\prime}),\mu)
, is not in
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
.
The same holds for
\lambda(U^{\prime},U)
as well as
\lambda(T^{\prime},T)
and
\lambda(V,V^{\prime})
in
\Lambda_{e}
.
This proves that
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
is homeomorphic to the open square
(0,1)^{2}
.
Fixing one coordinate and applying the Cantor function to the other produces the desired foliations, obtaining (
1
).

We deduce that
\partial\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
lies in the union of the cusp leaves
\ell^{S}
,
\ell_{U}
,
\ell^{T}
, and
\ell_{V}
.
Note that
\ell^{S}\cap\ell_{V}
and
\ell^{T}\cap\ell_{U}
give points of
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
.
These are the material corners of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
.
On the other hand
\ell^{S}
and
\ell_{U}
have a common cusp class in
\operatorname{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{V})/{\sim}
coming from
c
.
Similarly,
\ell^{T}
and
\ell_{V}
have a common cusp class coming from
d
.
These are the ideal corners of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
.
This gives (
2
).

With Lemma
10.0.13
in hand (and recalling Remark
10.0.3
), we make the following definition.

Definition 10.0.16
.

We now define the
induced orientation
on edge rectangles.
Suppose that
e
is red.
Then we orient the sides of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
as follows.
The sides of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
contained in
\ell^{S}
and
\ell^{T}
are oriented towards the cusps
c
and
d
, respectively.
The sides of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
contained in
\ell_{U}
and
\ell_{V}
are oriented away from the cusps
c
and
d
, respectively.
This induces an orientation on
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
.
Edge rectangles for blue edges are oriented using the opposite convention.

In the following, we will abuse notation and refer to a “cusp class coming from a cusp
c
” simply as
c
.

Remark 10.0.17
.

We have the following useful characterisation of points of
\partial\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
:
these are the classes where some (but not every) representative links
e
.

Lemma 10.0.18
.

Suppose that
K
is a layer of a layering. Then

\left\{{\overline{\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)}}\mathbin{\mid}\mbox{$e$ is an edge of $K$}\right\}

is a closed cover of
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
.

Proof 10.0.19
.

Fix any linking pair
(\lambda,\mu)\in\operatorname{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{V})
.
Let
\ell
and
m
be the train lines in
\tau^{K}
and
\tau_{K}
given by Lemma
8.0.13
(
3
).
These cross.
Consulting Figure
5.4
we deduce that
\ell
and
m
are tangent to each other along some train interval, perhaps of length zero.
Some edge or edges are transverse to this interval;
any such edge
e
links both
\lambda
and
\mu
.
Thus
[(\lambda,\mu)]
lies in
{\overline{\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)}}
.

10.6.
Face rectangles

Definition 10.0.20
.

Fix a face
f
in
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
.
We say that a pair
(\lambda,\mu)\in\operatorname{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{V})

links

f
if the pair links at least one of edges of
f
.
The
face rectangle

\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f)
is defined to be

\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f)=\{p\in\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})\mathbin{\mid}\mbox{every representative $(\lambda,\mu)$ of $p$ links $f$}\}

Suppose that
e
,
e^{\prime}
, and
e^{\prime\prime}
are the edges of a face
f
. We choose the labels so that
e^{\prime}
and
e^{\prime\prime}
have the same colour, and the track-cusp of
\tau^{f}
meets
e^{\prime\prime}
. Note that this implies that the track-cusp of
\tau_{f}
meets
e^{\prime}
. See Figure
5.4
.
From the definitions we deduce that the edge rectangles
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
,
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\prime})
, and
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\prime\prime})
are contained in
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f)
.
However,
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f)
is not the union of the edge rectangles.
For example, there are points in
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
with one representative linking only
e
, and another representative linking only
e^{\prime}
, say.
See the right side of Figure
10.7
.

Figure 10.7.
Left: a majority red triangle with upper and lower tracks shown.
Right: the three edges
e
,
e^{\prime}
,
e^{\prime\prime}
of a face
f
of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
give three edge rectangles, all subrectangles of the face rectangle
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f)
.

Definition 10.0.21
.

Let
Q
and
R
be rectangles.
We say that
Q

south-north spans
R

if there is a leaf of
R\cap F^{\mathcal{V}}
contained in
Q
.
As a refinement of this, we say that
Q

strictly south-north spans
R

if there is a leaf
\ell
of
R\cap F^{\mathcal{V}}
so that
{\overline{\ell}}
is contained in
Q
and is compact.

We make similar definitions, replacing south-north with west-east and replacing
F^{\mathcal{V}}
with
F_{\mathcal{V}}
.

Note that in Definition
10.0.21
, we do
not
assume symmetry.
That is,
Q
may south-north span
R
while
R
does not west-east span
Q
.

Lemma 10.0.22
.

Suppose that
f
is a face of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
with edges
e
,
e^{\prime}
, and
e^{\prime\prime}
.
Suppose that
e^{\prime}
and
e^{\prime\prime}
have the same colour, and that the track cusp of
\tau^{f}
meets
e^{\prime\prime}
.
Suppose that
c
,
c^{\prime}
, and
c^{\prime\prime}
are the cusps opposite
e
,
e^{\prime}
, and
e^{\prime\prime}
respectively.

(1)

The face rectangle
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f)
is a rectangle in the sense of Definition
10.0.9
.

(2)

The boundary of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f)
is contained in a union of six cusp leaves.
Two sides of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f)
are segments connecting the ideal corner
c
to distinct material corners.
The two remaining sides meet two material corners and contain
c^{\prime}
and
c^{\prime\prime}
respectively.

(3)

The edge rectangles
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\prime})
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\prime\prime})
respectively south-north and west-east span the face rectangle
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f)
.

Proof 10.0.23
.

Breaking symmetry, suppose that
e^{\prime}
and
e^{\prime\prime}
are red and
e
is blue.
Let
s
be the track-cusp of
\tau^{f}
on
e^{\prime\prime}
;
let
u
be the track-cusp of
\tau_{f}
on
e^{\prime}
.
Let
S
be the upper branch line through
s
;
let
U
be the lower branch line through
u
.

Claim
.

The intersection
{\overline{\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)}}\cap{\overline{\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\prime})}}
is equal to
\ell^{S}\cap\partial\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
.

Proof 10.0.24
.

Suppose that
p
is a point of
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
.
Thus
p
is not a cusp class.
We claim that the following are equivalent.

(a)

The point
p
lies in
{\overline{\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)}}\cap{\overline{\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\prime})}}
.

(b)

The point
p
has representatives
(\lambda,\mu)
and
(\lambda^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
, where
(\lambda,\mu)
links
e
, where
(\lambda^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
links
e^{\prime}
, where
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
are distinct and asymptotic, and where
\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}
are asymptotic.

(c)

The point
p
has representatives
(\lambda,\mu)
and
(\lambda^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
, where
(\lambda,\mu)
links
e
, where
(\lambda^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
links
e^{\prime}
, where
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
are the distinct boundary leaves sharing the endpoint
\partial S
, and where
\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}
are asymptotic.

(d)

The point
p
lies in
\ell^{S}\cap\partial\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
.

Statement (a) implies the first half of statement (b) by definition.
Consulting the left-hand side of Figure
10.7
, we see that
\lambda
does not link
e^{\prime}
and that
\lambda^{\prime}
does not link
e
, so
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
are distinct.
The various leaves are asymptotic by Lemma
10.0.5
.
Statement (b) implies statement (a) by definition.

Statement (b), and Lemma
8.0.13
(
6
), imply that
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
are boundary leaves. They share an endpoint;
examining the track
\tau^{f}
this endpoint must be
\partial S
.
Thus (b) implies (c);
the converse direction follows from the definition of asymptotic.

The first part of statement (c) implies that
p
lies on
\ell^{S}
.
Since
(\lambda,\mu)
links
e
, but
(\lambda^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
does not, Remark
10.0.17
tells us that the point
p
lies in
\partial\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
.
This gives (d).
To see the converse, since
p
lies in
\ell^{S}
, we may choose two representatives,
(\lambda,\mu)
and
(\lambda^{\prime},\mu)
, so that
\lambda
and
\lambda^{\prime}
are distinct asymptotic boundary leaves sharing the endpoint
\partial S
and
\lambda
links
e
while
\lambda^{\prime}
links
e^{\prime}
.
If
\mu
links
e
then it also links
e^{\prime}
and we are done.
If not, then as
p
lies in
\partial\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
, we deduce that
\mu
is asymptotic to some
\mu^{\prime}
which links
e
.
Replacing
\mu
with
\mu^{\prime}
proves the claim.

Similarly, the intersection
{\overline{\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)}}\cap{\overline{\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\prime\prime})}}
is equal to
\ell_{U}\cap\partial\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
.

We adopt the following notation.
Set
R_{00}=\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
.
From
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\prime})
we remove the open interval
\ell_{U}\cap\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\prime})
to obtain a pair of open rectangles
R_{10}
and
R_{11}
. We choose these so that
{\overline{R}}_{00}
and
{\overline{R}}_{10}
intersect in an arc;
thus
{\overline{R}}_{00}\cap{\overline{R}}_{10}
is exactly
\ell^{S}\cap\partial\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
.
Appealing to Lemma
10.0.13
(
2
) this interval is homeomorphically embedded in the boundaries of
R_{00}
and
R_{10}
.
We cut
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\prime\prime})
using the interior interval
\ell^{S}\cap\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\prime\prime})
into a pair of rectangles
R_{01}
and
R^{\prime}_{11}
.
Again
R_{00}
and
R_{01}
are nicely glued along
\ell_{U}\cap\partial\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
.
We claim that
R^{\prime}_{11}=R_{11}
.
To see this, note that if
\lambda
and
\mu
link each other, link
e^{\prime}
, and neither link
e
, then necessarily both link
e^{\prime\prime}
.

We deduce that the closures of the four rectangles
R_{00}
,
R_{10}
,
R_{11}
, and
R_{01}
meet cyclically along subintervals of
\ell^{S}
and
\ell_{U}
.
The only point contained in all four closures is their common material corner
\ell^{S}\cap\ell_{U}
.
This proves (
1
).
Conclusion (
2
) follows from (
1
) and Lemma
10.0.13
(
2
).
The two parts of Conclusion (
3
) follow from the decomposition of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f)
given above.

Lemma 10.0.25
.

Suppose that
K
is a layer of a layering.
Suppose that
e
is an edge of
K
.
Suppose that
s
is a side of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
.
Then there is a face of
K
whose face rectangle contains the interior of
s
.

[bl] at 174 760

[l] at 175 646

[tr] at 427 560
