\endlabellist

Figure 10.8.

The logic of the proof of Lemma
10.0.25
. The material point at the end of the side
s
is marked with a yellow dot.

Proof 10.0.26
.

Breaking symmetry, we suppose that
e
is red and
s
is the east side of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
.
Let
c
be the cusp corresponding to the northeastern ideal corner of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
.
See Figure
10.8
(a).
Let
f
be the face of
K
incident to
e
so that
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f)
extends to the south or east of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
.

Label the edges of
f
as
e
,
e^{\prime}
, and
e^{\prime\prime}
, ordered anticlockwise.
Thus
e^{\prime\prime}
is the other edge of
f
incident to
c
.
There are now two cases as
e^{\prime\prime}
is blue or red.
If
e^{\prime\prime}
is blue then the interior of
s
lies in
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f)
and we are done.
The two subcases in which
e^{\prime\prime}
is blue are shown in Figure
10.8
(b).

Suppose instead that
e^{\prime\prime}
is red.
See Figure
10.8
(c).
Suppose that
f^{\prime}
is the face that meets
f
along
e^{\prime\prime}
.
Let
e^{\prime\prime\prime}
be the edge of
f^{\prime}
that meets
c
.
There are two cases as
e^{\prime\prime\prime}
is red or blue.

•

If
e^{\prime\prime\prime}
is red then
s
again lies in
\partial\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f^{\prime})
.
So, in this case, we replace
f
by
f^{\prime}
and continue rotating about
c
in the anticlockwise direction.
See Figure
10.8
(d).
By Lemma
5.0.26
we do not revisit this case forever and must eventually find that
e^{\prime\prime\prime}
is blue.

•

If
e^{\prime\prime\prime}
is blue then there are two cases, as the remaining edge of
f^{\prime}
is red (Figure
10.8
(e), two possible combinatorial configurations) or blue (Figure
10.8
(f)).
In all cases, the interior of
s
lies in
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f^{\prime})
.

Lemma 10.0.27
.

Suppose that
K
is a layer of a layering. Then

\{\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f)\mathbin{\mid}\mbox{$f$ is a face of $K$}\}

is an open cover of
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
.
This cover has no finite subcover.

Proof 10.0.28
.

Fix a point
p
in
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
.
By Lemma
10.0.18
there is some edge
e
in
K
so that
p
lies in
{\overline{\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)}}
.
Suppose that
p
lies in
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
.
Then let
f
be either of the two faces in
K
incident to
e
.
Thus
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)\subset\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f)
, proving the first statement.
Suppose instead that
p
lies in
\partial\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
.
If
p
lies in the interior of a side of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
then we are done by Lemma
10.0.25
.
The final possibility is that
p
is a material corner of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
.
Let
s
be either of the sides of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
meeting
p
.
By Lemma
10.0.25
, we obtain a face
f
so that the interior of
s
lies in
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f)
.
We deduce that
p
lies in the interior of a side
s^{\prime}
of some edge rectangle in
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f)
.
Applying Lemma
10.0.25
again proves the first statement.

Finally, note that any finite union of face rectangles has only finitely many cusp classes as ideal points.
Thus the open cover by face rectangles has no finite subcover.

Proof 10.0.29

(Proof of Theorem
10.1
)
.

Fix
K
, a layer of a layering
\mathcal{K}
.
We fix one edge
e_{0}
of
K
;
we order the faces
(f_{i})
of
K
by their combinatorial distance from
e_{0}
and break ties arbitrarily.
Set
\mathcal{L}_{k}=\bigcup_{i=0}^{k}\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f_{i})
.

We claim that
\mathcal{L}_{k}
is homeomorphic to a closed disk with
k+3
points removed from its boundary.
The base case of
\mathcal{L}_{0}=\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f_{0})
follows from Lemma
10.0.22
(
2
).
To pass from
\mathcal{L}_{k}
to
\mathcal{L}_{k+1}
we note that
\mathcal{L}_{k}\cap\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f_{k+1})
is an edge rectangle.

We now take
D_{k}\subset\mathcal{L}_{k}
to be a compact disk, obtained from
\mathcal{L}_{k}
by removing small neighbourhoods of the
k+3
cusp classes.
By appropriately shrinking these neighbourhoods we arrange that
D_{k}\subset D_{k+1}
.
By Lemma
10.0.27
, the link space
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
is the increasing union of the closed disks
D_{k}
.
Thus
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
is a non-compact connected surface without boundary so that any compact subsurface is planar.
By Kerékjártó’s
Hauptsatz der Flächentopologie für offen Flächen

[
64
, page 170]
, we deduce that
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
is homeomorphic to the plane, as desired.
(See also
[
49
, Theorem 1]
.)
This gives (
1
).

By Lemma
10.0.22
(
1
), we have that
F^{\mathcal{V}}
and
F_{\mathcal{V}}
are foliations and are transverse inside of every face rectangle.
By Lemma
10.0.27
these cover
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
, and we obtain (
2
).
From Lemma
8.0.19
, we obtain (
3
).

The action of
\pi_{1}(M)
on
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
preserves the laminations (perhaps swapping them), hence the induced action preserves the foliations (perhaps swapping them).
The action of
\pi_{1}(M)
on
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
sends faces to faces so the action on
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
sends face rectangles to face rectangles.
Thus the action is continuous.

Note that
\pi_{1}(M)
acts faithfully on the edges of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
.
So fix
\gamma\in\pi_{1}(M)
as well as distinct edges
e
and
e^{\prime}
with
\gamma(e)=e^{\prime}
.
If
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)=\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\prime})
then the cusps at the ends of
e
and
e^{\prime}
must agree, by Lemma
10.0.13
(
2
).
However, this contradicts Lemma
5.0.22
.
We deduce that the action on
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
is faithful.
Thus we obtain (
4
).

Suppose now that
M
is oriented and that
\mathcal{V}
is transverse veering.
Suppose that
f
is a face of
K
, the given layer of
\mathcal{K}
.
Let
e,e^{\prime}
, and
e^{\prime\prime}
be the three edges of
f
, labelled as in Figure
10.7
.
The combinatorics of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f)
(see Lemma
10.0.22
(
2
)) implies that the induced orientation on
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\prime})
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\prime\prime})
agrees on their intersection.
Furthermore, the induced orientations on
\partial\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
and
\partial\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\prime})
disagree on their intersection.
The same holds on the intersection of
\partial\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
and
\partial\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\prime\prime})
.
This induces an orientation on
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f)
.
The same holds for any face of
K
.
By Lemma
10.0.27
, and since face rectangles (for faces in
K
) are either disjoint or intersect in an edge rectangle, this induces an orientation on
K
.
Note that any other layer
K^{\prime}
of
\mathcal{K}
shares infinitely many faces with
K
and thus has the same orientation.
Finally, any other layering is made up of the same faces as
\mathcal{K}
.
Thus we obtain (
5
).

The action of
\pi_{1}(M)
on
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
preserves the red and blue edges and the transverse orientation.
Also the action of
\pi_{1}(M)
does not swap the laminations and thus does not swap the foliations.
Thus it preserves the given orientation of face rectangles.
Thus we obtain (
6
).

10.9.
Cardinal directions

Applying Theorem
10.1
(
1
) we deduce that
F^{\mathcal{V}}
and
F_{\mathcal{V}}
are orientable.
Thus, in addition to the choices made in Remark
10.0.3
,
we fix an arbitrary orientation on
F_{\mathcal{V}}
; we call this orientation
east
.
This together with Theorem
10.1
(
5
) determines an orientation on
F^{\mathcal{V}}
, which we will call
north
.
As usual, the opposite of east is
west
, and the opposite of north is
south
.

Suppose that
p
and
q
are points of
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
.
Suppose that
\rho=(\rho_{i})
is a
polygonal path
from
p
to
q
.
That is, each
\rho_{i}
is an interval in some leaf, and for all
i
, the arcs
\rho_{i}
and
\rho_{i+1}
are in opposite foliations.
If every
\rho_{i}
lying in
F_{\mathcal{V}}
is oriented to the east, then we say that
q
is to the east of
p
.
We make similar definitions for the other cardinal directions.

Lemma 10.0.30
.

The relation
to the east of
gives a partial order on
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
.
The same holds for the other three cardinal directions.

Proof 10.0.31
.

Transitivity follows by concatenating.
Antisymmetry follows because
F^{\mathcal{V}}
is a foliation of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
(Theorem
10.1
(
1
) and (
2
)) so the leaves of
F^{\mathcal{V}}
are properly embedded lines (as in
[
7
, page 168]
), which thus separate
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
.

For the remainder of the paper we require all rectangles
R\colon(0,1)^{2}\to\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
to send oriented segments in the positive
y
–direction (
x
–direction) to oriented segments of
F^{\mathcal{V}}
(
F_{\mathcal{V}}
).

Lemma 10.0.32
.

With the choice of orientations of
F^{\mathcal{V}}
and
F_{\mathcal{V}}
as above we have the following.
An edge
e\in{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
is red if and only if the northeast and southwest corners of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
are ideal.

Proof 10.0.33
.

We refer to the proof of Lemma
10.0.13
, where
e
was assumed to be red.
With notation as in that lemma, we have that

c,\partial U,\partial V^{\prime},d,\partial T^{\prime},\partial S,

appear in
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
in that anticlockwise order.
See Figure
10.5
.
Fix
[(\lambda,\mu)]\in\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
so that both
\lambda
and
\mu
are interior leaves.
Breaking symmetry, we may assume that
\partial_{+}\mu
lies in
[\partial U,\partial V^{\prime}]^{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\curvearrowleft$}}}
.
Since
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
is connected, we deduce that
\partial_{+}\lambda
lies in
[\partial T^{\prime},\partial S]^{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\curvearrowleft$}}}
.
Thus
\ell_{U}
and
\ell^{S}
both point away from
c
.
Thus
c
lies in the southwest corner of the rectangle
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
.

In this paper, all figures showing parts of
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
are drawn using this choice of orientation on
F^{\mathcal{V}}
and on
F_{\mathcal{V}}
.

10.10.
Tetrahedron rectangles

Definition 10.0.34
.

Fix a tetrahedron
t
in
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
.
We say that a pair
(\lambda,\mu)\in\operatorname{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{V})

links

t
if the pair links at least one of the six edges of
t
.
The
tetrahedron rectangle

\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)
is defined to be

\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)=\{p\in\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})\mathbin{\mid}\mbox{every representative $(\lambda,\mu)$ of $p$ links $t$}\}

Recall from Section
2.3
that two of the edges of
t
are the upper and lower edges (respectively) of
t
and the remaining four are the equatorial edges of
t
.
Similarly we have two upper and two lower faces of
t
.

Lemma 10.0.35
.

Suppose that
t
is a tetrahedron of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
.
Let
f
and
g
be the upper faces of
t
and also
f^{\prime}
and
g^{\prime}
be the lower faces.

(1)

The tetrahedron rectangle
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)
is a rectangle in the sense of Definition
10.0.9
.

(2)

The boundary of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)
is contained in a union of eight cusp leaves.
There are four material corners.
Each of the four sides contains exactly one ideal point, corresponding to one of the four cusps of
t
.

(3)

The tetrahedron rectangle
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)
is equal to both
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f)\cup\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(g)
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f^{\prime})\cup\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(g^{\prime})
.
Moreover,
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f)
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(g)
both south-north span
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)
, while
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f^{\prime})
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(g^{\prime})
both west-east span
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)
.

(4)

No pair of ideal points in the boundary of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)
share a common leaf.

Proof 10.0.36
.

It follows from the definitions that
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f)\cup\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(g)\subset\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)
and, similarly,
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f^{\prime})\cup\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(g^{\prime})\subset\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)
.

Claim
.

\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)\subset\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f)\cup\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(g)
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)\subset\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f^{\prime})\cup\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(g^{\prime})
.

Proof 10.0.37
.

Suppose that
p
is a point of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)
.
We prove that
p
is contained in
R(f)\cup R(g)
; the other case is similar.
If
p
is contained in an edge rectangle for an equatorial edge of
t
, or the upper edge of
t
, then we are done by the remarks following Definition
10.0.20
.

In the remaining cases,
p
lies on the common boundary of two edge rectangles, both contained in one of
R(f)
or
R(g)
; see Figure
10.8
(e).
By Lemma
10.0.22
(
1
), the face rectangles
R(f)
and
R(g)
are rectangles in the sense of Definition
10.0.9
.
Thus the common boundary of the two edge rectangles, and so
p
, lies in the interior of
R(f)
or of
R(g)
.

The claim proves the first statement of (
3
).
Let
e=f\cap g
and
e^{\prime}=f^{\prime}\cap g^{\prime}
be the upper and lower (respectively) edges of
t
.
By Lemma
10.0.22
(
3
), the rectangle
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
south-north spans both
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f)
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(g)
.
Thus
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
south-north spans
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)
.
Since
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f)
contains
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
, we deduce that
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f)
south-north spans
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)
.
The other three statements of (
3
) are proved similarly.

Note, by Lemma
10.0.22
(
3
), that
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
is a subrectangle of, and south-north spans, both
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f)
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(g)
.
Figure
10.8
(e) shows the cases where the upper edge
e
is red.
Thus
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)
is a rectangle, and we obtain (
1
).

Note that
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f)
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(g)
share precisely two cusp classes, namely those on the boundary of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
.
Thus there are four cusp classes in
\partial\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)
.
No two can be in a single side as that would give a cusp leaf meeting two cusp classes, contradicting Corollary
7.0.10
.
This gives (
2
).

Finally, suppose that
a
and
c
are cusps in the southern and northern sides of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)
respectively.
Again appealing to Corollary
7.0.10
, we find that
a
and
c
cannot lie on a common leaf.
This gives (
4
).

The next result is needed to relate the combinatorics of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
to the combinatorics of rectangles in
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
.

Lemma 10.0.38
.

Suppose that
f
and
t
are, respectively, a face and a tetrahedron of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
.
Then
f\subset t
if and only if
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f)\subset\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)
.

Proof 10.0.39
.

The forwards direction follows from Lemma
10.0.35
(
3
).

We prove the contrapositive of the backwards direction.
Suppose that
f
is not a face of
t
.
Fix a layering
\mathcal{K}
of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
.
We break symmetry and suppose that
f
is contained in a layer
K
, of
\mathcal{K}
, that lies above
t
.
Furthermore, we take
K
to be the lowest such layer in
\mathcal{K}
.

Suppose that the upper faces of
t
lie in
K
.
Thus there is some edge, say
e
, of
f
that separates
f
(in
K
) from the upper faces of
t
.
Thus the cusp of
f
not meeting
e
cannot lie in a side of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)
and we are done.
See Figures
10.7
and
10.8
(e).

Suppose instead that the upper faces of
t
do not lie in
K
.
In this case let
e
be the edge that is removed from
K
in order to obtain the immediately lower layer.
We deduce that
e
is an edge of
f
.
Let
c
and
d
be the endpoints of
e
.
The faces of
t
are all strictly below
K
, hence
e
is not an edge of
t
.
Therefore by Lemma
5.0.22
, no edge of
t
connects
c
and
d
.
Therefore at least one of
c
or
d
does not lie in any side of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)
.
Therefore
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f)
is not a subset of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)
and we are done.

10.11.
All rectangles

We now prove that the tetrahedron rectangles control all rectangles of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
.

Theorem 10.0.39
.

Suppose that
R
is a rectangle in
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
.
Then there is a tetrahedron
t
of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
so that
R\subset\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)
.

Before giving the proof we need several lemmas.

Lemma 10.0.40
.

Suppose that
K
is a layer of a layering and
R
is a rectangle.
Then
R
is south-north spanned by at most finitely many edge rectangles
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
for
e
in
K
.
The same holds west-east.

Proof 10.0.41
.

We consider only the south-north case.
Applying the second statement of Lemma
8.0.19
, we choose distinct non-cusp leaves
m,m^{\prime}\in F_{\mathcal{V}}
which intersect
R
.
For any edge
e
, if
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
south-north spans
R
, then
m
and
m^{\prime}
both cross
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
.

Since
m
is non-cusp, by Definition
10.0.8
, there is a unique leaf
\mu
of
\Lambda_{\mathcal{V}}
so that

m=\big\{[(\lambda,\mu)]\in\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})\mbox{ for some $\lambda\in\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}$}\big\}

Let
\mu^{\prime}
be the corresponding leaf for
m^{\prime}
.
Thus
\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}
are distinct interior leaves.
Lemma
8.0.13
(
3
) provides train lines
m_{K}
and
m^{\prime}_{K}
carried by the train track
\tau_{K}
with the same endpoints as
\mu
and
\mu^{\prime}
respectively.
Note that for any edge
e
in
K
, if the leaf
m
crosses
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
then the train line
m_{K}
links
e
.

By Lemma
8.0.13
(
6
), the train lines
m_{K}
and
m^{\prime}_{K}
share no endpoints;
thus they fellow travel for at most a finite collection of edges
E
of
K
.
Thus, at most
|E|
edge rectangles south-north span the subrectangle of
R
between
m
and
m^{\prime}
.
Therefore, at most
|E|
edge rectangles south-north span
R
.

Definition 10.0.42
.

Let
f
be a face with edges
e
,
e^{\prime}
, and
e^{\prime\prime}
.
The
median
of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f)
is the intersection
{\overline{\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)}}\cap{\overline{\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\prime})}}\cap{\overline{\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\prime\prime})}}
.

In the right-hand side of Figure
10.7
, the median is the central yellow dot.

Lemma 10.0.43
.

Suppose that
f
is a face with edges
e
,
e^{\prime}
, and
e^{\prime\prime}
, where
e
has the minority colour in
f
.
If
R
is a rectangle that contains the median of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f)
then
R
is spanned by at least one of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\prime})
or
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\prime\prime})
.

Proof 10.0.44
.

Breaking symmetry, suppose that
e
is blue while
e^{\prime}
and
e^{\prime\prime}
are red.
For example, see Figure
10.7
.
Since
R
contains the median, it cannot extend out of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f)
to the west or south;
the cusp leaves running through the median end at ideal points in the sides of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f)
.
The rectangle
R
may extend out of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f)
to either the east or north, but not both.
This is because the northeastern ideal corner of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f)
is not contained in
R
.

We apply Lemma
10.0.22
(
3
).
If
R
extends out of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f)
to the east then
R
is south-north spanned by
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\prime})
.
If it extends out the north then it is west-east spanned by
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\prime\prime})
.
If
R
is contained in
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f)
then it is spanned by both edge rectangles.

Lemma 10.0.45
.

Suppose that
K
is a layer of a layering and
R
is a rectangle.
Then there is an edge
e
in
K
so that
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
either south-north or west-east spans
R
.

Proof 10.0.46
.

By Lemma
10.0.18
, there is an edge
e_{0}
in
K
so that
R\cap{\overline{\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e_{0})}}
is non-empty.
Since
R
is open, we also have that
R\cap\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e_{0})
is non-empty.
Breaking symmetry, suppose that
e_{0}
is red.
If
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e_{0})
does not span
R
in either direction, then
R
contains precisely one of the two material corners of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e_{0})
.
Breaking symmetry again and applying Lemma
10.0.32
, we assume that
R
contains
p_{0}
, the southeastern corner of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e_{0})
.

Consider now the maximal strip
P\subset K
of edges and triangles
(f_{i},e_{i})_{i\geq 1}
so that

(1)

all edges
e_{i}
are red,

(2)

all triangles
f_{i}
are majority red,

(3)

e_{0}
is contained in
f_{1}
,

(4)

e_{i}
is contained in
f_{i}
(and in
f_{i+1}
when
f_{i}
is not the last triangle in
P
),

(5)

R
contains
p_{i}
, the southeastern material corner of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e_{i})
.

Let
c_{0}
and
d_{0}
be the endpoints of
e_{0}
.
We label the other vertices of
P
as follows.
Suppose that we have labelled the vertices of the edges
e_{0},\ldots,e_{k-1}
.
Two of the vertices of
f_{k}
are already labelled.
The remaining vertex of
f_{k}
receives the label
c_{k}
if we turn left through
f_{k}
when travelling from
e_{k-1}
to
e_{k}
,
or the label
d_{k}
if we turn right.
For an example, see the left side of Figure
10.13
.

)

The four possible face rectangles, labelled by the intercardinal direction of the ideal corner.

Claim
.

The closure of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e_{k})
contains
p_{0}
.

Proof 10.0.47
.

The base case of the induction is trivial.
Suppose that the claim holds for
e_{k}
.
Breaking symmetry, suppose that the southwest corner of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f_{k+1})
is ideal.
See Figure
10.14c
.
By the inductive hypothesis,
p_{0}
is in the closure of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e_{k})
: the western half of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f_{k+1})
.
Note that
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e_{k+1})
is the southern half;
thus
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e_{k})
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e_{k+1})
intersect non-trivially, in a rectangle lying in the southwest of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f_{k+1})
.
Again see Figure
10.14c
.
Since
R
contains
p_{k+1}
, the north side of
R
is (perhaps non-strictly) south of
d_{k+1}
.
Since
R
contains
p_{0}
, this and Lemma
10.0.30
means that
p_{0}
is strictly south of
d_{k+1}
.
Thus
p_{0}
is contained in the closure of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e_{k+1})
.

Claim
.

Every
d_{k^{\prime}}
is northeast of every
c_{k}
.

Proof 10.0.48
.

The previous claim implies that the northeast (southwest) corner of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e_{k})
is northeast (southwest) of
p_{0}
.
Thus all
d_{k^{\prime}}
are northeast of
p_{0}
, and all
c_{k}
are southwest of
p_{0}
.
Now Lemma
10.0.30
gives the claim.

Claim
.

The strip
P
is finite.

Proof 10.0.49
.

Suppose that
P
is infinite.
We produce a sequence of strips
(P_{i})
below
P=P_{0}
, where
P_{i+1}
is obtained from
P_{i}
by flipping down through the first pair of triangles in
P_{i}
that form the top of a tetrahedron.
Such a pair must exist by Lemma
5.0.26
.
Note that the vertices and boundary edges of
P_{i+1}
are the same as those of
P_{i}
, and hence the same as those of
P_{0}=P
.
Let
P_{n}
be the first strip that contains an interior blue edge,
e_{\triangledown}
say.
Note that
K
exists by Corollary
6.0.9
(for lower branch lines).
An example sequence of strips is shown in Figure
10.15
.

The edge
e_{\triangledown}
connects vertices on opposite sides of the strip
P_{n}
.
Suppose that the endpoints of
e_{\triangledown}
are
c_{k}
and
d_{k^{\prime}}
.
Applying Lemma
10.0.32
, we find that one of
c_{k}
and
d_{k^{\prime}}
is southeast of the other.
By Lemma
10.0.30
this contradicts the previous claim.

We finish the proof of Lemma
10.0.45
as follows.
Let
e_{n}
be the last edge of
P
.
Let
f^{\prime}
be the face adjacent to
e_{n}
that is not in
P
.
Either
f^{\prime}
is majority blue (see Figure
10.14d
) or majority red (see Figure
10.14b
or Figure
10.14c
).
In the majority blue case,
R
contains the median of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f^{\prime})
, so by Lemma
10.0.43
we are done.
In the majority red case, let
e^{\prime}
be the other red edge of
f^{\prime}
.
Since
P
is a maximal strip,
R
does not contain the southeastern corner of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\prime})
.
Thus
R
is spanned by
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\prime})
(south-north in the case of Figure
10.14b
, and west-east in the case of Figure
10.14c
) and we are done.

(
a
)

(
b
)

(
c
)

(
