)

(
e
)

Figure 10.15.
On the left in each subfigure we see a strip of triangles from above.
On the right, we see a side view, showing the height of the mid-curve through the strip.
In particular, the top edge of a tetrahedron appears as a local maximum of the curve.

Proof 10.0.50

(Proof of Theorem
10.12
)
.

It suffices to consider the case when
R
is maximal.
Fix
K
, a layer of a layering.
By Lemma
10.0.45
, there is an edge of
K
whose edge rectangle spans
R
.
Breaking symmetry, we suppose that
R
is south-north spanned.
By Lemma
10.0.40
, the set
E
of edges in
K
, whose rectangles south-north span
R
, is finite.
For
e,e^{\prime}\in E
we say that
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
is
to the west
of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\prime})
if

•

the west side of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
is to the west of the west side of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\prime})
or, in the case of a tie,

•

the east side of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
is to the west of the east side of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\prime})
.

Let
e_{\triangleleft}
be any edge whose rectangle
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e_{\triangleleft})
is the furthest to the west.
(The subscript indicates a cone pointing to the west.)

Breaking symmetry, suppose that
e_{\triangleleft}
is red.
Let
f_{\triangleleft}
be the face containing
e_{\triangleleft}
and so that the (material) southeastern corners of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e_{\triangleleft})
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f_{\triangleleft})
coincide.

There are four possible face rectangles, depending on which corner is ideal.
See Figure
10.14
.

Claim
.

The rectangle
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f_{\triangleleft})
has its ideal corner to its northeast.

Proof 10.0.51
.

We rule out three of the four cases.

•

By assumption, the southeastern corner of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f_{\triangleleft})
is material.

•

Suppose that the ideal corner of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f_{\triangleleft})
is in the southwest, as shown in Figure
10.14c
.
Let
e
be the other red edge of
f_{\triangleleft}
.
Note that
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
extends further to the north than
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e_{\triangleleft})
.
Since
R
is maximal, it meets the west side of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e_{\triangleleft})
.
Thus
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
also spans
R
; we deduce that
e\in E
.
This contradicts our assumption that
e_{\triangleleft}
is the westmost edge in
E
.

•

A similar argument rules out the ideal corner being in the northwest. See Figure
10.14a
.

Since
R
is maximal, its west side contains the cusp class at the west side of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f_{\triangleleft})
.
We have a similar situation on the east of
R
, with an eastmost edge
e_{\triangleright}
and face
f_{\triangleright}
.
See Figure
10.16
.

Figure 10.16.
Above: An example of a maximal rectangle
R
(shaded).
It has a westmost south-north spanning edge rectangle that is in the east of a face rectangle
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f_{\triangleleft})
as in Figure
10.14b
.
It also has an eastmost south-north spanning edge rectangle that is in the west of a face rectangle
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f_{\triangleright})
as in Figure
10.14a
.
Below: a sketch of the corresponding strip of triangles
P
in
K
, connecting
f_{\triangleleft}
with
f_{\triangleright}
.

Since
K
is a copy of the Farey triangulation (Lemma
4.0.2
), there is a unique strip of triangles
P
connecting
f_{\triangleleft}
with
f_{\triangleright}
.
By Theorem
10.1
(
3
), we may take
m
to be a non-cusp leaf of
F_{\mathcal{V}}
meeting
R
.
By Lemma
8.0.13
(
3
), there is a corresponding train line
m_{K}\subset\tau_{K}
.
Note that
m_{K}\cap\tau_{P}
contains a train interval which runs from the track-cusp in
f_{\triangleleft}
to the track-cusp in
f_{\triangleright}
.
These track-cusps point towards each other, so there is at least one large switch of
\tau_{P}
at an edge in the interior of
P
.
The two faces incident to such an edge are the upper two faces of a tetrahedron.
Moving
P
down through such a tetrahedron has the effect of performing a split on
\tau_{P}
.
After finitely many such moves,
P
consists of two faces only.
These are thus the top two faces of a tetrahedron
t
.
Thus
R
is contained in (and in fact equal to)
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)
.

10.17.
Loom spaces

We now recall the definition of a loom space from our previous paper
[
54
, Definition 2.11]
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{L}
is a
bifoliated plane
:
a copy of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
equipped with a pair of transverse foliations
F^{\mathcal{L}}
and
F_{\mathcal{L}}
.
We define rectangles in
\mathcal{L}
just as in Definition
10.0.9
.
We define the sides of rectangles just as in Definition
10.0.10
.
In both cases, we replace
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
,
F^{\mathcal{V}}
, and
F_{\mathcal{V}}
with
\mathcal{L}
,
F^{\mathcal{L}}
, and
F_{\mathcal{L}}
.

Definition 10.0.52
.

Suppose that
R
is a rectangle in the bifoliated plane
\mathcal{L}
.
Suppose that
f_{R}\colon(0,1)^{2}\to R
is an associated embedding.
We say that
R
is a
south-west cusp rectangle
if there is a continuous extension of
f_{R}
to a homeomorphism

{\overline{f}}_{R}\colon[0,1]^{2}-\{(0,0)\}\to{\overline{R}}

We call the southern and western sides of
R
its
cusp sides
.
We orient these to the east and north respectively.
We define the other three intercardinal cusp rectangles, and their cusp sides, similarly.

As in
[
54
, Definitions 3.1 and 3.3]
, a
loom-cusp
of
\mathcal{L}
is an equivalence class of cusp rectangles.
The relation is generated by shrinking or expanding cusp rectangles, and walking from one cusp rectangle to another through a shared cusp side.

Definition 10.0.53
.

Suppose that
R
is a rectangle in the bifoliated plane
\mathcal{L}
.
Suppose that
f_{R}\colon(0,1)^{2}\to R
is an associated embedding.
We say that
R
is a
loom-tetrahedron rectangle
if there are
a,b,c,d\in(0,1)
and a continuous extension of
f_{R}
to a homeomorphism

{\overline{f}}_{R}\colon[0,1]^{2}-\{(a,0),(1,b),(c,1),(0,d)\}\to{\overline{R}}

Definition 10.0.54
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{L}
is a bifoliated plane.
We say that
\mathcal{L}
is a
loom space
if it satisfies the following two axioms.

(1)

For every cusp side
s
of every cusp rectangle
R
, some rectangle
Q
contains an initial open interval of
s
.

(2)

For every rectangle
R
, some loom-tetrahedron rectangle
Q
contains
R
.

Definition 10.0.55
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{M}
are loom spaces.
Suppose that
f\colon\mathcal{L}\to\mathcal{M}
is a homeomorphism.
We say that
f
is a
loom isomorphism
if it sends leaves to leaves.

We now show that link spaces are loom spaces.

Theorem 10.0.55
.

Suppose that
M
is a three-manifold equipped with a locally veering triangulation
\mathcal{V}
.

(1)

The link space
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
, equipped with the foliations
F^{\mathcal{L}}
and
F_{\mathcal{L}}
is a loom space.

(2)

Every tetrahedron rectangle is a loom-tetrahedron rectangle; and conversely.

(3)

The set of cusp rectangles in
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
, with ideal point a given cusp class of
\operatorname{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{V})/{\sim}
, is a loom-cusp; and conversely.

(4)

The action of
\pi_{1}(M)
is faithful and by loom automorphisms.

Proof 10.0.56
.

By Theorem
10.1
(
1
) the link space
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
is a copy of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
By Theorem
10.1
(
2
), we have that
F^{\mathcal{V}}
and
F_{\mathcal{V}}
are foliations and are transverse.

Suppose that
R
is a cusp rectangle in
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
.
Suppose that
s
is a cusp side of
R
.
By Theorem
10.12
, there is a tetrahedron
t
of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
so that
R\subset\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)
.
We deduce that the cusp sides of
R
accumulate, in
\operatorname{\mathsf{P}}(\mathcal{V})
, at one of the four ideal points in
\partial\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)
.
Therefore we are justified in saying that the cusp rectangle
R
has exactly one ideal corner.
Consulting Figure
10.8
(e), we deduce that an initial segment of
s
is contained in a side of some edge rectangle.
Applying Lemma
10.0.25
, we obtain Definition
10.0.54
(
1
).
Definition
10.0.54
(
2
) follows from Theorem
10.12
.
Thus
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
satisfies all axioms of a loom space and we have obtained (
1
).

By Theorem
10.12
, each loom-tetrahedron rectangle is contained in some tetrahedron rectangle.
By Lemma
10.0.35
(parts (
1
) and (
2
)) each tetrahedron rectangle is a loom-tetrahedron rectangle.
Thus we have obtained (
2
).

Fix any cusp
c
of
\Delta_{\mathcal{V}}
.
As in Lemma
9.0.6
, this yields a pair of interleaving crowns
\Lambda^{c}
and
\Lambda_{c}
.
As in Definition
10.0.8
, the boundary leaves of
\Lambda^{c}
collapse to give cusp-leaves in
F^{\mathcal{V}}
, and similarly for
\Lambda_{c}
and
F_{\mathcal{V}}
.
By Lemma
10.0.27
, some initial segment of each cusp leaf is contained in some face rectangle.
Cutting down gives a cusp rectangle
R
in
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
.
The cusp rectangles generated in this way form a loom-cusp by another application of Lemma
9.0.6
.

In the other direction, suppose that
R
is a cusp rectangle.
By parts (
1
) and (
2
), the rectangle
R
is contained in a tetrahedron rectangle.
Thus it arises as in the previous paragraph, and we have obtained (
3
).

By construction, the action of
\pi_{1}(M)
on
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
sends tetrahedron rectangles to tetrahedron rectangles, thus is continuous.
It also sends leaves to leaves.
Since elements of
\pi_{1}(M)
have inverses, we have obtained (
4
).

Chapter 11
…and back again

Let
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loom}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
be the category in which objects are loom spaces and arrows are loom isomorphisms.
Let
\operatorname{\mathsf{Veer}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
be the category in which objects are locally veering triangulations of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and arrows are
taut isomorphisms
: that is, isomorphisms of ideal triangulations that preserve tautness.

In our previous work
[
54
, Proposition 5.19 and Theorem 6.50]
, following Guéritaud, we gave a functor

\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}\colon\operatorname{\mathsf{Loom}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Veer}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})

defined as follows.
In a loom space
\mathcal{L}
, there are three kinds of
skeletal rectangles
.
These are the loom-tetrahedron rectangles (Definition
10.0.53
), the loom-face rectangles, and the loom-edge rectangles.
These meet four, three, or two cusps respectively, with exactly one cusp in the closure of each side.

Definition 11.0.1
.

We define the
induced
locally veering triangulation
\mathcal{V}=\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}(\mathcal{L})
by taking one model cell
\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(R)

for every skeletal rectangle
R\subset\mathcal{L}
.
We glue two model cells
\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(R)
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(R^{\prime})
along another model cell
\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(S)
if
R\cap R^{\prime}=S
.

Our goal in Chapter
11
is to show that the link space construction

\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}\colon\operatorname{\mathsf{Veer}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Loom}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})

is a functor (Proposition
11.0.40
) and is an “inverse” for
\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}
(Theorem
11.9
).

11.1.
Partial orders

Throughout this section we make parallel definitions for loom spaces and locally veering triangulations.

Suppose that
\mathcal{L}
is a loom space.
We choose an orientation on
\mathcal{L}
.
We also choose one of the foliations to be upper, that is
F^{\mathcal{L}}
, and the other to be lower, that is
F_{\mathcal{L}}
.
The loom automorphisms of
\mathcal{L}
may not respect these choices.
However, there is a subgroup of finite index which does.

Definition 11.0.2
.

Suppose that
Q
and
R
are loom-tetrahedron rectangles of
\mathcal{L}
so that
Q
west-east spans
R
.
In this case, we write
Q\prec R
.

Note that
Q\prec R
if and only if
R
south-north spans
Q
.
Also, Definition
11.0.2
gives a partial order on loom-tetrahedron rectangles.

Suppose that
\mathcal{V}
is a locally veering triangulation of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
We choose an orientation of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
and a transverse veering structure for
\mathcal{V}
.
The taut automorphisms of
\mathcal{V}
may not respect these choices.
However, there is a subgroup of finite index which does.

Definition 11.0.3
.

Suppose that
s
and
t
are tetrahedra of
\mathcal{V}
sharing a face
f
.
If the transverse orientation on
f
points from
s
to
t

then we write
s\prec t
, and take the transitive closure.

The orders given by Definitions
11.0.2
and
11.0.3
are related as follows.

Lemma 11.0.4
.

•

Suppose that
Q
and
R
are loom-tetrahedron rectangles of
\mathcal{L}
.
Then
Q\prec R
if and only if
\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(Q)\prec\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(R)
.

•

Suppose that
s
and
t
are tetrahedra of
\mathcal{V}
.
Then
s\prec t
if and only if
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(s)\prec\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)
.

Proof 11.0.5
.

Suppose that
Q\prec R
.
By
[
54
, Lemma 4.22]
and by the construction given in
[
54
, Definition 5.9]
, we deduce that
\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(Q)\prec\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(R)
.

Now suppose that
\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(Q)\prec\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(R)
.
Let

(\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(Q)=\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(R_{0}),\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(R_{1}),\ldots,\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(R_{n})=\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(R))

be an alternating sequence of tetrahedra and faces with the following property.

•

For all
i
, we have
\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(R_{2i+1})
is an upper face of
\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(R_{2i})
and is a lower face of
\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(R_{2i+2})
.

By the construction given in
[
54
, Definition 5.9]
, we have that
R_{2i}\prec R_{2i+2}
.
By transitivity,
Q\prec R
.

Suppose
s\prec t
.
As above, there is an alternating sequence

(s=c_{0},c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}=t)

of tetrahedra and faces with the following property.

•

For all
i
, we have
c_{2i+1}
is an upper face of
c_{2i}
and is a lower face of
c_{2i+2}
.

By Lemma
10.0.35
(
3
), we have that
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(c_{i})
west-east spans
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(c_{i+1})
.
We deduce that
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(s)
west-east spans
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)
.
Thus
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(s)\prec\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)
.

Finally, suppose that
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(s)\prec\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)
.
By Theorem
10.18
, we may apply
[
54
, Lemma 4.22]
.
From Lemma
10.0.35
(
3
) we deduce that
s\prec t
.

Remark 11.0.6
.

Lemma
11.0.4
implies that the relation given in Definition
11.0.3
, on the tetrahedra of
\mathcal{V}
, is isomorphic to the relation given in Definition
11.0.2
, on tetrahedron rectangles of
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
.
Since the latter is a partial order, so is the former.
Thus it suffices to carry out constructions in the setting of loom spaces.

Lemma 11.0.7
.

Suppose that
R\prec S\prec T
are loom-tetrahedron rectangles of
\mathcal{L}
.
Then
R\cap T
is nonempty and is contained in
S
.

Proof 11.0.8
.

Since
\prec
is transitive,
R\prec T
, and so
R\cap T
is nonempty.
Suppose that
p
is a point of
R\cap T
.
Let
\ell
and
m
be the leaves of
F^{\mathcal{V}}
and
F_{\mathcal{V}}
, respectively, through
p
.
Note that
p\in\ell\cap R\subset\ell\cap S
.
Similarly,
p\in m\cap T\subset m\cap S
.
Therefore
p\in S
and we are done.

Recalling Definition
10.0.21
, we have the following.

Lemma 11.0.9
.

Suppose that
R\prec T
are loom-tetrahedron rectangles in
\mathcal{L}
.
Then
T
strictly south-north spans
R
if and only if
the south and north vertices of
T
are disjoint from the south and north vertices of
R
.
In similar fashion
R
strictly west-east spans
T
if and only if
the west and east vertices of
R
are disjoint from the west and east vertices of
T
. ∎

Note that neither hypothesis in Lemma
11.0.9
implies the other.
See Figure
11.2
.
We now give a slightly weakened version of the
astroid lemma

[
54
, Lemma 4.10]
.

Lemma 11.0.10
.

Suppose that
p
is a point of a loom space
\mathcal{L}
.
Suppose that
S
is a rectangle of
\mathcal{L}
containing
p
.
Then there is a loom-tetrahedron rectangle
R
with the following properties.

•

p\in R
.

•

R
west-east spans
S
.

•

S
strictly south-north spans
R
.

Similarly, there is a loom-tetrahedron rectangle
T
with the corresponding properties, swapping west-east and south-north.

\labellist
\hair

2pt

\pinlabel
R
at 330 187

\pinlabel
T
at 140 65

\endlabellist

(
a
)

\labellist
\hair

2pt

\pinlabel
R
at 330 210

\pinlabel
T
at 190 65

\endlabellist

(
b
)

\labellist
\hair

2pt

\pinlabel
R
at 330 187

\pinlabel
T
at 190 65

\endlabellist

(
c
)

Figure 11.2.
Loom-tetrahedron rectangles
R\prec T
.
In Figure
11.2a
we have that
T
strictly south-north spans
R
, but
R
does not strictly west-east span
T
.
In Figure
11.2b
we have that
R
strictly west-east spans
T
, but
T
does not strictly south-north span
R
.
In Figure
11.2c
we have that each strictly spans the other.

Proof 11.0.11

(Sketch proof of Lemma
11.0.10
)
.

Let
m
be the west-east leaf through
p
.
By
[
54
, Lemma 3.10]
, the leaf
m
meets at most one cusp.
Breaking symmetry, we may assume that this cusp, if it exists, is not to the west of
p
.
Take a copy of
S
and push its northern side slightly to the south and its southern side slightly to the north to obtain a new rectangle, still containing
p
.
Now push its western side to the west.
By the astroid lemma
[
54
, Lemma 4.10]
, the western side eventually meets a cusp, say
c_{0}
, and we stop.

By our assumption,
c_{0}
does not lie on
m
.
Breaking symmetry we assume that
c_{0}
is to the north of
p
.
Push the northern side of our rectangle south until
c_{0}
lies at its north-west corner.
We repeat this process, replacing
c_{0}
with
c_{i}
, until we find
c_{n}
, a cusp on the western side of the rectangle but which is to the south of
p
.
Again, we terminate after a finite number of steps by the astroid lemma.

Now push the southern side of our rectangle north until
c_{n}
lies at its south-west corner.
At this point, push the western side west and the eastern side east until they meet cusps,
c_{W}
and
c_{E}
say.
Again, the astroid lemma ensures that
c_{W}
and
c_{E}
exist.

We now have a loom-tetrahedron rectangle
R
with
c_{n-1}
as its northern cusp, with
c_{n}
as its southern cusp, and with
c_{W}
and
c_{E}
as its western and eastern cusps.
Moreover, since we never increased the south-north extent, and in the first step, decreased it by a definite amount,
S
strictly south-north spans
R
.
Since we never decreased the west-east extent,
R
west-east spans
S
.

Corollary 11.0.12
.

The loom-tetrahedron rectangles give a subbasis for the topology of the loom space. ∎

We also have the following finiteness property
[
54
, Lemma 4.16]
.

Lemma 11.0.13
.

Suppose that
S
is a rectangle of
\mathcal{L}
.
Then there are only finitely many loom-tetrahedron rectangles containing
S
. ∎

Lemma 11.0.14
.

Suppose that
S
and
S^{\prime}
are loom-tetrahedron rectangles of
\mathcal{L}
.
Suppose that
p
is a point of
S\cap S^{\prime}
.
Then there are loom-tetrahedron rectangles
R
and
T
so that
p\in R\cap T
and so that
S
and
S^{\prime}
strictly south-north span
R
and strictly west-east span
T
.

Proof 11.0.15
.

We apply Lemma
11.0.10
to the (not necessarily loom-tetrahedron) rectangle
S\cap S^{\prime}
. This gives loom-tetrahedron rectangles
R
and
T
with the desired properties.

11.3.
Astroids and axes

Definition 11.0.16
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{L}
is a loom space.
Suppose that
p
is a point of
\mathcal{L}
.
The
astroid

\operatorname{Ast}(p)
is the set of loom-tetrahedron rectangles containing
p
.

Figure 11.4.
Some of the astroid
\operatorname{Ast}(p)
when
p
is a Weierstrass point (centre) for the fibre of the monodromy of the figure eight knot complement.
If
p
lies on one or two cusp leaves then the corresponding “arms” of the astroid are truncated.

Lemma 11.0.17
.

The intersection of all rectangles in
\operatorname{Ast}(p)
equals
\{p\}
.

Proof 11.0.18
.

Let
P
be the intersection of all rectangles in
\operatorname{Ast}(p)
.
Suppose that
S
is any (not necessarily loom-tetrahedron) rectangle containing
p
.
By Lemma
11.0.10
, there is a rectangle
R\in\operatorname{Ast}(p)
so that
S
strictly south-north spans
R
.
Thus no point of
P
lies to the south or to the north of
S
.
By a similar argument, no point of
P
lies to the west or to the east of
S
.
Since
S
was arbitrary,
P=\{p\}
.

Definition 11.0.19
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{L}
is a loom space.
We say that a non-empty collection
A
of loom-tetrahedron rectangles is a
loom-axis
if it satisfies the following properties.

(1)

For all
S,S^{\prime}\in A
there are
R,T\in A
so that
S
and
S^{\prime}
strictly south-north span
R
and strictly west-east span
T
.

(2)

A
is maximal with respect to the previous property.

Theorem 11.0.19
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{L}
is a loom space.
Suppose that
A
is a set of loom-tetrahedron rectangles in
\mathcal{L}
.
Then
A
is a loom-axis if and only if it is an astroid.

Proof 11.0.20
.

Suppose that
A
is a loom-axis.
By definition,
A
is nonempty.
From
[
54
, Corollary 4.25]
we deduce that
A
is countable.
We order the elements of
A=(S_{i})
.
By Definition
11.0.19
(
1
) there is a sequence
(R_{k})\subset A
so that
each element of the collection

\{S_{0},S_{1},\ldots,S_{k}\}\cup\{R_{k}\}

strictly south-north spans
R_{k+1}
.
There is a similar sequence
(T_{k})\subset A
replacing south-north by west-east.
We deduce that

R_{k+1}\prec R_{k}\prec T_{k}\prec T_{k+1}\quad\mbox{and}\quad R_{k+1}\prec S_{k}\prec T_{k+1}

By Lemma
11.0.7
, the intersection
R_{k+1}\cap T_{k+1}
is nonempty and lies in
R_{k}\cap S_{k}\cap T_{k}
.
Since
R_{k}
and
T_{k}
strictly span
R_{k+1}
and
T_{k+1}
, respectively, the closure of
R_{k+1}\cap T_{k+1}
is strictly contained in
R_{k}\cap T_{k}
.
Lemma
11.0.13
implies that the nested intersection is a single point
p
of
\mathcal{L}
.
Lemma
11.0.7
now implies that
p
lies in
S_{k}
for all
k
.
Thus
A\subset\operatorname{Ast}(p)
.

On the other hand, by Lemma
11.0.14
, the astroid
\operatorname{Ast}(p)
satisfies Definition
11.0.19
(
1
).
This, together with
A\subset\operatorname{Ast}(p)
and Definition
11.0.19
(
2
) for
A
proves that
A=\operatorname{Ast}(p)
.

For the opposite direction, fix
p
and consider the astroid
\operatorname{Ast}(p)
.
Again, by Lemma
11.0.14
, we have that
\operatorname{Ast}(p)
satisfies Definition
11.0.19
(
1
).
Suppose that
A
is a set of loom-tetrahedron rectangles that also satisfies Definition
11.0.19
(
1
) and contains
\operatorname{Ast}(p)
.
Suppose, for a contradiction, that there is some loom-tetrahedron rectangle
S
so that
S\in A-\operatorname{Ast}(p)
.
There are two cases as
p
misses or lies in
\partial S
.

Suppose that
p
is not in
\partial S
.
Breaking symmetry, suppose that
m
is the leaf of
F_{\mathcal{L}}
which contains a side of
S
and separates
S
from
p
.
By Lemma
11.0.10
there is a loom-tetrahedron rectangle
S^{\prime}
that contains
p
and is disjoint from
m
, and thus is disjoint from
S
.
Since
S^{\prime}
contains
p
, we have that
S^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Ast}(p)\subset A
.
Now applying Definition
11.0.19
(
1
) and Lemma
11.0.7
, we contradict the fact that
S
and
S^{\prime}
are disjoint.

Suppose instead that
p
lies in
\partial S
.
Breaking symmetry, suppose that
p
lies in the north side of
S
.
By Definition
11.0.19
(
1
) (applied to the loom-tetrahedron rectangle
S
twice)
there is a loom-tetrahedron rectangle
R
in
A
so that
S
strictly south-north spans
R
.
Thus
R
and
S
have disjoint north sides.
Thus
p
does not lie in the closure of
R
.
Taking
R
in place of
S
, we are now in the first case:
since
R\in A
and
p
misses
\partial{R}
we reach a contradiction.

Thus
\operatorname{Ast}(p)
is maximal and so satisfies Definition
11.0.19
(
2
).
Therefore
\operatorname{Ast}(p)
is a loom-axis, as desired.

11.6.
Bijections

We now translate Lemma
11.0.9
into the language of veering triangulations.

Definition 11.0.21
.

As in Section
2.3
, for every tetrahedron
t
in
\mathcal{V}
, the transverse orientation chooses one of the two non-equatorial edges to be the upper edge of
t
.
The other non-equatorial edge is the lower edge of
t
.
We refer to the two vertices of
t
incident to the upper edge as its
upper vertices
.
The remaining two vertices of
t
are its
lower vertices
.

Definition 11.0.22
.

Suppose that
r\prec t
are tetrahedra in
\mathcal{V}
.
We say that
t

strictly south-north spans

r
if the upper vertices of
t
are disjoint from the upper vertices of
r
.
In similar fashion, we say that
r

strictly west-east spans

t
if
the lower vertices of
r
are disjoint from the lower vertices of
t
.

We transport Definition
11.0.19
to veering triangulations, as follows.

Definition 11.0.23
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{V}
is a transverse veering triangulation of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
We say that a non-empty collection
A\subset\mathcal{V}
of tetrahedra is a
(coarse flow) axis
if

(1)

For all
s,s^{\prime}\in A
there are
r,t\in A
so that
s
and
s^{\prime}
strictly south-north span
r
and strictly west-east span
t
.

(2)

A
is maximal with respect to the previous property.

Remark 11.0.24
.

Choosing the other transverse veering structure on
\mathcal{V}
swaps upper with lower but does not change the set of flow-axes.

Note that, following Remark
11.0.6
, we do not need to define “astroids” for veering triangulations.
We now define two functions.

Definition 11.0.25
.

Suppose that
p
is a point of a loom space
\mathcal{L}
.
Recall that if
R
is a loom-tetrahedron rectangle then
\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(R)
is the corresponding tetrahedron of
\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}(\mathcal{L})
.
We define
\operatorname{\mathsf{A}}(p)
, the
flow-axis for

p
, as follows.

\operatorname{\mathsf{A}}(p)=\{\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(R)\in\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}(\mathcal{L})\mathbin{\mid}R\in\operatorname{Ast}(p)\}

By Theorem
11.5
and then Lemma
11.0.4
, we have that
\operatorname{\mathsf{A}}(p)
is a flow-axis in
\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}(\mathcal{L})
.
Thus
\operatorname{\mathsf{A}}
is a function from
\mathcal{L}
to the set of flow-axes in
\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}(\mathcal{L})
.

Definition 11.0.26
.

Suppose that
A
is a flow-axis of a veering triangulation
\mathcal{V}
.
Recall that if
t
is a tetrahedron then
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)
is the corresponding rectangle of
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
.
We define
\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}(A)
, the
point for
A
, as follows.

\{\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}(A)\}=\cap\{\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)\mathbin{\mid}t\in A\}

By Lemma
11.0.4
,
Theorem
11.5
,
and Lemma
11.0.17

we have that the intersection is a singleton set in
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
.
Therefore,
\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}(A)
is a well-defined point of
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
.
That is,
\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}
is a function from the set of flow-axes in
\mathcal{V}
to
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
.
Applying Lemma
11.0.4
and Theorem
11.5
again, we deduce the following.

Corollary 11.0.27
.

The functions
\operatorname{\mathsf{A}}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}
are bijections. ∎

Restricting to tetrahedra and loom-tetrahedron rectangles, and applying Definition
11.0.1
and Theorem
10.12
, we also have the following.

Corollary 11.0.28
.

The functions
\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}
are bijections. ∎

Lemma 11.0.29
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{V}
is a veering triangulation.
Suppose that
A
is a flow-axis of
\mathcal{V}
.
Then we have the following.

(1)

\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(A)=\operatorname{Ast}(\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}(A))
.

(2)

Suppose that
t
is a tetrahedron of
\mathcal{V}
.
Then
t\in A
if and only if
\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}(A)\in\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)
.

Proof 11.0.30
.

By Lemma
11.0.4
and Corollary
11.0.28
, we have that
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(A)
is a loom-axis.
By Theorem
11.5
, we have that
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(A)
is an astroid.
By Definition
11.0.16
, there is some
q\in\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
so that
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(A)=\operatorname{Ast}(q)
.
By Lemma
11.0.17
, we have that
\{q\}=\cap\operatorname{Ast}(q)=\cap\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(A)
.
However, by Definition
11.0.26
, we have that
\cap\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(A)=\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}(A)
.
Thus
\{q\}=\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}(A)
.
Substituting, we find that
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(A)=\operatorname{Ast}(\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}(A))
, giving (
1
).

Now suppose that
t
lies in
A
.
So
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)\in\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(A)
and thus by Definition
11.0.26
, we have that
\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}(A)\in\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)
.

Suppose instead that
\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}(A)\in\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)
.
By Definition
11.0.16
, we have that
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)\in\operatorname{Ast}(\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}(A))
.
Since
\operatorname{Ast}(\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}(A))=\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(A)
, we have that
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)\in\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(A)
.
Applying Corollary
11.0.28
, we obtain (
2
).

Definition 11.0.31
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{V}
is a veering triangulation.
Suppose that
t
is a tetrahedron of
\mathcal{V}
.
The
quiver

\operatorname{Qui}(t)
is the set of flow-axes in
\mathcal{V}
containing
t
.

Lemma 11.0.32
.

\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}(\operatorname{Qui}(t))=\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)
.

Proof 11.0.33
.

Suppose that
p\in\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}(\operatorname{Qui}(t))
.
That is, there is a flow-axis
A\in\operatorname{Qui}(t)
with
p=\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}(A)=\cap\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(A)
.
Since
t\in A
, by the forward direction of Lemma
11.0.29
(
2
) we have that
p\in\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)
.

Suppose that
p\in\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)
.
Set
A=\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}^{-1}(\operatorname{Ast}(p))
.
By Theorem
11.5
, Lemma
11.0.4
, and Corollary
11.0.28
, the set
A
is a flow-axis.
Since
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)\in\operatorname{Ast}(p)
, applying Corollary
11.0.28
again we have that
t\in A
.
By Definition
11.0.31
, we have that
A\in\operatorname{Qui}(t)
.
On the other hand,
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(A)=\operatorname{Ast}(p)
, so by Lemma
11.0.29
(
1
), we have that
p=\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}(A)
.
Since
A\in\operatorname{Qui}(t)
, we may apply
\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}
and obtain
p=\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}(A)\in\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}(\operatorname{Qui}(t))
.

11.7.
Functoriality

For the convenience of the reader, we recall the definition of the taut isomorphism induced by a loom isomorphism
[
54
, Definition 5.10]
.

Definition 11.0.34
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{L}
and
\mathcal{M}
are loom spaces.
Suppose that
\phi\colon\mathcal{L}\to\mathcal{M}
is a loom isomorphism.
We define
\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}(\phi)=\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}_{\phi}
, the
induced map
from
\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}(\mathcal{L})
to
\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}(\mathcal{M})
, as follows.

\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}_{\phi}(\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(R))=\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(\phi(R))

Lemma 11.0.35
.

With hypotheses as in Definition
11.0.34
, suppose that
p
is a point of
\mathcal{L}
.
Then we have the following.

\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}_{\phi}(\operatorname{\mathsf{A}}(p))=\operatorname{\mathsf{A}}(\phi(p))

Proof 11.0.36
.

Recall from Definition
11.0.16
that
\operatorname{Ast}(p)
is the set of loom-tetrahedron rectangles containing
p
.
Since
\phi
is a loom isomorphism, we have that
R\in\operatorname{Ast}(p)
if and only if
\phi(R)\in\operatorname{Ast}(\phi(p))
.
This proves the following.

Claim 3
.

\phi(\operatorname{Ast}(p))=\operatorname{Ast}(\phi(p))
∎

We now compute.

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}_{\phi}(\operatorname{\mathsf{A}}(p))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}_{\phi}(\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(\operatorname{Ast}(p)))

Definition
11.0.25

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(\phi(\operatorname{Ast}(p)))

Definition
11.0.34

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(\operatorname{Ast}(\phi(p)))

Claim
3

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathsf{A}}(\phi(p))

Definition
11.0.25

We now set out the parallel definition and lemma in the context of veering triangulations.

Definition 11.0.37
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{V}
and
\mathcal{W}
are locally veering triangulations of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
Suppose that
f\colon\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{W}
is a taut isomorphism.
We define
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(f)=\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}_{f}
, the
induced map
from
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
to
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{W})
, as follows.

\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}_{f}(\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}(A))=\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}(f(A))

Note that in making this definition, by Corollary
11.0.27
it suffices to consider points of
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
of the form
\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}(A)
where
A
is a flow-axis in
\mathcal{V}
.

Lemma 11.0.38
.

With hypotheses as in Definition
11.0.37
, suppose that
t
is a tetrahedron of
\mathcal{V}
.
Then we have the following.

\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}_{f}(\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t))=\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f(t))

Proof 11.0.39
.

Recall from Definition
11.0.31
that
\operatorname{Qui}(t)
is the set of flow-axes containing
t
.
Since
f
is a taut isomorphism,
t
lies in a flow-axis
A
if and only if
f(t)\in f(A)
.
This proves the following.

Claim 4
.

f(\operatorname{Qui}(t))=\operatorname{Qui}(f(t))
∎

We now compute.

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}_{f}(\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}_{f}(\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}(\operatorname{Qui}(t)))

Lemma
11.0.32

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}(f(\operatorname{Qui}(t)))

Definition
11.0.37

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}(\operatorname{Qui}(f(t)))

Claim
4

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f(t))

Lemma
11.0.32

We are now equipped to prove the following.

Proposition 11.0.40
.

The link space construction
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}
is a functor.

Proof 11.0.41
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{V}
is a locally veering triangulation of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
By Theorem
10.18
, the link space
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})
is a loom space.

Applying Definition
11.0.37
twice, we have

\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{id}}_{\mathcal{V}}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}(A))=\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{id}}_{\mathcal{V}}(A))=\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}(A)=\operatorname{\mathsf{id}}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})}(\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}(A))

Thus
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{id}}_{\mathcal{V}})=\operatorname{\mathsf{id}}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})}
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{U}
and
\mathcal{W}
are also locally veering triangulations of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
Suppose that
f\colon\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{V}
and
g\colon\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{W}
are taut isomorphisms.
Suppose that
A
is a flow-axis in
\mathcal{U}
.
Applying Definition
11.0.37
three times we have the following.

\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}_{g\circ f}(\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}(A))=\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}((g\circ f)(A))=\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}(g(f(A)))=\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}_{g}(\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}(f(A)))=\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}_{g}\circ\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}_{f}(\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}(A))

Since
f
is a taut isomorphism, so is
f^{-1}\colon\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{U}
.
We now compute.

\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(f)\circ\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(f^{-1})=\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(f\circ f^{-1})=\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{id}}_{\mathcal{V}})=\operatorname{\mathsf{id}}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})}

Similarly,
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(f^{-1})\circ\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(f)=\operatorname{\mathsf{id}}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{U})}
.

By Lemma
11.0.38
, we have that
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(f)
sends tetrahedron rectangles to tetrahedron rectangles.
The same holds for
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(f^{-1})
.
By Corollary
11.0.12
, we deduce that
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(f)
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(f^{-1})
are both continuous.
Thus both are homeomorphisms.

Claim 5
.

Suppose that
p
and
p^{\prime}
are points of a loom space
\mathcal{L}
.
Then
p
and
p^{\prime}
are on a common leaf of
F^{\mathcal{L}}
if and only if for any
S\in\operatorname{Ast}(p)
and
S^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Ast}(p^{\prime})
there is a loom-tetrahedron rectangle which south-north spans both
S
and
S^{\prime}
.
The same holds, replacing
F^{\mathcal{L}}
with
F_{\mathcal{L}}
and south-north with west-east.

Proof 11.0.42
.

Suppose that
p
and
p^{\prime}
lie on
\ell
, a leaf of
F^{\mathcal{L}}
.
Fix
S\in\operatorname{Ast}(p)
and
S^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Ast}(p^{\prime})
.
Let
\ell^{\prime}
be an interval in
\ell
, not containing any non-cusp end of
\ell
but meeting both the north and south sides of both
S
and
S^{\prime}
.

Since rectangles provide a basis for the topology of
\mathcal{L}
, and applying Definition
10.0.54
(
1
) if needed, we may cover the interval
\ell^{\prime}
by a finite collection of rectangles.
Thus
\ell^{\prime}
is contained in a single rectangle
S^{\prime\prime}
.
We deduce that
S^{\prime\prime}
south-north spans both
S
and
S^{\prime}
.
By Lemma
11.0.10
, there is a loom-tetrahedron rectangle which contains
p
and which south-north spans
S^{\prime\prime}
.

We now prove the converse.
Suppose that for every
S\in\operatorname{Ast}(p)
and
S^{\prime}\in\operatorname{Ast}(p^{\prime})
there is a loom-tetrahedron rectangle that south-north spans both.
By Theorem
11.5
, we have a sequence
(S_{n})\subset\operatorname{Ast}(p)
so that
S_{n}
strictly west-east spans
S_{n+1}
.
Let
\ell_{p}
be the leaf of
F^{\mathcal{L}}
containing
p
.
From Lemma
11.0.13
we deduce that
\limsup S_{n}
is contained in
\ell_{p}
.
Suppose for a contradiction that
\limsup S_{n}
is not all of
\ell_{p}
.
Breaking symmetry, suppose that
\limsup S_{n}
has a material boundary point, say
q
, to its south.
Let
Q
be a small rectangle about
q
.
Thus
Q
contains the southern sides of infinitely many of the
S_{n}
.
We deduce that these loom-tetrahedron rectangles
S_{n}
do not have cusps on their southern sides, a contradiction.

Similarly, there is a sequence
(S^{\prime}_{n})\subset\operatorname{Ast}(p^{\prime})
so that
\limsup S^{\prime}_{n}=\ell_{p^{\prime}}
.
Let
T_{n}
be the given loom-tetrahedron rectangle that south-north spans both
S_{n}
and
S^{\prime}_{n}
.
We deduce that
\limsup T_{n}
is equal to both
\ell_{p}
and
\ell_{p^{\prime}}
.
Thus
\ell_{p}=\ell_{p^{\prime}}
, as desired.

Claim
5
gives a combinatorial characterisation of leaves in terms of loom-axes.
From Lemma
11.0.4
we deduce that
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(f)
sends leaves to leaves.
Thus
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(f)
is a loom isomorphism.

11.8.
Equivalence of categories

The goal of this section is to prove the following.

Theorem 11.0.42
.

The functors

\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}\colon\operatorname{\mathsf{Veer}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Loom}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}\colon\operatorname{\mathsf{Loom}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to\operatorname{\mathsf{Veer}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})

give an equivalence of categories.

To prove the theorem, we produce two natural isomorphisms:

\eta\colon\operatorname{Id}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{Loom}}}\to\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}\circ\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\zeta\colon\operatorname{Id}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{Veer}}}\to\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}\circ\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}

Definition 11.0.43
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{L}
is a loom space.
We define a map

\eta_{\mathcal{L}}\colon\mathcal{L}\to\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}\circ\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}(\mathcal{L})\quad\mbox{by setting}\quad\eta_{\mathcal{L}}(p)=\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{A}}(p))

Proposition 11.0.44
.

\eta
is a natural isomorphism.

Proof 11.0.45
.

We verify the defining properties as follows.

Claim 6
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{L}
is a loom space.
Then the map
\eta_{\mathcal{L}}
is a loom isomorphism.

Proof 11.0.46
.

By Corollary
11.0.27
, the map
\eta_{\mathcal{L}}
is a bijection.

Let
R
be a loom-tetrahedron rectangle in
\mathcal{L}
.
We now have the following.

\displaystyle p\in R\,\,

if and only if
R\in\operatorname{Ast}(p)

Definition
11.0.16

if and only if
\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(R)\in\operatorname{\mathsf{A}}(p)

Definition
11.0.25

if and only if
\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{A}}(p))\in\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(R))

Lemma
11.0.29
(
2
)

We deduce that
\eta_{\mathcal{L}}(R)=\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(R))
is a loom-tetrahedron rectangle in
\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}\circ\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}(\mathcal{L})
.
By Corollary
11.0.12
, the map
\eta_{\mathcal{L}}
is a homeomorphism.

By Claim
5
, Lemma
11.0.4
, and Theorem
11.5
the map
\eta_{\mathcal{L}}
sends leaves to leaves.

Claim 7
.

Suppose that
\phi\colon\mathcal{L}\to\mathcal{M}
is a loom isomorphism.
Then the following diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{L}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{M}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}\circ\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}(\mathcal{L})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}\circ\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}(\mathcal{M})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{\mathcal{L}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{\mathcal{M}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}\circ\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}(\phi)}

Proof 11.0.47
.

Suppose that
p\in\mathcal{L}
.
We now compute.

\displaystyle(\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}\circ\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})_{\phi}(\eta_{\mathcal{L}}(p))

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}\circ\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})_{\phi}(\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{A}}(p)))

Definition
11.0.43

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}(\phi)}(\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{A}}(p)))

(\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}\circ\operatorname{\mathsf{V}})_{\phi}=\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}(\phi))=\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}(\phi)}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}_{\phi}(\operatorname{\mathsf{A}}(p)))

Lemma
11.0.35

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathsf{p}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{A}}(\phi(p)))

Definition
11.0.34

\displaystyle=\eta_{\mathcal{M}}(\phi(p))

Definition
11.0.43

This completes the proof that
\eta
is a natural isomorphism.

Definition 11.0.48
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{V}
is a veering triangulation of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
We define a map

\zeta_{\mathcal{V}}\colon\mathcal{V}\to\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}\circ\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})\quad\mbox{by setting}\quad\zeta_{\mathcal{V}}(t)=\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t))

Proposition 11.0.49
.

\zeta
is a natural isomorphism.

Proof 11.0.50
.

We verify the defining properties as follows.

Claim 8
.

Suppose that
\mathcal{V}
is a veering triangulation of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
.
Then the map
\zeta_{\mathcal{V}}
is a taut isomorphism.

Proof 11.0.51
.

By Corollary
11.0.27
, the map
\zeta_{\mathcal{V}}
is a bijection.

Suppose that
f
is a face of
\mathcal{V}
.
Let
t
and
t^{\prime}
be tetrahedra of
\mathcal{V}
.
By Lemma
10.0.38
, we have that
t
meets
t^{\prime}
along
f
if and only if
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)\cap\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t^{\prime})=\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f)
.
By
[
54
, Lemma 2.31 and Definition 5.9]
, we have that
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)\cap\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t^{\prime})=\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f)
if and only if
\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t))
meets
\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t^{\prime}))
along the face
\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f))
.
Thus
\zeta_{\mathcal{V}}
is an isomorphism of triangulations.

By Lemma
10.0.35
, we have that
e
is an upper or lower edge of
t
if and only if
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
is contained in and spans
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)
(either south-north or west-east).
By
[
54
, Definition 5.9]
we have that
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
is contained in and spans
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)
if and only if
\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e))
is an upper or lower edge of
\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t))
.
Thus
\zeta_{\mathcal{V}}
sends the taut structure of
\mathcal{V}
to that of
\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V}))
.

Claim 9
.

Suppose that
f\colon\mathcal{V}\to\mathcal{W}
is a taut isomorphism.
Then the following diagram commutes.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{W}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}\circ\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{V})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}\circ\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\mathcal{W})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{\mathcal{V}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\zeta_{\mathcal{W}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}\circ\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(f)}

Proof 11.0.52
.

Suppose that
t\in\mathcal{V}
is a tetrahedron.
We now compute.

\displaystyle(\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}\circ\operatorname{\mathsf{L}})_{f}(\zeta_{\mathcal{V}}(t))

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}\circ\operatorname{\mathsf{L}})_{f}(\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)))

Definition
11.0.48

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(f)}(\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)))

(\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}\circ\operatorname{\mathsf{L}})_{f}=\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(f))=\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}_{\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(f)}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}_{f}(\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t)))

Definition
11.0.34

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathsf{c}}(\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(f(t)))

Lemma
11.0.38

\displaystyle=\zeta_{\mathcal{W}}(f(t))

Definition
11.0.48

This completes the proof that
\zeta
is a natural isomorphism.

Proof 11.0.53

(Proof of Theorem
11.9
)
.

Propositions
11.0.44
and
11.0.49
prove that
\eta
and
\zeta
are natural isomorphisms.
Thus the categories
\operatorname{\mathsf{Veer}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{Loom}}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
are equivalent.

With Theorem
11.9
in hand, we prove the following.

Corollary 11.0.54
.

Suppose that
M
is a connected three-manifold.
Suppose that
\mathcal{V}
is a locally veering triangulation on
M
.
Suppose that
\Gamma=\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}\circ\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}(\pi_{1}(M))
is the induced subgroup of
\operatorname{Aut}(\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}\circ\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}({\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}))
.
Then
\zeta_{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
descends to give an isomorphism from
\mathcal{V}
to
\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}\circ\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}({\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}})/\Gamma
.

Proof 11.0.55
.

By Claim
8
, the map
\zeta_{\mathcal{V}}
is a taut isomorphism from
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
to
\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}\circ\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}({\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}})
.
By Theorem
11.9
, we have that
\zeta
carries the
\pi_{1}(M)
–action on
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
to the
\Gamma
–action on
\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}\circ\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}({\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}})
.
In particular,
\zeta_{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
takes orbits of cells to orbits of cells.
Thus, the quotient of
\zeta_{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
is the desired isomorphism between
\mathcal{V}
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{V}}\circ\operatorname{\mathsf{L}}({\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}})/\Gamma
.

Appendix A
Towards Cannon–Thurston maps

Here we give results required for our work with Manning
[
40
]
.

A.1.
Parabolics revisited

Suppose that
M
is an oriented three-manifold equipped with a transverse veering triangulation
\mathcal{V}
.
Here we suppose that
\mathcal{V}
contains finitely many tetrahedra.
Thus
M
is a compact three-manifold with non-empty toroidal boundary.
As in Section
2.3
, we take
\Delta_{\mathcal{V}}
to be the collection of vertices of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
.
Fixing a cusp
c\in\Delta_{\mathcal{V}}
, we call
\operatorname{Stab}(c)<\pi_{1}(M)
a
(maximal) parabolic
subgroup.

We continue the analysis of the action of
\operatorname{Stab}(c)
on the veering circle
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
, as begun in Section
7.5
.
We begin with a review of the
ladder decomposition
of the
cusp torus
due to Futer–Guéritaud
[
17
]
.

A.2.
Ladders

Let
L(c)
be a
vertex link
about
c
in
{\widetilde{M}}
.
That is, if
t
is a tetrahedron incident to
c
then
t\cap L(c)
is a small triangle close to
c
.
We choose
L(c)
equivariantly, so it is stabilised by
\operatorname{Stab}(c)\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.
We also take
L(c)
sufficiently close to
c
to ensure that
L(c)\subset N^{c}\cap N_{c}
, the intersection of the upper and lower cusp neighbourhoods, as defined in Section
6.3
.

As discussed in Section
2.5
, the taut structure makes the two-skeleton of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
into a branched surface, denoted
{\widetilde{B}}
.
Thus the intersection
\tau(c)=L(c)\cap{\widetilde{B}}
is a
bigon train track
.
See Figure
A.3
for an example of a fundamental domain of the
\operatorname{Stab}(c)
action on
\tau(c)\subset L(c)
.
The veering census
[
21
]
contains many more examples.
Also see
Figure 3 of
[
17
]
, Figure 6 of
[
34
]
, and Figure 6 of
[
35
]
.

\labellist
\hair

2pt

\pinlabel
Q
at 10 15

\pinlabel
S
at 152 65

\pinlabel
P
at 290 15

\pinlabel
V
at 440 595

\endlabellist

Figure A.3.

The bigon track
\tau(c)
in the vertex link
L(c)\subset N(c)
, for the SnapPy manifold
s227
as determined by the veering triangulation
gLLAQbecdfffhhnkqnc_120012

from the veering census
[
21
]
.
Note that we only give a fundamental domain for the action of
\operatorname{Stab}(c)
.
Here the action of
\operatorname{Stab}(c)
is generated by two translations parallel to the
x
– and
y
–axes, respectively.
The colours and orientations are discussed in the main text.

Every edge
e
in
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
meeting
c
gives a switch
v
to the bigon track
\tau(c)
;
we colour
v
in
\tau(c)
with the veering colour of
e
.
Every face
f
in
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
meeting
c
gives a branch
b
to the bigon track
\tau(c)
;
we colour
b
with the veering colour of the third edge of
f
(that is, by Theorem
2.6
, the edge of
f
which does not meet
c
).
Suppose that
v
and
v^{\prime}
are the switches at the ends of
b
.
If these are
different
colours we call
b
a
rung branch
.
If these are the
same
colour we call
b
a
ladderpole branch
.
Again, see Figure
A.3
for an example.

Definition A.0.1
.

By Observation 2.4 of
[
17
]
, the union of the ladderpole branches give a collection of parallel loops in the torus
L(c)/\operatorname{Stab}(c)
;
this is the
ladderpole slope
.
Similarly, the union of the ladderpole branches in
L(c)
cut it into strips called
ladders
.
See also Remark
6.0.8
.

Following the above observation, we call the two sides of a ladder its
ladderpoles
.
We give a ladderpole the colour of its switches.

The colouring convention for ladderpoles, in Definition
A.0.1
, is forced by the convention set out in Figure
5.4
.
Note that, as we scan along
L(c)
from right to left, we encounter, cyclically, a red ladderpole, then an upper (green) branch line, then a blue ladderpole, and then a lower (purple) branch line.
This convention is the opposite of the one suggested by Lemma 6.5 and Figure 3 of
[
17
]
.

A.4.
Orienting the bigon track

Suppose that
f
is a face of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
meeting
c
;
let
b
be the resulting branch of
\tau(c)
.
Let
e
and
e^{\prime}
be the edges of
f
meeting
c
;
let
v
and
v^{\prime}
be the resulting switches of
\tau(c)
.
We also take
d
and
d^{\prime}
to be the other cusps (not
c
) on
e
and
e^{\prime}
respectively.
Suppose that
e^{\prime}
is immediately
before

e
in the boundary of
f
(with orientation induced by the transverse taut structure).
By Theorem
5.2
the circular order
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{V}}
is compatible;
so
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{V}}(c,d,d^{\prime})=1
.
We record this in
\tau(c)
by orienting
b
from
v
towards
v^{\prime}
.
Again, see Figure
A.3
for an example.
Note that, in the example, all rungs are oriented from right to left,
all red ladderpoles are oriented upwards, and
all blue ladderpoles are oriented downwards.
This is generally true; see Figure
5.5a
.

With notation as in the previous paragraph, we additionally assume that
e
is red and
e^{\prime}
is blue.
Let
e^{\prime\prime}
be the third edge of the face
f
.
In this case, the branch
b^{\prime\prime}
running from
v
to
v^{\prime}
is a rung.
Consulting Figure
5.4a
, we deduce that the upper track
\tau^{f}
has a switch
s
, associated to
c
, at the midpoint of
e^{\prime\prime}
.
The switch
s
lies on an upper branch line
S\subset\partial N^{c}
.
If we reverse the colours of
e
and
e^{\prime}
then instead a lower branch line
T
meets the midpoint of
b^{\prime\prime}
.
In any case, if
e
and
e^{\prime}
have the same colour then no branch line associated to
c
meets
b^{\prime\prime}
.
From this and Figure
6.2a
we deduce the following;
see also
[
33
, Lemma 2.7]
.

Lemma A.0.2
.

There is exactly one branch line running along the centre of every ladder;
the branch line is upper (green) if and only if the right ladderpole is red. ∎

It follows that the ladderpole slope and the branch slope
\beta
are equal (up to sign) in
\operatorname{Stab}(c)
.

A.5.
The action of the branch loop

Recall that
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
is the veering circle.
Suppose that
c\in\Delta_{\mathcal{V}}
is a cusp and
\beta\in\operatorname{Stab}(c)
is the branch slope, as defined in Definition
7.0.13
.
Recall that
\Lambda^{c}
and
\Lambda_{c}
are the crowns for
c
.
We will denote their tips in
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
by
\partial\Lambda^{c}
and
\partial\Lambda_{c}
.

Lemma A.0.3
.

Suppose that
\beta\in\operatorname{Stab}(c)
is the branch slope.
Its set of fixed points, as it acts on
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
, is

\{c\}\cup\partial\Lambda^{c}\cup\partial\Lambda_{c}

The points of
\partial\Lambda^{c}
are attracting while the points of
\partial\Lambda_{c}
are repelling.

Proof A.0.4
.

Suppose that
R
and
S
are adjacent upper branch lines.
Suppose that
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{V}}(c,\partial R,\partial S)=1
.
By Lemma
7.0.14
(
1
) it suffices to consider the action of
\beta
on the interval
[\partial R,\partial S]^{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\curvearrowleft$}}}
.
By Lemma
9.0.6
there is exactly one lower branch line
V
with
\partial V
in
[\partial R,\partial S]^{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\curvearrowleft$}}}
.

By Lemma
A.0.2
there are ladders
L(S)
and
L(V)
in
L(c)
containing
S
and
V
, respectively.
By our choice of
V
, the ladders
L(S)
and
L(V)
share a ladderpole
P
.
Breaking symmetry, we will assume that
P
is red.
See Figure
A.3
for such an example; there
S
is the green branch line,
V
is the purple branch line, and
P
is the ladderpole inbetween.
Let
(d_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}\subset\Delta_{\mathcal{V}}
be the cusps corresponding to the switches of
P
.
We arrange matters so that
d_{i}
and
d_{i+1}
share a face with
c
and so that
\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{V}}(c,d_{i},d_{i+1})=1
.
Again, see Figure
A.3
.

Let
e_{i}
be any rung meeting, and to the left of,
d_{i}
.
Thus
e_{i}
crosses
S
.
Note that the orientations on the ladderpoles show that the endpoints of
A(e_{i+1})
, and thus
A(e_{i+1})
itself, are contained in
A(e_{i})
.
From Lemma
7.0.6
and Definition
7.0.8
we deduce that
\bigcap A(e_{i})=\{\partial S\}
.
Thus the cusps
d_{i}
converge to
\partial S
(in
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
) as
i
tends to infinity.
(Also, the cusps
d_{i}
converge to
\partial V
as
i
tends to negative infinity.)
We can repeat this argument for the blue ladderpole
Q
on the other side of
S
.

Let
k
be the number of switches on the ladderloop
P/\beta
.
We deduce that
\beta(d_{i})=d_{i+k}
, for all
i
.
A similar statement holds for
Q
.
Thus
\partial S
is an attracting fixed point of
\beta
as it acts on
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
.
A similar argument shows that
\partial T
is a repelling fixed point.
Finally,
\beta
sends
[d_{i-k},d_{i}]^{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\curvearrowleft$}}}
to
[d_{i},d_{i+k}]^{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\curvearrowleft$}}}
; thus
\beta
has no fixed points in the interval
(\partial T,\partial S)^{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\curvearrowleft$}}}
.

A.6.
Conical limit points

We will also need delicate control of neighbourhoods of
singletons
in
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
and leaves in
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{V})
.

Definition A.0.5
.

A point
p\in S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
is a
singleton
if

•

it is not a cusp and

•

it is not an endpoint of a leaf of
\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}
or of
\Lambda_{\mathcal{V}}
.

Suppose that
e
is a co-oriented edge of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
.
Recall that
A(e)
is the closed arc of
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
between the endpoints of
e
, which is pointed at by the co-orientation. See Figure
7.3
.

Lemma A.0.6
.

Let
x\in S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
be a singleton.
Then there are two co-oriented edges
e
and
e^{\prime}
in
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
and a sequence

(\gamma_{i})\subset\pi_{1}(M)
with the following properties.

(1)

A(e^{\prime})
is contained in the interior of
A(e)
,

(2)

A(\gamma_{i+1}(e))\subset A(\gamma_{i}(e^{\prime}))
, for all
i
, and

(3)

\bigcap A(\gamma_{i}(e))=\{x\}
.

Proof A.0.7
.

Let
K
be a layer of a layering.
We co-orient every edge
e
of
K
so that the co-orientation points at
x
.
Fix any edge
e_{0}
of
K
.
Let
P
be the strip of triangles in
K
so that every interior edge of
P
separates
e_{0}
from
x
.

By Lemma
7.0.2
,
since
x
is not a cusp the strip
P
turns right and left infinitely often as it travels away from
e_{0}
.
As in Remark
7.0.11
, the edges of
P
give a neighbourhood basis for
x
in
S^{1}(\mathcal{V})
.

\labellist
\hair

2pt

\pinlabel
e_{0}
[r] at 6 64

\pinlabel
e_{n}
[l] at 263.5 70

\pinlabel
e^{\prime\prime}_{n}
[tl] at 317 70

\pinlabel
e^{\prime}_{n}
[l] at 374 50

\pinlabel
x
[l] at 620 64

\endlabellist

Figure A.7.
The strip
P
turns right and then left immediately before and after, respectively, the edge
e^{\prime\prime}_{n}
.

Consider the (infinitely many) edges
e^{\prime\prime}_{n}
of
P
where
P
turns right in the triangle before
e^{\prime\prime}_{n}
and left in the triangle after
e^{\prime\prime}_{n}
.
Let
e_{n}
and
e^{\prime}_{n}
be the edges immediately before and after
e^{\prime\prime}_{n}
along
P
.
Note that
e_{n}
and
e^{\prime}_{n}
share no cusps.
See Figure
A.7
.
Note that, up to the action of
\pi_{1}(M)
, there are only finitely many orbits of such pairs of edges.
Thus there is at least one such orbit which is represented infinitely often in
P
.
Any such orbit gives us a subsequence of pairs of edges with the desired properties.

We now turn our attention to leaves.
Here we require neighbourhood bases in the Möbius band past infinity,
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{V})
.
See Definition
8.0.10
.

Definition A.0.8
.

Suppose that
e
is a red edge of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
.
Suppose that
c
and
d
are the cusps meeting
e
, with
c
being the southwest corner of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
.
Following the notation of Figure
10.5
we choose
\lambda(S,S^{\prime})
and
\lambda(T,T^{\prime})
to be the boundary leaves,
in Definition
10.0.8
, giving rise to the upper cusp leaves
\ell^{S}
and
\ell^{T}
.
These, in turn, contain the west and east sides of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
.

We define the
upper product neighbourhood

P^{\mathcal{V}}(e)
to be the image of
[\partial S^{\prime},\partial T]^{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\curvearrowleft$}}}\times[\partial T^{\prime},\partial S]^{\rotatebox[origin]{-90.0}{\scalebox{0.7}{$\curvearrowleft$}}}
inside of
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{V})
.
We note that this is a closed neighbourhood for any leaf of
F^{\mathcal{V}}
meeting the interior of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
.
We define the
lower product neighbourhood

P_{\mathcal{V}}(e)
similarly, using
F_{\mathcal{V}}
.

We make similar definitions in the case that
e
is blue.

Lemma A.0.9
.

Suppose that
\lambda
is an interior leaf of
\Lambda^{\mathcal{V}}
.
Let
\ell=\ell^{\lambda}
be the corresponding non-cusp leaf of
F^{\mathcal{V}}
.
Then there are two edges
e
and
e^{\prime}
in
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
and a sequence
(\gamma_{i})\subset\pi_{1}(M)
as follows:

(1)

e^{\prime}
and
e
have no cusps in common,

(2)

\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\prime})
south-north spans
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
, and

(3)

the products
P^{\mathcal{V}}(\gamma_{i}(e^{\prime}))
give a neighbourhood basis for
\lambda
in
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{V})
, as do the products
P^{\mathcal{V}}(\gamma_{i}(e))
.

A similar statement holds for an interior leaf
\mu
of
\Lambda_{\mathcal{V}}
replacing
\lambda
.

Proof A.0.10
.

In order to prove the lemma, we will need the following.

Claim 10
.

Suppose that
e^{\prime\prime}
is an edge of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
so that
\ell
meets
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\prime\prime})
.
Then there are edges
e
and
e^{\prime}
such that

(i)

e^{\prime}
and
e
have no cusps in common,

(ii)

\ell
meets
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\prime})
, which south-north spans
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e)
, which south-north spans
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\prime\prime})
, and

(iii)

There are faces
f
and
f^{\prime}
, sharing an edge, such that
e
lies in
f
and
e^{\prime}
lies in
f^{\prime}
.

This implies Lemma
A.0.9
as follows.
Suppose that
K
is a layer of a layering.
By Lemma
10.0.13
(
2
) the boundary of any edge rectangle is contained in a union of cusp leaves.
Thus, by Lemma
10.0.18
there is some edge
e^{\prime\prime}_{0}
in
K
so that
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\prime\prime}_{0})
meets
\ell
.
Claim
10
now gives us
e_{0}
and
e^{\prime}_{0}
.
Given
e_{n}
and
e^{\prime}_{n}
, we set
e^{\prime\prime}_{n+1}=e^{\prime}_{n}
and appeal to Claim
10
to obtain
e_{n+1}
and
e^{\prime}_{n+1}
.

With the sequence of pairs of edges
(e_{n},e^{\prime}_{n})
now in hand, we proceed as follows.
Conclusions
(i)
and
(ii)
of the claim imply that
e_{n}
and
e^{\prime}_{n}
satisfy conclusions (
1
) and (
2
) of the lemma.
Also, each product
P^{\mathcal{V}}(e^{\prime}_{n})
is a neighbourhood of
\ell
in
\mathcal{M}(\mathcal{V})
,
and if
m<n
then
P^{\mathcal{V}}(e^{\prime}_{n})
is contained in the interior of
P^{\mathcal{V}}(e^{\prime}_{m})
.

Suppose, for a contradiction, that the sequence
(P^{\mathcal{V}}(e^{\prime}_{n}))
is not a neighbourhood basis of
\ell
.
We deduce that
\limsup\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\prime}_{n})
contains
\ell
as a proper subset.
Let
t_{n}
be any tetrahedron that contains
e^{\prime}_{n}
.
Thus
\limsup\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(t_{n})
contains
\ell
as a proper subset.
Appealing to Theorem
10.18
, we obtain a contradiction to Lemma
11.0.13
.
Thus the sequence
(P^{\mathcal{V}}(e^{\prime}_{n}))
is a neighbourhood basis of
\ell
.

By conclusion
(iii)
of the claim, there are only finitely many orbits (under the action of
\pi_{1}(M)
) of such pairs of edges.
We pass to an infinite subsequence consisting of a single orbit and reindex.
This gives the sequence
(\gamma_{i})\subset\pi_{1}(M)
.
We set
\gamma_{0}=1
.
We set
e=e_{0}
and
e^{\prime}=e^{\prime}_{0}
.
By the previous paragraph, the sequence
(P^{\mathcal{V}}(\gamma_{i}(e^{\prime})))
is a neighbourhood basis of
\ell
.
The same argument applies to
(P^{\mathcal{V}}(\gamma_{i}(e)))
.
This gives conclusion (
3
) of the lemma.

)

\endlabellist

(

)
Toggle.

Figure A.8.

Proof A.0.11

(Proof of Claim
10
)
.

As in Section
10.9
, we choose orientations on the foliations
F^{\mathcal{V}}
and
F_{\mathcal{V}}
.
We use the following notation.
Suppose that
e
is any red edge of
{\widetilde{\mathcal{V}}}
.
Let
t
be the tetrahedron immediately above
e
.
See Figure
A.8
.
We use
e^{\odot}
to denote the upper edge of
t
. (The superscript indicates a cone pointing out of the page.)
We use
e^{\negthinspace\triangledown}
and
e^{\vartriangle}
to denote the red equatorial edges of
t
which meet the southern and northern cusps of
e
, respectively.
(The superscripts indicate a cone pointing south or north.)

Breaking symmetry, we suppose that the given edge
e^{\prime\prime}
is red.
Set
e_{0}=e^{\prime\prime}
.
If
\ell
meets
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\odot}_{0})
, then we take
e=e_{0}
, we take
e^{\prime}=e^{\odot}_{0}
, and we are done.

Otherwise, let
t_{0}
be the tetrahedron immediately above
e_{0}
.
Let
e^{\negthinspace\triangledown}_{0}
and
e^{\vartriangle}_{0}
be the red equatorial edges of
t_{0}
.
By Lemma
10.0.22
(
3
), both
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\negthinspace\triangledown}_{0})
and
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\vartriangle}_{0})
south-north span
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e_{0})
.
See Figure
A.8
.
Since
\ell
does not meet
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\odot}_{0})
, it must meet exactly one of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\negthinspace\triangledown}_{0})
or
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\vartriangle}_{0})
.
Breaking symmetry again, assume that
\ell
meets
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\negthinspace\triangledown}_{0})
.
Let
c
be the cusp shared by
e_{0}
and
e^{\negthinspace\triangledown}_{0}
.
Again, see Figure
A.8
.

By induction, at stage
k
we now have the following.

(
\textrm{A}_{k}
)

e_{k}
is red,

(
\textrm{B}_{k}
)

e_{k}
and
e^{\negthinspace\triangledown}_{k}
meet
c
,

(
\textrm{C}_{k}
)

\ell
meets
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e_{k})
,

(
\textrm{D}_{k}
)

\ell
does not meet
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\odot}_{k})
, and

(
\textrm{E}_{k}
)

\ell
meets
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\negthinspace\triangledown}_{k})
.

We must now either produce
e
and
e^{\prime}
fulfilling the conclusion of Claim
10
or extend the induction to stage
k+1
.

Let
t_{k}
be the tetrahedron immediately above
e_{k}
.
By definition,
e^{\negthinspace\triangledown}_{k}
is red.
Set
e_{k+1}=e^{\negthinspace\triangledown}_{k}
, and note that we have (
\textrm{A}_{k+1}
).
By definition, the edges
e_{k+1}
and
e^{\negthinspace\triangledown}_{k+1}
have the same north-east cusp.
By (
\textrm{B}_{k}
), this is
c
, verifying (
\textrm{B}_{k+1}
).
By (
\textrm{E}_{k}
), the leaf
\ell
meets
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e_{k+1})
, giving (
\textrm{C}_{k+1}
).

Let
t_{k+1}
be the tetrahedron immediately above
e_{k+1}
.
If
\ell
meets
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\odot}_{k+1})
then we take
e=e_{k+1}
, we take
e^{\prime}=e^{\odot}_{k+1}
, and Claim
10
holds.
If not, then (
\textrm{D}_{k+1}
) holds.

\hair

2pt

\pinlabel

\pinlabel
e_{k}
[b] at 82 172

\pinlabel

\endlabellist

Figure A.9.

There are two cases, as
t_{k}
(not
t_{k+1}
) is a fan tetrahedron or a toggle.

•

Suppose that
t_{k}
is a fan tetrahedron.
See Figure
A.8a
.
Then the common face of
t_{k}
and
t_{k+1}
contains the edges
e^{\negthinspace\triangledown}_{k}=e_{k+1}
and
e^{\odot}_{k}=e^{\vartriangle}_{k+1}
.
By (
\textrm{D}_{k}
) we have that
\ell
does not meet
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\odot}_{k})=\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\vartriangle}_{k+1})
.
Since
\ell
does not meet
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\odot}_{k+1})
(by (
\textrm{D}_{k+1}
) above) and it does not meet
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\vartriangle}_{k+1})
, it must meet
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\negthinspace\triangledown}_{k+1})
, giving (
\textrm{E}_{k+1}
).

•

Suppose that
t_{k}
is a toggle.
See Figure
A.9
.
If
\ell
meets
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\vartriangle}_{k+1})
then we take
e=e_{k}
, we take
e^{\prime}=e^{\vartriangle}_{k+1}
, and Claim
10
holds.
Suppose instead that
\ell
does not meet
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\vartriangle}_{k+1})
.
By (
\textrm{D}_{k+1}
), the leaf
\ell
does not meet
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\odot}_{k+1})
either.
Thus it must meet
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e^{\negthinspace\triangledown}_{k+1})
, giving (
\textrm{E}_{k+1}
).

Suppose that the induction never terminates.
Then we have found an infinite sequence of distinct edges
e_{k}
, all meeting
c
,
with
\ell
meeting each
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e_{k})
, which south-north spans
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e_{0})
.
Let
q
be the intersection of
\ell
with the south side of
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e_{0})
.
Let
R
be the rectangle with south-west corner at
q
and north-east corner at
c
.
Note that
R
is contained in
\operatorname{\mathsf{R}}(e_{k})
for all
k
.
Since every edge rectangle is contained in a tetrahedron rectangle, and each tetrahedron rectangle contains only six edge rectangles, this contradicts Lemma
11.0.13
.

Thus the induction does terminate, giving Claim
10
.

With Claim
10
in hand, Lemma
A.0.9
follows.

Appendix B
Normal paths are essential

Here we sketch a proof of Theorem
B.6
, a version of
[
53
, Theorem 5.1]

that applies to proper paths instead of loops.
Many of the definitions, statements, and proofs are unchanged;
we indicate only the necessary changes.
Let
M
be a connected three-manifold equipped with a taut ideal triangulation
\mathcal{T}
.
Since we are dealing with properly immersed paths we use
truncated
models –
that is, we have removed a small open neighbourhood of all model vertices.
For a picture of a truncated tetrahedron, see Figure
B.1
.
Note that truncated faces are hexagons with three sides in
\partial M
.

\labellist
\hair

2pt

\pinlabel
d
[br] at 153 198

\pinlabel
\textsc{e}_{1}
[br] at 220 187

\pinlabel
f
[b] at 304 131

\pinlabel
\textsc{e}_{2}
[br] at 246 52

\endlabellist

Figure B.1.
A truncated taut tetrahedron containing the four additional types of raised arcs of
\delta
coming from normal arcs. Compare with
[
53
, Figure 3]
.

B.2.
Normal paths

We adapt
[
53
, Definition 2.2]

to properly immersed paths.

Definition B.0.1
.

Suppose that
\gamma
is a smooth path properly immersed in the horizontal branched surface
B=B(\mathcal{T})
and transverse to the edges of
B
.
Suppose that for every truncated model face
f
of
B
and for every component
J
of
\gamma^{-1}(f)
the arc
\gamma|J
connects distinct, non-adjacent sides of (the hexagon)
f
.
Then we say that the path
\gamma
is
normal
with respect to
B
.

For examples, see the dashed arcs of Figure
B.1
.

B.3.
Peripheral homotopies

Let
D=D^{2}\subset\mathbb{C}
be the closed unit disk.
We partition
\partial D=b\cup c
with
b
lying in the left half-plane and
c
lying in the right.
Suppose that
\gamma\colon c\to M
is a proper path, transverse to
B
.
We say that
\gamma
is
peripherally homotopic
if there is a map
H\colon D\to M
so that
H|c=\gamma
and
H(b)\subset\partial M
.
If there is no such
H
we call
\gamma

essential
.

Given a peripheral homotopy
H
, we may homotope it, keeping the image of
b
in
\partial M
and not altering
H|c
, to ensure that
H
is transverse to
\gamma
and to
B
.
This done we set
\tau=H^{-1}(B)
.
In Figure
B.4
we give pictures of, and names to, the disk regions of
D-\tau
with positive index which do not already appear in
[
53
, Table 1]
.

(
a
)
Index
1/2
.

(
b
)
Index
1/2
.

(
c
)
Index
1/4
.

Figure B.4.
Disk regions (which are not already listed in
[
53
, Table 1]
)
with positive index arising from proper paths.
From left to right these are
a
peripheral bigon
,
a
complete bigon
, and
a
peripheral trigon
.
We draw arcs of
b
in purple and arcs of
c
in green.

Suppose that
H\colon D\to M
is a peripheral homotopy, as above. The sum of the indices of the regions of
D-\tau
is
1/2
; to see this, note that there are two corners at the intersection of
b
and
c
.

The proof of
[
53
, Lemma 3.1]

goes through without changes;
now the possible regions of
D-\tau
with positive index are a single complete bigon, or a non-empty collection of boundary bigons and peripheral trigons (shown in
[
53
, Table 1]

and Figure
B.4
).

B.5.
New results

Here is the promised version of
[
53
, Theorem 5.1]

for normal paths.

Theorem B.0.1
.

Let
M
be a three-manifold equipped with a taut ideal triangulation
\mathcal{T}
.
Let
B=B(\mathcal{T})
be the resulting horizontal branched surface in
M
.
Any path
\gamma
in
M
, which is normal with respect to
B
, is essential.

With
M
,
\mathcal{T}
, and
B
as above, let
{\widetilde{B}}
be the lift of
B
to
{\widetilde{M}}
.
We then have the following.

Corollary B.0.2
.

Suppose that
F
is a connected surface (perhaps with boundary) carried by
{\widetilde{B}}
and realised as a union of faces of
{\widetilde{B}}
.
Then distinct vertices of
F
are distinct vertices of
{\widetilde{B}}
. ∎

B.7.
Minimal homotopies

We define minimal peripheral homotopies as before:
that is, the number
r(H)
of regions of
D-\tau
is minimised across all peripheral homotopies.
We alter the statement of
[
53
, Lemma 3.1]
,
taking
\delta
to be a proper path, and taking
H
to be a minimal peripheral homotopy.
The conclusions are unchanged except for the final sentence.
Now the allowed regions of positive index are boundary bigons, complete bigons, and
peripheral trigons.
The proof that peripheral bigons do not appear in minimal peripheral homotopies is very similar to that of
[
53
, Lemma 3.1(2)]
.
That is, peripheral bigons give non-normal branches of
H(\tau)
in some truncated face and these can be used to reduce
r(H)
.

We now indicate how to extend the proof of
[
53
, Theorem 5.1]
to include normal paths.

Proof B.0.3

(Sketch proof of Theorem
B.6
)
.

The model annulus
A
in the proof of
[
53
, Theorem 5.1]

is replaced with a model rectangle.
The homotopy
G
is proper, so sends the vertical boundary of
A
into
\partial M
.
There are now an additional four types of raised arc; see Figure
B.1
.

Suppose that
H\colon D\to M
is a minimal peripheral homotopy of
\delta
.
Recall that
D
has index
1/2
.
Applying the modification of
[
53
, Lemma 3.1]

gives us that
D-\tau
has either
at least one boundary bigon, or
at least two peripheral trigons.
Using the transverse orientation, every region with positive index is either a max-bigon, max-trigon, min-bigon, or min-trigon.
The discussion of
[
53
, Section 5.2]

applies to min-trigons just as it does to min-bigons; see Figure
B.8
.

Figure B.8.
Pushing over a min-peripheral trigon, necessarily of type
d
(see Figure
B.1
).
Compare with
[
53
, Figure 4]
.

Now
[
53
, Definition 5.6]

becomes a definition of
properly transverse
homotopies: that is, these homotopies must also respect the cellulation of
\partial M
.

Furthermore
[
53
, Section 5.3]

applies to max-bigons of the peripheral homotopy as before, except that the region
R_{N}
may meet
\partial M
.
Each max-trigon
R_{0}
is either
left
or
right
depending on whether it contains the final, or initial, point of
\delta
as equipped with the (tangential) orientation it receives from
D
.
Let
d_{0}=\partial R_{0}-(s\cup\partial M)
.
In the first conclusion of
[
53
, Claim 5.4]
,
the raised arc
H(d_{0})
has type
c
or
f
, as
R_{0}
is a bigon or trigon respectively.

Given a left max-trigon
R_{0}
, the argument of
[
53
, Section 5.3]

extends it to the right to obtain
B_{\triangleright}
.
Similarly, if
R_{0}
is a right max-trigon, the argument extends it to the left to obtain
B_{\triangleleft}
.
Finally,
S(R_{0})
is either
B_{\triangleright}\cup Q_{\triangleright}
or
B_{\triangleleft}\cup Q_{\triangleleft}
.
Now
[
53
, Claim 5.8]

applies to any pair of regions of positive index.

The remainder of the argument is unchanged.
